\displaystyle F_{A}^{\star}:=\mathrm{d}A+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}A]=\mathrm{d}A+A\wedge_{\star}A\ \in\ \Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})

(3.5)

transforms covariantly:

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\star}F_{A}^{\star}=-[\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}F_{A}^{\star}]\ .

(3.6)

This similarity to the analysis of ordinary nonabelian gauge theories was used in
[
29
]
to cast noncommutative
\mathsf{U}(1)
Chern–Simons and Yang–Mills theories into the framework of standard
L_{\infty}
-algebras, which we now describe.

Noncommutative Chern–Simons theory

The
L_{\infty}
-algebra formulation of noncommutative Chern–Simons theory in three dimensions follows the analysis of the usual nonabelian Chern–Simons gauge theory presented in Section
2.1
. In this case the organising
L_{\infty}
-algebra is simply the differential graded Lie algebra with underlying graded vector space

\displaystyle V=\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,2})

(3.7)

and the non-zero brackets

\displaystyle\ell_{1}(\alpha)=\mathrm{d}\alpha\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell_{2}(\alpha,\beta)=-[\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}\beta]

(3.8)

for
\alpha,\beta\in V
. Note that the underlying cochain complex (
2.45
) (the de Rham complex in this case) is unchanged by the noncommutative deformation; that is, the free fields are the same and only the interactions are modified by the noncommutativity, as is well-known for Moyal–Weyl type noncommutative field theories.

Applying the standard machinery from Section
2.4
, one easily verifies that the noncommutative gauge symmetry (
3.3
) is reproduced in the expected way as

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\star}A=\ell_{1}(\lambda)+\ell_{2}(\lambda,A)\ ,

(3.9)

with the closure (
3.4
) expressed through

\displaystyle\big[\delta^{\star}_{\lambda_{1}},\delta^{\star}_{\lambda_{2}}\big]_{\circ}A=\delta^{\star}_{-\ell_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}A\ .

(3.10)

The Maurer–Cartan equations
\mathfrak{F}_{A}=0
reproduce the noncommutative Chern–Simons equation of motion

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{A}=\ell_{1}(A)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{2}(A,A)=F_{A}^{\star}\ ,

(3.11)

with the covariance (
3.6
) expressed as

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\star}\mathfrak{F}_{A}=\ell_{2}(\lambda,\mathfrak{F}_{A})\ .

(3.12)

The Noether identities

\displaystyle 0\ =\ {\mathsf{d}}_{A}\mathfrak{F}_{A}=\ell_{1}(\mathfrak{F}_{A})+\ell_{2}(\mathfrak{F}_{A},A)=\mathrm{d}F_{A}^{\star}+[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}F_{A}^{\star}]=:\mathrm{d}_{\star}^{A}F_{A}^{\star}

(3.13)

are simply the star-gauge Bianchi identities
\mathrm{d}_{\star}^{A}F_{A}^{\star}=0
satisfied by the noncommutative field strength
F_{A}^{\star}
, where
\mathrm{d}_{\star}^{A}
is star-gauge covariant derivative.

The inner product

\displaystyle\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}}\,\alpha\wedge_{\star}\beta=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}}\,\alpha\wedge\beta

(3.14)

on
V
pairs differential forms
\alpha
and
\beta
on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}
in complementary degrees, where the second equality follows from an integration by parts and is sometimes expressed as saying that the Moyal–Weyl star-product is
cyclic
. This makes
V
into a cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra, and the corresponding Maurer–Cartan action functional then reproduces the standard noncommutative
\mathsf{U}(1)
Chern–Simons functional:

\displaystyle S(A)=\frac{1}{2}\,\langle A,\ell_{1}(A)\rangle-\frac{1}{3!}\,\langle A,\ell_{2}(A,A)\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\,\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,2}}\,A\wedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}A+\frac{2}{3}\,A\wedge_{\star}A\wedge_{\star}A\ ,

(3.15)

where we used
[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}A]=2\,A\wedge_{\star}A
. Thus, like the classical theory, noncommutative
\mathsf{U}(1)
Chern–Simons theory fits nicely into the algebraic framework of a differential graded Lie algebra.

Noncommutative Yang–Mills theory

A similar analysis can be done for noncommutative Yang–Mills theory in any spacetime dimension
d
, using the
L_{\infty}
-algebra of usual nonabelian Yang–Mills theory from Section
2.5
. Let
\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}:\Omega^{k}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})\to\Omega^{d-k}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})
be the Hodge duality operator induced by the standard flat Minkowski metric of
\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
.

The underlying graded vector space is
V=V^{0}\oplus V^{1}\oplus V^{2}\oplus V^{3}
, where

\displaystyle\begin{split}V^{0}=\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})\ ,\quad V^{1}=\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})\ ,\quad V^{2}=\Omega^{d-1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})\ ,\quad V^{3}=\Omega^{d}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})\ .\end{split}

(3.16)

Denoting the corresponding elements by
\lambda\in V^{0}
,
A\in V^{1}
,
\mathcal{A}\in V^{2}
and
\varLambda\in V^{3}
, the differential is given by

\displaystyle\ell_{1}(\lambda)=\mathrm{d}\lambda\ ,\quad\ell_{1}(A)=\mathrm{d}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}A\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell_{1}(\mathcal{A})=\mathrm{d}\mathcal{A}\ ,

(3.17)

the 2-brackets are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=-[\lambda_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}\lambda_{2}]\qquad&,\qquad\ell_{2}(\lambda,A)=-[\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}A]\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})=-[\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}\mathcal{A}]\qquad,\qquad\ell_{2}(\lambda,\varLambda)&=-[\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}\varLambda]\qquad,\qquad\ell_{2}(A,\mathcal{A})=-[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}\mathcal{A}]\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}(A_{1},A_{2})=-\mathrm{d}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}A_{2}]-&[A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}A_{2}]+(-1)^{d}\,[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}A_{2}]\ ,\end{split}

(3.18)

and finally there is a non-zero 3-bracket

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell_{3}(A_{1},&A_{2},A_{3})=-\big[A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}A_{3}]\big]-\big[A_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}A_{3}]\big]+(-1)^{d}\,\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}A_{2}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}A_{3}\big]\ .\end{split}

(3.19)

The noncommutative gauge transformations and their closure again follow as in (
3.9
) and (
3.10
), while the Maurer–Cartan equations
\mathfrak{F}_{A}=0
reproduce the vacuum equation of motion for noncommutative
\mathsf{U}(1)
Yang–Mills theory:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{A}=\ell_{1}(A)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{2}(A,A)-\tfrac{1}{3!}\,\ell_{3}(A,A,A)=\mathrm{d}_{\star}^{A}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}F_{A}^{\star}\ ,

(3.20)

with covariance again captured by (
3.12
). The Noether identities

\displaystyle 0\ =\ {\mathsf{d}}_{A}\mathfrak{F}_{A}=\ell_{1}(\mathfrak{F}_{A})+\ell_{2}(\mathfrak{F}_{A},A)=\mathrm{d}_{\star}^{A}\mathfrak{F}_{A}=\big(\mathrm{d}_{\star}^{A}\big)^{2}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}F_{A}^{\star}=[F_{A}^{\star}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}F_{A}^{\star}]

(3.21)

may be checked directly using symmetry properties of the
d
-form
[F_{A}^{\star}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}F_{A}^{\star}]\in\Omega^{d}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})
.

The inner product on
V
given by

\displaystyle\langle A,\mathcal{A}\rangle=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}}\,A\wedge_{\star}\mathcal{A}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\langle\lambda,\varLambda\rangle=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}}\,\lambda\wedge_{\star}\varLambda

(3.22)

defines a cyclic structure on the noncommutative Yang–Mills
L_{\infty}
-algebra, and the corresponding Maurer–Cartan action functional reproduces (after integration by parts) the standard noncommutative
\mathsf{U}(1)
Yang–Mills functional:

\displaystyle S(A)=\frac{1}{2}\,\langle A,\ell_{1}(A)\rangle-\frac{1}{3!}\,\langle A,\ell_{2}(A,A)\rangle-\frac{1}{4!}\,\langle A,\ell_{3}(A,A,A)\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\,\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}}\,F_{A}^{\star}\wedge_{\star}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}F_{A}^{\star}\ .

(3.23)

3.2
Fuzzy field theories in the
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism

A particularly well understood and tractable class of noncommutative gauge theories on D-branes wrapping curved submanifolds with a non-constant gauge flux is provided by D-branes in WZW models. These involve target spaces which are group manifolds
\mathsf{G}
with non-trivial NS–NS
3
-form fluxes, and they contain D-branes that wrap conjugacy classes of
\mathsf{G}
carrying non-constant
2
-form flux
[
1
]
. Here we focus on the simplest example
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{SU}(2)
for illustration, where the conjugacy classes are
2
-spheres
\mathbbm{S}^{2}\subset\mathbbm{S}^{3}
labelled by the spin
j
of a representation of the
\mathfrak{su}(2)_{k}
current algebra at WZW level
k\in\mathbbm{Z}_{>0}
. In the semi-classical limit with
k\to\infty
, the space of open string ground states forms a finite-dimensional matrix algebra, in contrast to the infinite-dimensional flat space case from Section
3.1
, and the noncommutative matrix product
f\,g
now plays the analogue role of the Moyal–Weyl star-product.

The noncommutative gauge theory supported by the two-dimensional worldvolume of the D-brane is thus no longer a continuum field theory but rather a matrix model
[
2
]
, called a ‘fuzzy’ field theory, or alternatively a field theory on the ‘fuzzy sphere’ which is a matrix approximation of the standard
2
-sphere
\mathbbm{S}^{2}
obtained by quantizing conjugacy classes in
\mathsf{SU}(2)
, and which is covariant under
\mathsf{SU}(2)
. Being finite-dimensional systems, these noncommutative field theories are particularly well tailored to the formalism developed in Section
2
, as they avoid the many functional analytic complications brought in by continuum field theories.

Let us first explain how to set up a differential calculus on the fuzzy sphere suitable for the formulation of fuzzy field theories. The algebra of functions on the fuzzy sphere
\mathbbm{S}_{N}^{2}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{N}:=(j)\otimes(j)^{*}\simeq{\sf Mat}(N)\ ,

(3.24)

where
(j)
denotes the irreducible spin
j=(N-1)/2
representation of
\mathfrak{su}(2)
for an integer
N>1
and
(j)^{*}
denotes the dual representation; the isomorphism with
N{\times}N
complex matrices follows from the fact that
(j)
is
N
-dimensional. Fix a basis of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{su}(2)
where the structure constants are given by the Levi–Civita symbol
\epsilon_{ab}{}^{c}
, for
a,b,c=1,2,3
, and denote by
\frac{1}{r_{N}}\,X_{a}\in\mathcal{C}_{N}
the generators of
\mathfrak{su}(2)
in the spin
j
representation, where
r_{N}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{j\,(j+1)}}
. Then the matrices
X_{a}
generate the algebra
\mathcal{C}_{N}
and satisfy the relations

\displaystyle[X_{a},X_{b}]=\mathrm{i}\,r_{N}\,\epsilon_{ab}{}^{c}\,X_{c}\ ,\quad\delta^{ab}\,X_{a}\,X_{b}=\mathbbm{1}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad X_{a}^{\ast}=X_{a}

(3.25)

of the fuzzy unit sphere, where

\displaystyle[\lambda,\rho]:=\lambda\,\rho-\rho\,\lambda

(3.26)

denotes the matrix commutator of
\lambda,\rho\in\mathcal{C}_{N}
, which makes
\mathcal{C}_{N}
into a Lie algebra, and
∗
denotes Hermitian conjugation.

The fuzzy
2
-sphere
\mathbbm{S}_{N}^{2}
has a three-dimensional
\mathfrak{su}(2)
-covariant differential calculus given by the Chevalley–Eilenberg algebra

\displaystyle\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{C}_{N}):=\mathcal{C}_{N}\otimes\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}\mathfrak{su}(2)^{*}

(3.27)

of
\mathfrak{su}(2)
with coefficients in the fuzzy sphere algebra (
3.24
), which as an
\mathfrak{su}(2)
-module decomposes into irreducible representations as

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{N}\simeq\bigoplus_{J=0}^{N-1}\,(J)\ .

(3.28)

Let
\vartheta^{a}\in\mathfrak{su}(2)^{*}\subset\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{C}_{N})
for
a=1,2,3
be a compatible coframe for the
\mathcal{C}_{N}
-module of
1
-forms defined by the dual of the chosen Lie algebra basis, with
\vartheta^{a}\wedge\vartheta^{b}=-\vartheta^{b}\wedge\vartheta^{a}
, which generates the entire differential calculus
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{C}_{N})
and commutes with
\Omega^{0}(\mathcal{C}_{N})=\mathcal{C}_{N}
.
The Chevalley–Eilenberg differential
\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}
is then defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}\lambda:=\tfrac{1}{r_{N}}\,[X_{a},\lambda]\,\vartheta^{a}

(3.29)

for
\lambda\in\Omega^{0}(\mathcal{C}_{N})=\mathcal{C}_{N}
, together with the Maurer–Cartan equations

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}\vartheta^{a}=-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\,\epsilon^{a}{}_{bc}\,\vartheta^{b}\wedge\vartheta^{c}\ ,

(3.30)

and extended to the whole of
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{C}_{N})
by the graded Leibniz rule. We extend the matrix commutator on
\mathcal{C}_{N}
to arbitrary homogeneous differential forms
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{C}_{N})
by

\displaystyle[\alpha,\beta]:=\alpha\wedge\beta-(-1)^{|\alpha|\,|\beta|}\,\beta\wedge\alpha\ ,

(3.31)

which makes
\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{C}_{N}),[-,-],\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}\big)
into a differential graded Lie algebra, enabling the construction of field theories on the fuzzy sphere
\mathbbm{S}_{N}^{2}
.

With this noncommutative differential calculus it is now straightforward to formulate noncommutative gauge symmetries in the standard way: The
fuzzy gauge transformation
of a gauge field
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{C}_{N})
by a gauge parameter
\lambda\in\Omega^{0}(\mathcal{C}_{N})
is given by the usual formula

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny CE}}A=\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}\lambda-[\lambda,A]\ ,

(3.32)

and using the by now familiar arguments one shows that they close to the Lie algebra
(\mathcal{C}_{N},[-,-])
:

\displaystyle\big[\delta_{\lambda_{1}}^{\textrm{\tiny CE}},\delta_{\lambda_{2}}^{\textrm{\tiny CE}}\big]_{\circ}=\delta^{\textrm{\tiny CE}}_{[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]}\ .

(3.33)

The field strength defined by

\displaystyle F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny CE}}:=\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}A+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[A,A]=\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}A+A\wedge A\ \in\ \Omega^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{N})

(3.34)

transforms covariantly under this gauge symmetry:

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny CE}}F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny CE}}=-[\lambda,F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny CE}}]\ .

(3.35)

The low-energy effective field theory on a D-brane of type
j
wrapping this fuzzy
2
-sphere was derived in
[
2
]
as a noncommutative gauge theory whose action functional is a sum of a Yang–Mills term and a Chern–Simons term, each of which is individually invariant under the gauge transformations (
3.32
).
18
18

18

One can extend this description to stack of
n>1
D-branes of type
j
by considering the differential graded noncommutative algebra
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{C}_{N},\mathfrak{u}(n)):=\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{C}_{N})\otimes\mathfrak{u}(n)
of
\mathfrak{u}(n)
-valued forms on the fuzzy
2
-sphere, with the differential
\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}
, and the (graded) Lie bracket extended to the matrix commutator on
{\sf Mat}(N)\otimes{\sf Mat}(n)
, and similarly for the normalised trace below.
The underlying
L_{\infty}
-algebra was described in
[
29
]
in a flat holonomic coframe. Here we give an alternative treatment which uses the more natural covariant coframe
\vartheta^{a}
appropriate to a curved space; Yang–Mills and Chern–Simons gauge theories in this formulation were first discussed in
[
53
]
. The advantage of this description is that, like the flat space Moyal–Weyl type noncommutative gauge theories of Section
3.1
, the two theories can be treated separately and the
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure exactly parallels the case of ordinary nonabelian gauge theories.

Chern–Simons theory on the fuzzy sphere

The
L_{\infty}
-algebra formulation of Chern–Simons theory on
\mathbbm{S}_{N}^{2}
in the coframe formalism was discussed in
[
77
]
. As usual it is based on a differential graded Lie algebra whose underlying graded vector space is

\displaystyle V=\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{C}_{N})

(3.36)

with the non-zero brackets

\displaystyle\ell_{1}(\alpha)=\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}\alpha\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell_{2}(\alpha,\beta)=-[\alpha,\beta]

(3.37)

for
\alpha,\beta\in V
. Notably, in this framework the underlying cochain complex is markedly different from the classical case, as its differential is the Chevalley–Eilenberg differential
\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}
, rather than the de Rham differential
\mathrm{d}
. This is not so surprising since the geometry of the fuzzy sphere
\mathbbm{S}_{N}^{2}
involves a three-dimensional differential calculus on a fuzzy version of a two-dimensional space. This is due to the imposition of
\mathfrak{su}(2)
-covariance, which requires an embedding of the classical
2
-sphere in three-dimensional flat space: the extra dimension comes from the pullback of the tangent bundle
T_{\mathbbm{R}^{3}}\to\mathbbm{R}^{3}
to
\mathbbm{S}^{2}
, which decomposes as the Whitney sum
T_{\mathbbm{R}^{3}}|_{\mathbbm{S}^{2}}=T_{\mathbbm{S}^{2}}\oplus N_{\mathbbm{S}^{2}}
with
N_{\mathbbm{S}^{2}}\to\mathbbm{S}^{2}
the normal bundle to the embedding
\mathbbm{S}^{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{3}
. Higher-dimensional covariant calculi are a common feature of a broad range of examples in noncommutative geometry.

The gauge symmetry (
3.32
)–(
3.33
) and field equation
F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny CE}}=0
arise in the standard way from these brackets, as does the usual Bianchi identity

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}^{A}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny CE}}:=\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny CE}}+[A,F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny CE}}]=0

(3.38)

from the corresponding Noether identity of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra. We introduce a cyclic structure in the usual way by the pairing

\displaystyle\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=\int_{\mathbbm{S}_{N}^{2}}\,\alpha\wedge\beta

(3.39)

of forms
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{C}_{N})
in complementary degrees, where the integration of top-forms
\Omega^{3}(\mathcal{C}_{N})
is defined via the normalized
N{\times}N
matrix trace

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbbm{S}_{N}^{2}}\,\lambda\ \vartheta^{1}\wedge\vartheta^{2}\wedge\vartheta^{3}:=\frac{4\pi}{N}\,\mathrm{Tr}(\lambda)\ ,

(3.40)

where
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}_{N}
. The Maurer–Cartan action functional

\displaystyle S(A)=\frac{1}{2}\,\langle A,\ell_{1}(A)\rangle-\frac{1}{3!}\,\langle A,\ell_{2}(A,A)\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\,\int_{\mathbbm{S}_{N}^{2}}\,A\wedge\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}A+\frac{2}{3}\,A\wedge A\wedge A

(3.41)

is just the standard Chern–Simons functional on the fuzzy sphere as written in
[
53
]
. It has a natural global
\mathsf{SU}(2)
symmetry given by rotations of the coframe
\vartheta^{a}
, and in the continuum limit
N\to\infty
it describes a
BF
-type topological field theory on the classical
2
-sphere
\mathbbm{S}^{2}
. We shall give a more thorough account of general
BF
theories in the
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism in Section
6.1
.

We can write the action functional (
3.41
) in a more transparent form of a matrix model. For this, we write the gauge field
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{C}_{N})
explicitly in component form
A=A_{a}\,\vartheta^{a}
with
A_{a}\in{\sf Mat}(N)
and define
\mathrm{L}_{a}A_{b}:=\frac{1}{r_{N}}\,[X_{a},A_{b}]
. Then we can write (
3.41
) as

\displaystyle S(A)=\tfrac{2\pi}{N}\,\mathrm{Tr}\big(\epsilon^{abc}\,{\tt CS}_{abc}\big)\ ,

(3.42)

where

\displaystyle{\tt CS}_{abc}=\mathrm{L}_{a}A_{b}\,A_{c}+\tfrac{1}{3}\,A_{a}\,[A_{b},A_{c}]-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\,\epsilon_{ab}{}^{d}\,A_{d}\,A_{c}\ .

(3.43)

This is the form of the fuzzy Chern–Simons functional derived in
[
2
]
from a worldsheet conformal field theory analysis.
19
19

19

This form of the Chern–Simons functional can be extended to any semi-simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of higher rank by replacing the Levi–Civita symbol
\epsilon_{ab}{}^{c}
with the structure constants
f_{ab}{}^{c}
of a basis of
\mathfrak{g}
, in which case it is part of the low-energy effective field theory on a D-brane wrapping conjugacy classes in the corresponding compact semi-simple Lie group
\mathsf{G}

[
2
]
. However, the noncommutative geometric origin of the action functional in these extensions is not clear.

Yang–Mills theory on the fuzzy sphere

To define Yang–Mills theory on
\mathbbm{S}_{N}^{2}
we need an analogue of the Hodge operator
\ast_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}:\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{C}_{N})\to\Omega^{3-k}(\mathcal{C}_{N})
, which is defined on the compatible coframe of
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{C}_{N})
by

\displaystyle\ast_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}\mathbbm{1}:=\tfrac{1}{3!}\,\epsilon_{abc}\,\vartheta^{a}\wedge\vartheta^{b}\wedge\vartheta^{c}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ast_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}\vartheta^{a}:=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\epsilon^{a}{}_{bc}\,\vartheta^{b}\wedge\vartheta^{c}\ ,

(3.44)

together with involutivity
(\ast_{\textrm{\tiny CE}})^{2}=\mathbbm{1}
. The underlying graded vector space of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra is again

\displaystyle V=\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{C}_{N})\ ,

(3.45)

and, with similar notation as in Section
3.1
, the differential is given by

\displaystyle\ell_{1}(\lambda)=\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}\lambda\ ,\quad\ell_{1}(A)=\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}A\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell_{1}(\mathcal{A})=\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}\mathcal{A}\ ,

(3.46)

the 2-brackets are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=-[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]\qquad&,\qquad\ell_{2}(\lambda,A)=-[\lambda,A]\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})=-[\lambda,\mathcal{A}]\qquad,\qquad\ell_{2}(\lambda,\varLambda)&=-[\lambda,\varLambda]\qquad,\qquad\ell_{2}(A,\mathcal{A})=-[A,\mathcal{A}]\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}(A_{1},A_{2})=-\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}[A_{1},A_{2}]-&[A_{1},\ast_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}A_{2}]-[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}A_{1},A_{2}]\ ,\end{split}

(3.47)

and the non-zero 3-bracket is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell_{3}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})=-\big[A_{1},\ast_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}[A_{2},A_{3}]\big]-\big[A_{2},\ast_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}[A_{1},A_{3}]\big]-\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}[A_{1},A_{2}],A_{3}\big]\ .\end{split}

(3.48)

The Maurer–Cartan equation
\mathfrak{F}_{A}=0
leads to the usual covariant equation of motion given by
\mathrm{d}_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}^{A}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny CE}}=0
, and, with the same cyclic inner product (
3.39
), the Maurer–Cartan action functional

\displaystyle S(A)=\frac{1}{2}\,\langle A,\ell_{1}(A)\rangle-\frac{1}{3!}\,\langle A,\ell_{2}(A,A)\rangle-\frac{1}{4!}\,\langle A,\ell_{3}(A,A,A)\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\,\int_{\mathbbm{S}_{N}^{2}}\,F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny CE}}\wedge\ast_{\textrm{\tiny CE}}F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny CE}}

(3.49)

is precisely the
\mathsf{SU}(2)
-symmetric Yang–Mills functional on the fuzzy sphere as presented in
[
53
]
. Written explicitly in component form
F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny CE}}=\frac{1}{2}\,F_{ab}\,\vartheta^{a}\wedge\vartheta^{b}
with
F_{ab}\in{\sf Mat}(N)
, this becomes explicitly a matrix model

\displaystyle S(A)=\tfrac{\pi}{N}\,\mathrm{Tr}\big(F_{ab}\,F^{ab}\big)\ ,

(3.50)

where

\displaystyle F_{ab}=\mathrm{L}_{a}A_{b}-\mathrm{L}_{b}A_{a}+[A_{a},A_{b}]-\epsilon_{ab}{}^{c}\,A_{c}

(3.51)

and
F^{ab}:=\delta^{ac}\,\delta^{bd}\,F_{cd}
, which is precisely the form of the fuzzy Yang–Mills functional derived in
[
2
]
.

3.3
Working with non-trivial fluxes

The two special classes of noncommutative field theories which we have considered thus far in this section have the special feature that they each admit a natural noncommutative differential calculus, which enabled a straightforward adaptation of techniques from ordinary nonabelian gauge theories to reformulate them algebraically in the formalism of standard
L_{\infty}
-algebras, even in the curved space example of the fuzzy sphere. As discussed in Section
1
, this will not be the case in general for curved D-branes and for curved backgrounds. For a non-constant Poisson bivector
\theta=\frac{1}{2}\,\theta^{\mu\nu}\,\partial_{\mu}\wedge\partial_{\nu}
, the deformation quantization of the worldvolume
M
is described by the Kontsevich star-product
[
63
]
which up to second order in the deformation parameter
\hbar
can be written in the form

\displaystyle f\circledast g=f\star g-\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{12}\,\theta^{\mu\rho}\,\partial_{\rho}\theta^{\nu\lambda}\,\big(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}f\,\partial_{\lambda}g+\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}g\,\partial_{\lambda}f\big)+O(\hbar^{3})

(3.52)

for fields
f
and
g
on
M
, where
f\star g
is given by the standard Moyal–Weyl formula (
1.1
).

The usual de Rham differential
\mathrm{d}
is evidently not a derivation of this star-product unless
\theta
is constant. This also implies that the naive definition (
3.3
) of noncommutative gauge transformations no longer satisfies the gauge closure property (
3.4
). Moreover, in the case that the pullback of the NS–NS
3
-form flux to the D-brane worldvolume is non-zero, the bivector
\theta
defines a
twisted
Poisson structure and the star-product (
3.52
) is no longer associative; in this case the star-commutator
[-,-]_{\circledast}
fails to define a Lie bracket on
\Omega^{0}(M)
.

In
[
29
]
a homotopy algebraic approach to constructing generic noncommutative gauge theories was proposed and dubbed the ‘
L_{\infty}
-bootstrap’. According to this prescription, one starts with the natural brackets

\displaystyle\ell_{1}(\lambda)=\mathrm{d}\lambda\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=-[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]_{\circledast}

(3.53)

on the vector space
V^{0}=\Omega^{0}(M)
of gauge parameters, and attempts to construct the rest of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure by consistently solving the homotopy Jacobi identities order by order in the deformation parameter
\hbar
. The approach of
[
67
,
66
]
, based on symplectic embeddings of (almost) Poisson structures, improves this perspective because it can in principle compute all orders expressions, which are sometimes asymptotic expansions of analytic functions known in closed form. However, while these approaches suggest novel algebraic constructions which go beyond earlier approaches, they suffer from a more serious problem: so far they have only been developed in detail for the semi-classical limit of a full noncommutative gauge theory, where the star-commutator is approximated by the underlying almost Poisson bracket. It is not currently clear how these methods can be extended to construct complete noncommutative gauge theories, beyond their semi-classical approximations.

In the remainder of this contribution we will explain an alternative homotopy algebraic construction of noncommutative field theories, which does not handle generic (almost) Poisson structures, but is suggestive of a more systematic and elegant approach to dealing with the general problems discussed above. Rather than attempting to fit noncommutative gauge symmetry into the standard structure of an
L_{\infty}
-algebra, we adapt the
L_{\infty}
-algebra itself by deforming it in a way which is compatible with the underlying noncommutative algebra of differential forms. This yields a new perspective on the implementation of general symmetries in noncommutative field theories while producing new deformations of the standard gauge theories, and it introduces a new type of homotopy algebraic structure which to our knowledge has not been previously discussed in the mathematics literature.

4
Braided gauge symmetry and braided
L_{\infty}
-algebras

In this section we come to the main topic of this paper. We shall introduce the notion of braided gauge symmetry using Drinfel’d twist deformation techniques, comparing it to the more conventional star-gauge symmetries that were discussed in Section
3.1
. After going back to our simple prototypical example of Chern–Simons gauge theory from Section
2.1
and describing its braided noncommutative deformation, we introduce braided
L_{\infty}
-algebras as the organising algebraic principle behind noncommutative field theories with braided gauge symmetry. Throughout we stress the novelties behind these ‘braided field theories’ as compared to the standard noncommutative field theories, and we discuss a braided version of the BV formalism which should serve as a useful tool for the quantization of these theories.

4.1
Closing noncommutative gauge transformations

In Section
3.3
we pointed out issues involved in attempting to close an algebra of star-gauge transformations (
3.3
) for generic (almost) Poisson structures. Let us discuss a somewhat different but equally well-known closure issue with these noncommutative gauge symmetries, which will serve to motivate the constructions which follow later on in this section.

As in Section
3.1
, consider a noncommutative field theory defined with the Moyal–Weyl star-product (
1.1
) for a constant Poisson bivector
\theta
on flat space
M=\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
. Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a matrix Lie algebra, and endow the exterior algebra
\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})=\Omega^{\bullet}(M)\otimes\mathfrak{g}
of
\mathfrak{g}
-valued forms with the graded star-commutator

\displaystyle[\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}\beta]_{\mathfrak{g}}:=\alpha\wedge_{\star}\beta-(-1)^{|\alpha|\,|\beta|}\,\beta\wedge_{\star}\alpha

(4.1)

for homogeneous differential forms
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})
, where here the star-products are composed with the matrix multiplication in
\mathfrak{g}
. Again we define the
star-gauge transformation
of a gauge field
A\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
by a gauge parameter
\lambda\in\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
as the naive deformation of a classical gauge transformation:

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\star}A=\mathrm{d}\lambda-[\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}A]_{\mathfrak{g}}\ .

(4.2)

The problem now is that these star-gauge variations do not generally close on the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
: whereas the commutator of two gauge variations still closes onto the star-commutator as
\big[\delta_{\lambda_{1}}^{\star},\delta_{\lambda_{2}}^{\star}\big]_{\circ}=\delta_{[\lambda_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}\lambda_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\star}
, the star-commutator itself does not close:

\displaystyle[\lambda_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}\lambda_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}=\lambda_{1}\star\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{2}\star\lambda_{1}\ \notin\ \Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})\ .

(4.3)

Moreover, the naive deformation of the field strength

\displaystyle F_{A}^{\star}=\mathrm{d}A+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}A]_{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathrm{d}A+A\wedge_{\star}A\ \notin\ \Omega^{2}(M,\mathfrak{g})

(4.4)

is no longer valued in
\mathfrak{g}
.
In other words, the star-commutator (
4.1
) does not generally define a Lie bracket on the exterior algebra
\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})
. An easy calculation in a basis of
\mathfrak{g}
shows that the exception is when
\mathfrak{g}
is a matrix Lie algebra which is also closed under the anticommutator of matrices, such as
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{gl}(n)
or
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{u}(n)
; if the gauge theory is coupled to matter fields then those must further be restricted to the trivial, (anti-)fundamental or adjoint representations of
\mathfrak{g}
. This is a well-known issue in noncommutative gauge theory, noted originally in
[
88
]
and subsequently elucidated by
[
30
,
37
]
. We note that exactly the same problem arises in the fuzzy field theories from Section
3.2
: the extension of the graded matrix commutator (
3.31
) to
{\sf Mat}(N)\otimes\mathfrak{g}
does not close in
\mathfrak{g}
unless
\mathfrak{g}
is also closed under anticommutators.

Let us briefly discuss the two most common workarounds of the problem of defining Moyal–Weyl star-gauge theories for generic Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}
, each of which has its own drawbacks. Firstly, one can notice that the star-gauge closure takes place not in the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
but rather in its universal enveloping algebra
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{g}
, which is the free unital algebra over
\mathbbm{R}
generated by
\mathfrak{g}
modulo the two-sided ideal generated by
X\,Y-Y\,X-[X,Y]_{\mathfrak{g}}
for all
X,Y\in\mathfrak{g}
. This leads to the notion of
enveloping algebra-valued gauge symmetry

[
61
,
10
]
, generated by extended gauge parameters
\lambda\in\Omega^{0}(M,\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{g})
acting on extended gauge fields
A\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{g})
. While this extension is appealing because it makes sense for arbitrary (not necessarily matrix) Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}
, its drawback is that it introduces infinitely many new degrees of freedom for the gauge fields
A
due to the infinite dimensionality of the algebra
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{g}
. These new fields have no clear meaning in the underlying gauge theory at
\hbar=0
, and so these noncommutative gauge theories do not possess good classical limits.

Alternatively, one can use the
Seiberg–Witten map

[
83
]
, which was originally found as an equivalence of low-energy effective descriptions of open strings in a constant
B
-field background in terms of ordinary as well as noncommutative gauge theories. This identifies the noncommutative and classical gauge orbits through a field redefinition which maps ordinary gauge parameters and fields
(\lambda,a)
to noncommutative gauge parameters and fields
\big(\hat{A}(a),\hat{\Lambda}(\lambda,a)\big)
defined by

\displaystyle\hat{A}(a+\delta_{\lambda}a)=\hat{A}(a)+\delta_{\hat{\Lambda}(\lambda,a)}^{\star}\hat{A}(a)\ .

(4.5)

If the gauge theory is coupled to matter fields, then there is a further map from ordinary matter fields
\phi
to noncommutative matter fields
\hat{\Phi}(\phi,a)
.
In this case no new degrees of freedom are introduced, while the noncommutative variables are functions of the corresponding ordinary variables and the background deformation. Interesting new interaction vertices appear which enable the construction of noncommutative gauge theories
[
62
]
; this has been used extensively over the years in phenomenological explorations of noncommutative field theories (see e.g.
[
86
]
for a review).

The Seiberg–Witten map has a clear geometric interpretation which enables its extension to arbitrary Poisson structures
[
58
,
59
]
and even to twisted Poisson structures
[
15
,
76
]
. It can also be interpreted as an
L_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphism of the underlying
L_{\infty}
-algebras
[
28
]
. However, the drawback of this approach is that it does not produce the intrinsically noncommutative differential graded algebra underlying a noncommutative gauge theory, along the lines we anticipated in Section
1
, but rather maps the problem into a non-local classical field theory.

In the following we review a more recent approach to the construction of noncommutative gauge theories which circumvents these problems: these field theories are defined analytically in closed form for arbitrary gauge algebras
\mathfrak{g}
and matter field representations without the introduction of spurious degrees of freedom, and they possess good classical limits to the corresponding classical field theories. Their construction is based on a precise and systematic homotopy algebraic organising principle which avoids the sometimes cumbersome ordering ambiguities in definitions of interaction vertices due to noncommutativity of fields (e.g.
\Phi_{1}\star A\star\Phi_{2}\neq\Phi_{1}\star\Phi_{2}\star A
): the entire noncommutative field theory is uniquely defined by the structure of an underlying ‘braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra’ which is constructed as an unambiguous and precise noncommutative deformation of the standard
L_{\infty}
-algebra underlying the classical field theory.

Noncommutative diffeomorphisms

To help motivate how the braided algebraic structure appears, let us bring gravity into the present discussion and attempt to analyse its noncommutative deformation through the methods just explained (recall the motivation from Section
1
). The symmetries underlying general relativity are spacetime diffeomorphisms which are generated by vector fields in
\mathfrak{v}:={\sf\Gamma}(TM)
, which is a Lie algebra with the usual Lie bracket of vector fields which we denote by
[-,-]_{\mathfrak{v}}
. In the Moyal–Weyl deformation, we define the star-bracket
[-\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}-]_{\mathfrak{v}}
in the enveloping algebra
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
by regarding the vector space
{\sf\Gamma}(TM)
as a module over the noncommutative algebra of functions
\big(C^{\infty}(M,\mathbbm{R}),\star\big)
, with the holonomic frame on
M=\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
obeying
f\star\partial_{\mu}=f\cdot\partial_{\mu}
and
\partial_{\mu}\star f=\partial_{\mu}f
for
f\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbbm{R})
, and
\partial_{\mu}\star\partial_{\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\,\partial_{\nu}=\partial_{\nu}\star\partial_{\mu}
in
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
. As above, if
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathfrak{v}
are vector fields, then an easy calculation in components reveals that the star-bracket
[\xi_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{v}}\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
is no longer a vector field in general. However, no analogue of the Seiberg–Witten map is generally known for deformed diffeomorphisms, which are instead most naturally defined through Drinfel’d twist techniques
[
19
,
16
]
. We now proceed to review these techniques, and then demonstrate how they can be fruitfully applied to internal gauge symmetries as well.

4.2
Drinfel’d twist deformation theory

We shall now briefly review the basics of the Drinfel’d twist deformation formalism, which is a method to consistenly deform symmetries (described by a Hopf algebra). Here we only recall the salient aspects that we will use explicitly in the following; see
[
74
,
24
]
for a more detailed and complete account. A lot of what we say in the remainder of this section can be applied in a much more general context, and in particular to incorporate the fuzzy field theories discussed in Section
3.2
(see
[
77
]
). For the examples we treat in the present paper, however, we will always start from a continuum field theory on a manifold
M
and suitably deform it analogously to the Moyal–Weyl type noncommutative field theories. Hence we start from the Lie algebra of vector fields
\mathfrak{v}:={\sf\Gamma}(TM)
, which generate infinitesimal diffeomorphisms of
M
, and later on specialize to suitable subalgebras appropriate to the spacetime symmetries of a given field theory example at hand.

The universal enveloping algebra
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{v}
is naturally a cocommutative Hopf algebra with coproduct
\Delta:\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}\to\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}\otimes\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
defined on
generators by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta(\xi)&=\xi\otimes 1+1\otimes\xi\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\Delta(1)=1\otimes 1\ ,\end{split}

(4.6)

for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{v}
. The map
\Delta
is
extended as an algebra homomorphism to all of
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
(analogously to (
2.18
)). Cocommutativity is the property
\tau\circ\Delta=\Delta
, where

\tau
is the transposition which interchanges the factors in a tensor
product of vector spaces; this is equivalent to saying that
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
has a triangular structure with trivial

{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix
1\otimes 1
. The Hopf algebra structure is completed by defining a counit
and antipode whose explicit forms will not be needed in this paper. These structure maps together satisfy a compatibility condition with the product and the unit
1
of
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
as a unital algebra
[
74
,
24
]
.

There is a symmetric
monoidal category
{}_{\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
whose objects are left

\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
-modules and whose morphisms are equivariant maps (see
e.g.
[
20
]
), that is, linear maps which commute with the action of

\mathfrak{v}={\sf\Gamma}(TM)
via the trivial coproduct
\Delta
. Since
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
is a cocommutative Hopf
algebra, the braiding isomorphism of
{}_{\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
is just the trivial
transposition
\tau
.
A
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
-module algebra
is an algebra in the category

{}_{\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
. By this we mean an associative algebra
(\mathcal{A},\mu)
with a left

\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
-action
\triangleright:\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}\otimes\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
which is compatible with the algebra multiplication
via the coproduct
\Delta
, that is

\displaystyle X\triangleright\mu(a\otimes b)=\mu\big(\Delta(X)\triangleright(a\otimes b)\big)

(4.7)

for all
X\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
and
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
, where
\mu:\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}

is the product on
\mathcal{A}
. In the following we will drop the symbol

\triangleright
to simplify the notation, and usually omit the adjective ‘left’ with the understanding that only left modules are considered in this paper.

Example 4.8
.

In our concrete applications to field theory later on,
(\mathcal{A},\mu)
will be, for instance, the space of functions
\mathcal{A}=C^{\infty}(M,\mathbbm{R})
on the manifold
M
with
\mu=\,\cdot\,
the pointwise product, the space of differential forms
\mathcal{A}=\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
with
\mu=\wedge
the exterior product, or more generally the space of tensor fields with the tensor product. These are all modules over the Lie
algebra of vector fields
\mathfrak{v}
, where the (left) action of
\xi\in\mathfrak{v}
on
a\in\mathcal{A}
is given by the Lie derivative
\mathrm{L}_{\xi}
along
\xi
:

\displaystyle\xi(a):=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}a\ .

(4.9)

This makes

(\mathcal{A},\mu)
into a
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
-module
algebra, by virtue of the Leibniz rule for the Lie derivative:

\displaystyle\xi\big(\mu(a\otimes b)\big)=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}\circ\mu(a\otimes b)=\mu(\mathrm{L}_{\xi}a\otimes b)+\mu(a\otimes\mathrm{L}_{\xi}b)=\mu\circ\Delta(\xi)(a\otimes b)\ .

(4.10)

This is of course just the basis of diffeomorphism symmetry: The algebras (
\mathcal{A},\mu)
are covariant under the action
of the universal enveloping algebra of infinitesimal diffeomorphisms

\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
.

If
V
is a real vector space, we denote by
V[[\hbar]]
the vector space of formal power series in a deformation parameter

\hbar
with coefficients in the complexification
V_{\mathbbm{C}}=V\otimes\mathbbm{C}
;
20
20

20

We should more accurately write
V_{\mathbbm{C}}[[\hbar]]
, but we drop the subscript
C
to simplify the notation, as no confusion should arise.
it is naturally a module over

\mathbbm{C}[[\hbar]]
. If
V
and
W
are real vector spaces, then

V[[\hbar]]\otimes W[[\hbar]]\simeq(V\otimes W)[[\hbar]]
.
21
21

21

To keep the notation simple, we do not distinguish between the appropriate topological tensor product needed for formal power series extensions and the usual tensor product of vector spaces over
\mathbbm{C}
.
With these conventions, we denote by

\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
the formal power series extension of the
cocommutative Hopf algebra
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
, with operations extended linearly over
\mathbbm{C}
and applied term by term to the coefficients of series.

Definition 4.11
.

A
Drinfel’d twist
on a manifold
M
is an invertible element

\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]\otimes\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
satisfying the cocycle
condition

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{12}\,(\Delta\otimes\mathbbm{1})\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{23}\,(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\Delta)\mathcal{F}\ ,

(4.12)

where
\mathcal{F}_{12}=\mathcal{F}\otimes 1
and
\mathcal{F}_{23}=1\otimes\mathcal{F}
, together
with the normalization condition
\mathcal{F}=1\otimes 1+O(\hbar)
.

We write the power series expansion of the twist as
\mathcal{F}=:{\sf f}^{k}\otimes{\sf f}_{k}\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]\otimes\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
, with the sum over

k
understood, and likewise for the inverse twist
\mathcal{F}^{-1}=:\bar{{\sf f}}^{k}\otimes\bar{{\sf f}}_{k}\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]\otimes\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
which satisfies a
condition similar to (
4.12
) that can be expressed as

\displaystyle\bar{\sf f}^{k}\otimes\Delta(\bar{\sf f}_{k})\,(\bar{\sf f}^{l}\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{l})=\Delta(\bar{\sf f}^{k})\,(\bar{\sf f}^{l}\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{l})\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{k}\ .

(4.13)

The normalization condition ensures that the twist deformations induced by
\mathcal{F}
below have good classical limits at
\hbar=0
.

A Drinfel’d twist
\mathcal{F}
defines a new Hopf algebra structure on the
universal enveloping algebra
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
, which we denote by

\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
. As algebras
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}=\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
, while the new coproduct
\Delta_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}
is given by

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}}(X):=\mathcal{F}\,\Delta(X)\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}\ ,

(4.14)

for all
X\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
.
This new Hopf algebra is not cocommutative in general,
but the cocommutativity is controlled up to a

braiding
given by the invertible
universal
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix

{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]\otimes\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
induced by the twist as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}=\mathcal{F}_{21}\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}=:\mathsf{R}^{k}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{k}\ ,

(4.15)

where
\mathcal{F}_{21}=\tau(\mathcal{F})={\sf f}_{k}\otimes{\sf f}^{k}
is the twist with its legs
swapped. Explicitly

\displaystyle\tau\circ\Delta_{{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}}(X)={\mathcal{R}}\,\Delta_{{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}}(X)\,{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}\ .

(4.16)

It is easy to see that the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix is triangular, that is

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{21}={\mathcal{R}}^{-1}=\mathsf{R}_{k}\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k}\ ,

(4.17)

and moreover that

\displaystyle(\Delta_{{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}){\mathcal{R}}={\mathcal{R}}_{13}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{23}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\Delta_{{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}}){\mathcal{R}}={\mathcal{R}}_{13}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{12}\ ,

(4.18)

where
{\mathcal{R}}_{13}=\mathsf{R}^{k}\otimes 1\otimes\mathsf{R}_{k}
, or equivalently

\displaystyle\Delta_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}(\mathsf{R}^{k})\otimes\mathsf{R}_{k}=\mathsf{R}^{l}\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{l}\,\mathsf{R}_{k}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\mathsf{R}^{k}\otimes\Delta_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}({\mathsf{R}_{k}})=\mathsf{R}^{l}\,\mathsf{R}^{k}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{k}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{l}\ .

(4.19)

These identities tell us that passing an element “at once” over a pair of elements in a triple tensor product
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]\otimes\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]\otimes\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
is the same as passing successively over “each one” individually.

Drinfel’d twist deformation quantization consists in twisting the enveloping
Hopf algebra
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
to a non-cocommutative Hopf algebra
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
,
while simultaneously twisting all of its modules
[
17
]
. A Drinfel’d twist
\mathcal{F}
defines a symmetric monoidal
category
{}_{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
of left

\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-modules and equivariant maps, that is, linear maps which
commute with the action of
{\sf\Gamma}(TM)
via the
twisted coproduct
\Delta_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}
. The category
{}_{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
is functorially equivalent to
{}_{\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}

[
20
]
. The braiding isomorphism
\tau_{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}
of

{}_{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
is now non-trivial and given by composing the transposition

\tau
with the action of the inverse
{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}
of the universal
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix. Since
{\mathcal{R}}
is
triangular,
{\mathcal{R}}_{21}={\mathcal{R}}^{-1}
, the braiding is symmetric,
i.e. the braiding isomorphism
\tau_{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}
squares to the identity
morphism.

If
(\mathcal{A},\mu)
is a (left)
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
-module algebra, then we
can deform the product
\mu
on
\mathcal{A}
by precomposing it with the
inverse of the twist

\mathcal{F}
to get a new product

\displaystyle\mu_{\star}(a\otimes b)=\mu\circ\mathcal{F}^{-1}(a\otimes b)=\mu\big(\bar{\sf f}^{k}(a)\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{k}(b)\big)\ ,

(4.20)

for
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
, where on the right-hand side we extend
\mu
(linearly over
\mathbbm{C}
) to

\mathcal{A}[[\hbar]]\otimes\mathcal{A}[[\hbar]]\simeq(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})[[\hbar]]
by
applying it term by term to the coefficients of a formal power series.
The cocycle condition (
4.12
) on
\mathcal{F}
guarantees that this
produces an associative star-product
\mu_{\star}
on
\mathcal{A}[[\hbar]]
, and it generally defines a
noncommutative
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-module algebra
(\mathcal{A}[[\hbar]],\mu_{\star})
,
that is, an algebra
in the category of
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-modules, because it carries a representation of the twisted Hopf algebra
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}X\big(\mu_{\star}(a\otimes b)\big)&=X\big(\mu\circ\mathcal{F}^{-1}(a\otimes b)\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=\mu\circ\Delta(X)\big(\mathcal{F}^{-1}(a\otimes b)\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=\mu\circ\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big(\Delta_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}(X)(a\otimes b)\big)=\mu_{\star}\big(\Delta_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}(X)(a\otimes b)\big)\ ,\end{split}

(4.21)

for all
X\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
and
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
.

If the algebra
(\mathcal{A},\mu)
is commutative, i.e.
\mu\circ\tau=\mu
,
then
(\mathcal{A}[[\hbar]],\mu_{\star})
is
braided commutative
, i.e.
\mu_{\star}\circ\tau_{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}=\mu_{\star}
. The noncommutativity in this case is controlled by the triangular
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix as

\displaystyle\mu_{\star}(a\otimes b)=\mu_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(b)\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k}(a)\big)\ ,

(4.22)

which is easily seen by recalling that
{\mathcal{R}}=\mathcal{F}_{21}\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}
.

Let us now illustrate the formalism above by giving some concrete examples of Drinfel’d twists which commonly appear in noncommutative field theory.

Example 4.23
.

The
Moyal–Weyl twist
on
M=\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{F}&=\exp\big(-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}\,\hbar}{2}\,\theta^{\mu\nu}\,\partial_{\mu}\otimes\partial_{\nu}\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=1\otimes 1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\Big(-\frac{\mathrm{i}\,\hbar}{2}\Big)^{n}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,\theta^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\cdots\theta^{\mu_{n}\nu_{n}}\,\partial_{\mu_{1}}\cdots\partial_{\mu_{n}}\,\otimes\,\partial_{\nu_{1}}\cdots\partial_{\nu_{n}}=:{\sf f}^{k}\otimes{\sf f}_{k}\ ,\end{split}

(4.24)

where
(\theta^{\mu\nu})
is a constant
d{\times}d
antisymmetric real-valued
matrix. This twist is built on the enveloping Hopf algebra
\mathsf{U}\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}\subset\mathsf{U}{\sf\Gamma}(T\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})
of
the abelian Lie algebra of infinitesimal spacetime translations. In this case
\mathcal{F}_{21}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}
so that the universal

{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix is given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}=\mathcal{F}^{-2}=\exp\big(\,\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\,\theta^{\mu\nu}\,\partial_{\mu}\otimes\partial_{\nu}\big)=:\mathsf{R}^{k}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{k}\ .

(4.25)

The twist deformation
\big(C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})[[\hbar]],\mu_{\star}\big)
of the algebra of functions on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
gives precisely the Moyal–Weyl star-product (
1.1
). More generally, the twist deformation
\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})[[\hbar]],\wedge_{\star}\big)
of the exterior algebra of differential forms on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
gives the noncommutative differential calculus discussed in Section
3.1
; for the holonomic coframe
\mathrm{L}_{\partial_{\nu}}\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}=0
, so that indeed

\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\wedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}x^{\nu}=\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{\nu}
and
\lambda\star\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}=\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\star\lambda=\lambda\cdot\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}
for
\lambda\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})
.
22
22

22

For these and other considerations in this paper, the Cartan formula
\mathrm{L}_{\xi}=\mathrm{d}\circ\imath_{\xi}+\imath_{\xi}\circ\mathrm{d}
for the action of the Lie derivative on forms is very useful, where
\imath_{\xi}
is the interior multiplication with the vector field
\xi\in\mathfrak{v}
.
Similarly, for the holonomic frame
\mathrm{L}_{\partial_{\mu}}\partial_{\nu}=[\partial_{\mu},\partial_{\nu}]_{\mathfrak{v}}=0
, so that indeed
\lambda\star\partial_{\mu}=\lambda\cdot\partial_{\mu}
.

The Moyal–Weyl twist (
4.24
) has a natural extension from
\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
to any manifold
M
with a non-constant Poisson bivector
\theta
whose components in a local holonomic frame satisfy

\displaystyle\theta^{\mu\rho}\,\partial_{\rho}\theta^{\nu\lambda}=0\ ,

(4.26)

see (
3.52
). An important class of examples arises in the noncommutative gauge theories on D-branes in non-geometric T-duals to string theory compactifications with duality twists, see e.g.
[
57
,
12
]
.

Example 4.27
.

The
angular twist
on
M=\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
is given by
[
46
]

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{F}&=\exp\big(-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}\,\hbar}{2}\,(\partial_{0}\otimes\mathsf{M}_{\theta}-\mathsf{M}_{\theta}\otimes\partial_{0})\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=1\otimes 1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\Big(-\frac{\mathrm{i}\,\hbar}{2}\Big)^{n}\ \sum_{l=0}^{n}\,\frac{(-1)^{l}}{l!\,(n-l)!}\,(\partial_{0})^{n-l}\,(\mathsf{M}_{\theta})^{l}\,\otimes\,(\partial_{0})^{l}\,(\mathsf{M}_{\theta})^{n-l}=:{\sf f}^{k}\otimes{\sf f}_{k}\ ,\end{split}

(4.28)

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}_{\theta}:=\theta^{ij}\,\mathsf{M}_{ij}\qquad\mbox{with}\quad\mathsf{M}_{ij}=x_{i}\,\partial_{j}-x_{j}\,\partial_{i}

(4.29)

for a constant
(d{-}1){\times}(d{-}1)
antisymmetric real matrix
(\theta^{ij})
and
i,j=1,\dots,d-1
. Here
we denote coordinates on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
by
(x^{\mu})=\big(x^{0},(x^{i})\big)=(x^{0},x^{1},\dots,x^{d-1})
with
x^{0}
the time coordinate. This twist is built on the enveloping Hopf subalgebra of
\mathsf{U}{\sf\Gamma}(T\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})
generated by the direct product Lie algebra
\mathbbm{R}\times\mathfrak{so}(d-1)
of infinitesimal time translations and spatial rotations. The corresponding triangular
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix is again given by
{\mathcal{R}}=\mathcal{F}^{-2}
.

For the corresponding twist deformation
\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})[[\hbar]],\wedge_{\star}\big)
of the exterior algebra, the holonomic coframe again has undeformed exterior products
\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\wedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}x^{\nu}=\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{\nu}
. To describe the deformation of the
C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})
-bimodule structure it is convenient to perform a spatial rotation of the coordinates which brings the matrix
(\theta^{ij})
into its Jordan canonical form with non-zero skew-eigenvalues
\theta^{a}
for
a=1,\dots,r
, where
2r
is the rank of
(\theta^{ij})
. Then

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}x^{2a-1}\star\lambda&=\cos(\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\,\theta^{a}\,\partial_{0})\lambda\star\mathrm{d}x^{2a-1}+\sin(\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\,\theta^{a}\,\partial_{0})\lambda\star\mathrm{d}x^{2a}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mathrm{d}x^{2a}\star\lambda&=-\sin(\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\,\theta^{a}\,\partial_{0})\lambda\star\mathrm{d}x^{2a-1}+\cos(\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\,\theta^{a}\,\partial_{0})\lambda\star\mathrm{d}x^{2a}\ ,\end{split}

(4.30)

for
a=1,\dots,r
and
\lambda\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})
,
whereas
\mathrm{d}x^{0}\star\lambda=\lambda\star\mathrm{d}x^{0}
and
\mathrm{d}x^{i}\star\lambda=\lambda\star\mathrm{d}x^{i}
for
i=2r+1,\dots,d-1
.

Example 4.31
.

Both the Moyal–Weyl twist of Example
4.23
and the angular twist of Example
4.27
are special instances of a more general class of twists called
abelian twists

[
16
]
. These can be defined on
any
manifold
M
and are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{F}&=\exp\big(-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}\,\hbar}{2}\,\theta^{ab}\,X_{a}\otimes X_{b}\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=1\otimes 1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\Big(-\frac{\mathrm{i}\,\hbar}{2}\Big)^{n}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,\theta^{a_{1}b_{1}}\cdots\theta^{a_{n}b_{n}}\,X_{a_{1}}\cdots X_{a_{n}}\,\otimes\,X_{b_{1}}\cdots X_{b_{n}}=:{\sf f}^{k}\otimes{\sf f}_{k}\ ,\end{split}

(4.32)

where
(\theta^{ab})
is a constant antisymmetric real-valued
matrix and
\{X_{a}\}\subset{\sf\Gamma}(TM)
is a set of mutually commuting vector fields,
[X_{a},X_{b}]_{\mathfrak{v}}=0
. These twists are built from an abelian subalgebra of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{v}
of vector fields on
M
. For such twists the universal
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix is always given as
{\mathcal{R}}=\mathcal{F}^{-2}
. They are a common choice in noncommutative geometry and field theory because they share many of the special simplifying properties enjoyed by the Moyal–Weyl twist. In particular, in an open neighbourhood of every point of
M
outside a set of measure zero, an abelian twist is equivalent to a Moyal–Weyl twist via a suitable change of local frame
[
11
]
. This is useful for practical considerations, as it means that the legs of the twist
\mathcal{F}
commute with the local basis vector fields in almost every open set.

Example 4.33
.

Twists need not only be associated to commuting vector fields. As an example of a Drinfel’d twist which is
not
an abelian twist, consider the
Jordanian twist
on
M=\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
given by
[
13
]

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{F}=\exp\big(-\mathsf{D}\otimes\log(1-\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\,\partial_{0})\big)=1\otimes 1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{(\mathrm{i}\,\hbar)^{n}}{n!}\,\mathsf{D}^{\underline{n}}\,\otimes\,(\partial_{0})^{n}=:{\sf f}^{k}\otimes{\sf f}_{k}\ ,\end{split}

(4.34)

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}:=x^{\mu}\,\partial_{\mu}\ ,

(4.35)

and the lower factorial
X^{\underline{n}}\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
of a vector field
X\in\mathfrak{v}
for
n\geq 1
is defined by

\displaystyle X^{\underline{n}}=X\,(X-1\big)\cdots\big(X-(n-1)\big)\ .

(4.36)

This is built on the enveloping Hopf algebra of the semi-direct product Lie algebra
\mathbbm{R}\ltimes\mathbbm{R}
of infinitesimal dilatations and time translations. The corresponding universal
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathcal{R}}&=\mathcal{F}_{21}\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}=\exp\big(-\log(1-\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\,\partial_{0})\otimes\mathsf{D}\big)\exp\big(\mathsf{D}\otimes\log(1-\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\,\partial_{0})\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=1\otimes 1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,(-\mathrm{i}\,\hbar)^{n}\ \sum_{l=0}^{n}\,\frac{(-1)^{l}}{l!\,(n-l)!}\,(\partial_{0})^{n-l}\,\mathsf{D}^{\underline{l}}\,\otimes\,(\partial_{0})^{l}\,\mathsf{D}^{\underline{n-l}}=:\mathsf{R}^{k}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{k}\ .\end{split}

(4.37)

The holonomic coframe
\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}
of the deformed exterior algebra
\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})[[\hbar]],\wedge_{\star}\big)
once again has trivial star-exterior products
\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\wedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}x^{\nu}=\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{\nu}
. The
C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})
-bimodule structure is non-trivial and given by

\displaystyle\lambda\star\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}=\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\star(1-\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\,\partial_{0})^{-1}\lambda\ ,

(4.38)

for all
\lambda\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1})
.

These twists appear in
\kappa
-deformed field theories on Minkowski spacetimes which are covariant under the action of a
\kappa
-Poincaré quantum group
[
13
]
. Jordanian twists can be more generally associated to the Borel subalgebra of any Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}\subset\mathfrak{v}={\sf\Gamma}(TM)

[
16
]
.
By further combining these with the abelian twists of Example
4.31
, we obtain more general Drinfel’d twists which provide realizations of the extended Jordanian twists of
[
64
]
. Such twists arise in the holographic dual gauge theories to backgrounds obtained via Yang–Baxter deformations of string theory in
\mathbbm{AdS}_{5}{\times}\mathbbm{S}^{5}

[
89
]
.

4.3
Braided gauge symmetry and noncommutative kinematics

Let us now spell out the kinematical ingredients of braided noncommutative gauge symmetry, incorporating dynamics later on. Let
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]\otimes\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
be any Drinfel’d twist on a manifold
M
, and let
\mathfrak{g}
be any Lie algebra. As in Section
2.1
, let
\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})=\Omega^{\bullet}(M)\otimes\mathfrak{g}
be the exterior algebra of
\mathfrak{g}
-valued differential forms on
M
with graded Lie bracket
[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}
given by the tensor product of exterior multiplication with the Lie bracket of
\mathfrak{g}
. The graded Lie algebra
\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g}),[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}\big)
is naturally a
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
-module with
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
acting trivially on
\mathfrak{g}
and via the Lie derivative on
\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
(see Example
4.8
), that is, it is a graded Lie algebra in the category
{}_{\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
.

Drinfel’d twist deformation then produces a
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-module
\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]],[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)
with the star-bracket

\displaystyle[\alpha,\beta]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}:=[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}\circ\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\alpha\otimes\beta)=[\bar{\sf f}^{k}(\alpha),\bar{\sf f}_{k}(\beta)]_{\mathfrak{g}}\ .

(4.39)

This bracket makes
\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]]
into a
braided
(or
quantum
)
graded Lie algebra
in the sense of
[
91
,
73
]
, that is, a graded Lie algebra in the category

{}_{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}

[
21
]
. It is braided graded antisymmetric:

\displaystyle[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}=-(-1)^{|\alpha_{1}|\,|\alpha_{2}|}\,[\mathsf{R}_{k}(\alpha_{2}),\mathsf{R}^{k}(\alpha_{1})]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,

(4.40)

and satisfies the braided graded Jacobi identity:

\displaystyle[\alpha_{1},[\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}=[[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star},\alpha_{3}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+(-1)^{|\alpha_{1}|\,|\alpha_{2}|}\,[\mathsf{R}_{k}(\alpha_{2}),[\mathsf{R}^{k}(\alpha_{1}),\alpha_{3}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,

(4.41)

for all homogeneous
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})
. This braided graded Lie algebra has a natural braided Lie subalgebra
\big(\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]],[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)
which will play an important role below.

It is important to note that this definition makes sense for
any
Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. In the special instance that
\mathfrak{g}
is a matrix Lie algebra, the braided commutator does
not
coincide with the graded star-commutator (
4.1
):

\displaystyle[\alpha,\beta]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}=\alpha\wedge_{\star}\beta-(-1)^{|\alpha|\,|\beta|}\,\mathsf{R}_{k}(\beta)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\alpha)\ \neq\ [\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}\beta]_{\mathfrak{g}}\ ,

(4.42)

where we used
\mathcal{F}_{21}^{-1}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\,{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{21}
. In particular, if
\mathfrak{g}
is an abelian Lie algebra, then the braided commutator (
4.42
) always vanishes on
\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]]
, while the star-commutator (
4.1
) does not.

Recall that the de Rham differential
\mathrm{d}:\Omega^{\bullet}(M)\to\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
makes
\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g}),[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}\big)
into a differential graded Lie algebra, as a consequence of the Leibniz rule on the underlying exterior algebra. By the Cartan structure equations,
\mathrm{d}\circ\mathrm{L}_{\xi}=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}\circ\mathrm{d}
for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{v}
, so the exterior derivative commutes with the Lie derivatives that enter into the definition of the star-exterior product via the inverse twist
\mathcal{F}^{-1}
. It follows that the standard undeformed de Rham differential is also an ordinary graded derivation of the underlying star-exterior algebra
\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(M)[[\hbar]],\wedge_{\star}\big)
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(\alpha\wedge_{\star}\beta)=\mathrm{d}\alpha\wedge_{\star}\beta+(-1)^{|\alpha|}\,\alpha\wedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}\beta\ .

(4.43)

This makes
\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]],[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star},\mathrm{d}\big)
into a differential graded braided Lie algebra, and provides a natural noncommutative differential calculus for the construction of field theories on
M
.

We are now ready to define notions of braided gauge fields and matter fields. The idea is to deform representations of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
in which fields transform to braided representations of
\mathfrak{g}
, of which there are two types, regarded as acting from the ‘left’ and from the ‘right’. The
left
and
right braided gauge transformations
of a gauge field
A\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
by a gauge parameter
\lambda\in\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
are given respectively by twisting the action of a classical gauge variation
\delta_{\lambda}
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}{}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}A&:=\delta_{\bar{\sf f}^{k}(\lambda)}\bar{\sf f}_{k}(A)=\mathrm{d}\lambda-[\lambda,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
{}^{\textrm{\tiny R}}\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}A&:={}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}\delta_{\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\mathsf{R}^{k}(A)=\delta_{\bar{\sf f}_{k}(\lambda)}\bar{\sf f}^{k}(A)=\mathrm{d}\lambda+[A,\lambda]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,\end{split}

(4.44)

where in the second line we again used
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{21}=\mathcal{F}_{21}^{-1}
.
Since
[A,\lambda]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}=-[\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda),\mathsf{R}^{k}(A)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}
, these are different transformations. In contrast, star-gauge transformations do not see this left/right distinction, as
[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}\lambda]_{\mathfrak{g}}=-[\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}A]_{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Similarly, if
\phi\in\Omega^{p}(M,W)
is a
p
-form matter field in a linear representation
W
of
\mathfrak{g}
, then its left and right gauge transformations are defined respectively by

\displaystyle{}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\phi=-\lambda\star\phi\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad{}^{\textrm{\tiny R}}\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\phi=-\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)\star\mathsf{R}^{k}(\phi)\ ,

(4.45)

where here
\lambda\star\phi:=\bar{\sf f}^{k}(\lambda)\cdot\bar{\sf f}_{k}(\phi)
is given by the tensor product of multiplication of forms by functions and the
\mathfrak{g}
-action on
W
, and
\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda)\star\mathsf{R}^{k}(\phi)=\bar{\sf f}_{k}(\lambda)\cdot\bar{\sf f}^{k}(\phi)
. As left and right braided gauge symmetries behave in a completely analogous way
[
45
]
, we will consider only left variations from now on and drop the superscript
{}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}
from the notation for simplicity; we will usually also omit the adjective ‘left’.

By setting
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\big|_{\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})}=0
and
\mathcal{V}:=\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]]\oplus\Omega^{\bullet}(M,W)[[\hbar]]
, a braided gauge transformation is extended to a map
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}
of bidegree
(0,0)
by commuting with arbitrary maps
\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}
and as a braided derivation of operations
\mu_{\star}
defined on
\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{V}
in the category
{}_{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
, that is, it obeys the
braided Leibniz rule

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\mu_{\star}(\upsilon_{1}\otimes\upsilon_{2})=\mu_{\star}\big(\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\upsilon_{1}\otimes\upsilon_{2}\big)+\mu_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(\upsilon_{1})\otimes\delta^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)}\upsilon_{2}\big)

(4.46)

for all
\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2}\in\mathcal{V}
. Generally, only braided (not ordinary) derivations are compatible with braided symmetry properties of such maps
\mu_{\star}

[
45
]
. This rule also defines the tensor products of braided representations. They furthermore close under the
braided commutator

\displaystyle\big[\delta_{\lambda_{1}}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}},\delta_{\lambda_{2}}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\big]_{\circ}^{\star}:=\delta_{\lambda_{1}}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\circ\delta_{\lambda_{2}}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}-\delta_{\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda_{2})}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\circ\delta^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda_{1})}=\delta^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}_{[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}}\ .

(4.47)

The braided commutator generally makes the braided derivations of a
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-module algebra into a braided Lie algebra
[
21
]
. That (
4.44
) and (
4.45
) give the representation (
4.47
) of the braided Lie algebra
\big(\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]],[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)
on
\mathcal{V}
is proven in
[
45
]
.

Braided noncommutative kinematics is now defined by the
braided left and right covariant derivatives

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{A}\phi:=\mathrm{d}\phi+A\wedge_{\star}\phi\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{A}\phi:=\mathrm{d}\phi+\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\phi)

(4.48)

in
\Omega^{p+1}(M,W)[[\hbar]]
, where
A\wedge_{\star}\phi=\bar{\sf f}^{k}(A)\wedge\bar{\sf f}_{k}(\phi)
is given by the tensor product of exterior multiplication of forms with the action of
\mathfrak{g}
on the representation
W
, and
\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\phi)=\bar{\sf f}_{k}(A)\wedge\bar{\sf f}^{k}(\phi)
. They are both
braided covariant
:

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\big(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{A}\phi\big)=-\lambda\star\big(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{A}\phi\big)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\big(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{A}\phi\big)=-\lambda\star\big(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{A}\phi\big)\ ,

(4.49)

as proven in
[
45
]
.

Similarly, one defines the
braided curvature

\displaystyle F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}:=\mathrm{d}A+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ \in\ \Omega^{2}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]]\ ,

(4.50)

where now there is no left/right distinction due to braided symmetry of the bracket
[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}
on
1
-forms, i.e.
[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}=[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\mathsf{R}^{k}(A)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}
. The braided curvature is also braided covariant:

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}=-[\lambda,F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ .

(4.51)

Note that for a matrix Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, one can write

\displaystyle F^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}_{A}=\mathrm{d}A+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(A\wedge_{\star}A+\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(A)\big)\ \neq\ F_{A}^{\star}\ ,

(4.52)

which differs from the usual field strength (
4.4
) in conventional noncommutative gauge theories (see e.g. (
3.5
) and (
3.34
)).

Braided diffeomorphisms

The discussion above carries through
verbatum
to spacetime symmetries of field theories, such as the diffeomorphism symmetry of gravity, which is in fact where the formalism was originally developed (see e.g.
[
16
,
17
,
14
]
) and which inspired the present treatment of gauge symmetries. This is generated infinitesimally by vector fields
\xi\in\mathfrak{v}={\sf\Gamma}(TM)
acting via the Lie derivative
\mathrm{L}_{\xi}
on generic tensor fields
T
:
\delta_{\xi}T=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}\,T
(see Example
4.8
). The Cartan structure equations tell us that this gives a representation of the Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{v},[-,-]_{\mathfrak{v}})
of vector fields on tensor fields:
[\mathrm{L}_{\xi_{1}},\mathrm{L}_{\xi_{2}}]_{\circ}=\mathrm{L}_{[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{v}}}
.

This is obviously a
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
-module, and following the twisting procedure above we define braided diffeomorphisms
\delta_{\xi}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}T=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}\,T
through the
braided Lie derivative

\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}
given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}\,T:=\mathrm{L}_{\bar{\sf f}^{k}(\xi)}\bar{\sf f}_{k}(T)\ .

(4.53)

The braided Lie derivative is a braided derivation of the star-tensor product
\otimes_{\star}=\otimes\circ\mathcal{F}^{-1}
which closes the braided Lie algebra
\big(\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]],[-,-]_{\mathfrak{v}}^{\star}\big)
:

\displaystyle\big[\mathrm{L}_{\xi_{1}}^{\star},\mathrm{L}_{\xi_{2}}^{\star}\big]_{\circ}^{\star}=\mathrm{L}_{[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{v}}^{\star}}^{\star}\ .

(4.54)

Note that the braided Lie bracket of vector fields may be represented as

\displaystyle[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{v}}^{\star}=\xi_{1}\star\xi_{2}-\mathsf{R}_{k}(\xi_{2})\star\mathsf{R}^{k}(\xi_{1})\ \neq\ [\xi_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{v}}

(4.55)

in the twist deformation of the universal enveloping algebra
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
, which by construction is again a vector field in
\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
, in contrast to the star-bracket
[\xi_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\star}}{{\scriptstyle,}}\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{v}}
.

It is worth stressing once more that these braided diffeomorphisms are
not
the same as the ‘twisted diffeomorphisms’ of
[
19
]
. Twisted diffeomorphisms act through the twisted enveloping Hopf algebra
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
, whose underlying algebra is the same as that of the classical Hopf algebra
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
. In particular, an infinitesimal twisted diffeomorphism of a single field simply follows the classical rule with the usual Lie derivative
\mathrm{L}_{\xi}
so that they also close on the classical Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{v},[-,-]_{\mathfrak{v}})
of diffeomorphisms, while on star-products of fields they follow a deformed Leibniz rule defined by the twisted coproduct
\Delta_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}(\mathrm{L}_{\xi})
from (
4.14
).

4.4
Example: Braided Chern–Simons theory

As a simple illustration of a field theory with braided gauge symmetry where we can explore some of the novelties that arise, let us go back to the beginning of our story with the simplest prototypical example, namely the Chern–Simons gauge theory from Section
2.1
, and consider its braided noncommutative deformation. We define the braided noncommutative version of the Chern–Simons functional (
2.2
) in the obvious way as

\displaystyle S_{\star}(A)=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\,A\wedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}A+\frac{1}{3!}\,A\wedge_{\star}[A,A]^{\star}_{\mathfrak{g}}\Big)\ ,

(4.56)

for a gauge field
A\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
.

In order to be able to use this in a standard variational principle, we need a technical restriction on the Drinfel’d twist
\mathcal{F}
: we require that the usual integral over
M
is
graded cyclic
with respect to the deformed exterior
product
\wedge_{\star}
, that is,

\displaystyle\int_{M}\,\alpha\wedge_{\star}\beta=(-1)^{|\alpha|\,|\beta|}\,\int_{M}\,\beta\wedge_{\star}\alpha\ ,

(4.57)

for all homogeneous forms
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M)[[\hbar]]
in complementary degrees. For example, this holds for abelian twists (see Example
4.31
), whereby
{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}=\mathcal{F}^{2}
and (
4.57
) follows from integration by parts with respective to the Lie derivative.

Using this graded cyclicity repeatedly, along with invariance of the quadratic form
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}
on
\mathfrak{g}
and the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix identities (
4.19
) as well as the braided Leibniz rule (
4.46
), one checks explicitly that the action functional (
4.56
) is invariant under the (left) braided gauge transformations

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}A=\mathrm{d}\lambda-[\lambda,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,

(4.58)

for an arbitrary gauge parameter
\lambda\in\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
, that is,
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}S_{\star}(A)=0
. This holds for arbitrary quadratic Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}
without the introduction of extra degrees of freedom for the gauge fields
A
. On the other hand, by the ordinary Leibniz rule its variation is given by

\displaystyle\delta S_{\star}(A)=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\Big(\delta A\wedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}A+\frac{1}{2}\,\delta A\wedge_{\star}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\Big)

(4.59)

for arbitrary variations
\delta A\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
of the gauge fields, as in the classical case. This leads to the equations of motion

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}=F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}=0

(4.60)

describing a flat braided
\mathfrak{g}
-connection, which are braided covariant by virtue of (
4.51
):

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\,\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}=-[\lambda,\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ .

(4.61)

So far everything we have said is just the obvious braided deformation of what was said in the classical case from Section
2.1
, as in the conventional noncommutative field theories of Section
3
. At this point, however, the braided field theory deviates from the naive expectations. Despite the covariance of the field equations, braided gauge symmetries
do not
produce new solutions: An infinitesimal gauge variation produces the field equation

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A+\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}A}=\mathrm{d}(A+\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}A)+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big([\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+[A,\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)+O(\lambda^{2})\ ,

(4.62)

and due to the braided Leibniz rule (
4.46
) this cannot be combined to the form
\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A}+\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\,\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}+O(\lambda^{2})
. That is,

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\,\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}\ \neq\ \mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A+\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}A}-\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}+O(\lambda^{2})\ ,

(4.63)

and hence a braided gauge transformation of a solution
A
to
\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}=0
may produce a field configuration with
\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A+\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}A}\neq 0
. In other words, there is generally no moduli space of flat
\mathfrak{g}
-connections modulo braided gauge transformations in this sense, and the space of physical states cannot be described as in the commutative case.
23
23

23

The trivial (and uninteresting) exception is of course when
\mathfrak{g}
is an abelian Lie algebra and the resulting Chern–Simons theory is a non-interacting field theory, whose classical and braided versions are the same.
We will say more about this unusual yet consistent feature of the “braided moduli space” from a more technical standpoint in Section
4.9
.
