How then should we interpret, or even justify, the meaning of the term ‘braided gauge symmetry’? The key point is our alternative interpretation of gauge symmetries via Noether’s second theorem, which for the standard Chern–Simons theories from Sections
2.1
and
3.1
coincides with the Bianchi identity for the curvature 2-form. The latter is known to be generally violated in braided noncommutative differential geometry (see e.g.
[
22
,
14
,
9
]
): The braided version of the Bianchi identity is readily calculated by taking the exterior derivative of the expression (
4.50
) and rewriting it in terms of
F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}
to get the differential identity

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,[F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}},A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[A,F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+\tfrac{1}{4}\,\big([[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star},A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-[A,[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)=0\ .

(4.64)

The last term vanishes in the classical case with trivial
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix
{\mathcal{R}}=1\otimes 1
by symmetry of the Lie bracket
[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}
on
1
-forms
\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
, but it is non-vanishing in general.
We can rewrite the modified Bianchi identity in terms of the left and right covariant derivatives (
4.48
) as

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{A}\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}+\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{A}\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}\big)=-\tfrac{1}{4}\,[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,

(4.65)

where we used the braided (odd) Jacobi identity (
4.41
) on 1-forms with
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{3}=A
.
Again this identity holds off-shell, but it differs from its classical form by inhomogeneous terms involving the gauge field
A
itself.

In analogy to the classical case, it is natural to interpret this as the ‘braided Noether identity’ that should follow via braided gauge invariance of the action functional. This is indeed the case, but one can no longer use the formula (
4.59
) as in the classical case, because braided gauge transformation obey a braided Leibniz rule and so are no longer special directions of a general field variation, which obeys the standard Leibniz rule; that is,

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}S_{\star}(A)\ \neq\ \int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big(\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}A\wedge_{\star}\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}\big)\ .

(4.66)

Nonetheless, the braided Noether identity (
4.65
) follows from
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}S_{\star}(A)=0
by using cyclicity and integration by parts, along with
{\rm ad}(\mathfrak{g})
-invariance of the inner product
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}
and the braided Jacobi identity, to isolate the gauge parameter
\lambda
while making the Euler–Lagrange derivative
\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}
appear; that is,

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}S_{\star}(A)=-\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\bigg(\lambda\wedge_{\star}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\,\big(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{A}\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}+\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{A}\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}\big)+\frac{1}{4}\,[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\Big)\bigg)\ .

(4.67)

This provides a braided version of Noether’s second theorem: braided gauge symmetries still induce interdependence among the equations of motion, and hence gauge redundancies. This justifies the interpretation of local braided symmetries as “gauge”. Note that there is only one braided Noether identity, corresponding to the fact that left and right braided gauge transformations are not independent (see (
4.44
)).

We now notice that, analogously to Section
2.1
, the formulation of braided Chern–Simons gauge theory can be written entirely in terms of the ‘bracket’ operations

\displaystyle\ell_{1}^{\star}=\mathrm{d}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell_{2}^{\star}=-[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}

(4.68)

which define a differential graded braided Lie algebra structure on the graded vector space
V:=\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]]
, as well as the (strictly) cyclic inner product

\displaystyle\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{\star}:=\langle-,-\rangle\circ\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\alpha\otimes\beta)=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\alpha\wedge_{\star}\beta)

(4.69)

on
V
, where strict cyclicity follows using the classical properties together with the graded commutativity (
4.57
) and the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix identities (
4.19
). That is, the braided gauge transformations of the fields, the equations of motion and the gauge redundancy via the braided Noether identities are encoded respectively by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}A=\ell_{1}^{\star}(\lambda)+\ell_{2}^{\star}(\lambda,A)&\qquad,\qquad\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}=\ell^{\star}_{1}(A)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{2}^{\star}(A,A)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
{\mathsf{d}}^{\star}_{A}\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}:=\ \ell_{1}^{\star}(\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star})+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(\ell^{\star}_{2}(\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star},A)-&\,\ell_{2}^{\star}(A,\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star})\big)+\tfrac{1}{4}\,\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\ell_{2}^{\star}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A)\big)\ =\ 0\ ,\end{split}

(4.70)

with gauge closure and braided covariance represented by

\displaystyle\big[\delta_{\lambda_{1}}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}},\delta_{\lambda_{2}}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\big]^{\star}_{\circ}A=\delta^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}_{-\ell_{2}^{\star}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}A\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\delta^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}_{\lambda}\,\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}=\ell_{2}^{\star}(\lambda,\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star})\ .

(4.71)

The braided Chern–Simons functional is encoded as

\displaystyle S_{\star}(A)=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\langle A,\ell_{1}^{\star}(A)\rangle_{\star}-\tfrac{1}{3!}\,\langle A,\ell_{2}^{\star}(A,A)\rangle_{\star}\ ,

(4.72)

from which the equations of motion
\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}=0
are derived by varying and using cyclicity of the inner product.

The structure maps in this formulation of the braided gauge theory are all evidently simply twist deformations, via the procedure of Section
4.2
, of those from Section
2.1
.
This motivates an extension of the notion of an
L_{\infty}
-algebra to the braided setting, and the construction of more general noncommutative field theories with braided gauge symmetry by twisting the formalism from Section
2.4
. This extension will occupy the remainder of this section.

4.5
Braided
L_{\infty}
-algebras

In this section we shall work with
L_{\infty}
-algebras as presented in Section
2
in terms of brackets and relations, and define a braided generalization in these terms, deferring a discussion of their incarnations as braided versions of coalgebras and Chevalley–Eilenberg algebras until Section
4.8
. The key observation is that the definition of a classical
L_{\infty}
-algebra takes place in the category of vector spaces and linear maps, which is a symmetric monoidal category with braiding given by the trivial transposition isomorphism
\tau
. This definition can be made in
any
symmetric monoidal category with a non-trivial braiding, just like the definition of a Lie algebra. In this paper we will only need the definition in the category
{}_{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
of left
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-modules, and we restrict to this setting for definiteness.

Definition 4.73
.

A
braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
is a
\mathbbm{Z}
-graded
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-module
24
24

24

We regard
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
itself as a
\mathbbm{Z}
-graded
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-module sitting in degree
0
.

V=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}\,V^{k}
with a collection
\ell_{n}:V^{\otimes n}\to V
,
n\geq 1
of equivariant graded braided antisymmetric multilinear maps of degree
2-n
that satisfy the
braided homotopy Jacobi identities

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,(-1)^{i\,(n-i)}\,\ell_{n-i+1}\circ\big(\ell_{i}\otimes\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes n-i}\big)\ \circ\ \sum_{\sigma\in{\rm Sh}(i;n-i)}\,{\rm sgn}(\sigma)\,\tau_{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}^{\sigma}=0

(4.74)

for each
n\geq 1
, where
\tau_{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}^{\sigma}:V^{\otimes n}\to V^{\otimes n}
denotes the action of the permutation
\sigma
via the symmetric braiding
\tau_{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}
of the category
{}_{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
times the Koszul sign multiplication which interchanges factors in a tensor product of graded
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-modules.

Let us unravel this definition in a bit more detail. Graded braided antisymmetry is the property

\displaystyle\ell_{n}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})=-(-1)^{|v_{i}|\,|v_{i+1}|}\ \ell_{n}\big(v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1},\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{i+1}),\mathsf{R}^{k}(v_{i}),v_{i+2},\dots,v_{n}\big)

(4.75)

for
i=1,\dots,n-1
and homogeneous elements
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in V
. The permutation action
\tau_{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}^{\sigma}
includes, in addition to the usual Koszul signs, appropriate insertions of the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix as in (
4.75
). The braided homotopy Jacobi identities (
4.74
) are unchanged from their classical counterparts for
n=1,2
, that is, (
2.26
) and (
2.27
) continue to hold. Thus, similarly to the classical case, every braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra has an underlying cochain complex
(V,\ell_{1})
in the category
{}_{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
and
\ell_{2}:V\otimes V\to V
is a cochain map in
{}_{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
. The first homotopy relation which differs from the classical case is for
n=3
, which is a modification of (
2.28
) in accordance with the non-trivial braiding (
4.75
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(v_{1},v_{2}),v_{3}\big)-(-1)^{|v_{2}|\,|v_{3}|}\,\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(v_{1},\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{3})),\mathsf{R}^{k}(v_{2})\big)\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt+(-1)^{(|v_{2}|+|v_{3}|)\,|v_{1}|}\,\ell_{2}\big(\ell_{2}(\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{2}),\mathsf{R}_{l}(v_{3})),\mathsf{R}^{l}\,\mathsf{R}^{k}(v_{1})\big)\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 28.45274pt=-\ell_{3}\big(\ell_{1}(v_{1}),v_{2},v_{3}\big)-(-1)^{|v_{1}|}\,\ell_{3}\big(v_{1},\ell_{1}(v_{2}),v_{3}\big)-(-1)^{|v_{1}|+|v_{2}|}\,\ell_{3}\big(v_{1},v_{2},\ell_{1}(v_{3})\big)\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt-\ell_{1}\big(\ell_{3}(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})\big)\ .\end{split}

(4.76)

When the only non-zero bracket is
\ell_{2}
, a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra is a braided Lie algebra in the sense of
[
73
]
and it coincides with the notion of Lie algebra in
{}_{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
discussed by
[
21
]
. Thus braided
L_{\infty}
-algebras are homotopy coherent generalizations of braided Lie algebras. In particular, the cohomology
H^{\bullet}(V,\ell_{1})
of a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra is a
\mathbbm{Z}
-graded braided Lie algebra.

In the following we regard the one-dimensional vector space
\mathbbm{C}[[\hbar]]
as a trivial graded left
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-module, sitting in degree
0
.

Definition 4.77
.

A
cyclic braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
is a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
together with a non-degenerate graded braided symmetric cochain map
\langle-,-\rangle:V\otimes V\to\mathbbm{C}[[\hbar]]
that satisfies the
braided cyclicity condition

\displaystyle\langle-,-\rangle\,\circ\,(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\ell_{n})={\rm sgn}(\sigma)\,\langle-,-\rangle\,\circ\,(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\ell_{n})\,\circ\,\tau_{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}^{\sigma}\ ,

(4.78)

for all
n\geq 1
and for all cyclic permutations
\sigma\in C_{n+1}
.

Evaluated on elements
v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in V
, cyclicity in the category
{}_{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
reads as

\displaystyle\big\langle v_{0},\ell_{n}(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n})\big\rangle=\pm\,\big\langle\mathsf{R}_{k_{0}}\,\mathsf{R}_{k_{1}}\cdots\mathsf{R}_{k_{n-1}}(v_{n}),\ell_{n}\big(\mathsf{R}^{k_{0}}(v_{0}),\mathsf{R}^{k_{1}}(v_{1}),\dots,\mathsf{R}^{k_{n-1}}(v_{n-1})\big)\big\rangle\ .

(4.79)

In this paper we are predominantly interested in braided
L_{\infty}
-algebras that result from twist deformations of classical
L_{\infty}
-algebras, via the techniques described in Section
4.2
. This can be done starting from an
L_{\infty}
-algebra
(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
in the category
{}_{\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
of left
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
-modules. This means that
V=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbbm{Z}}\,V_{k}
is a
\mathbbm{Z}
-graded

\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
-module and the
n
-brackets
\ell_{n}:V^{\otimes n}\to V
are
equivariant maps. Given any
Drinfel’d twist
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]\otimes\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
, we can
deform the brackets
\ell_{n}
to new brackets
\ell_{n}^{\star}:V[[\hbar]]^{\otimes n}\to V[[\hbar]]
which
are morphisms in the twisted representation category

{}_{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
of
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-modules. Following the
standard prescription (
4.20
), we set

\ell_{1}^{\star}:=\ell_{1}
and

\displaystyle\ell_{n}^{\star}(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n}):=\ell_{n}(v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n})

(4.80)

for
n\geq 2
, where
v\otimes_{\star}v^{\prime}:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(v\otimes v^{\prime})=\bar{\sf f}^{k}(v)\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{k}(v^{\prime})
for
v,v^{\prime}\in V
. The functorial equivalence between the symmetric monoidal categories
{}_{\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
and
{}_{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
then implies the following central result, whose proof is found in
[
45
, Proposition 4.8]
.

Proposition 4.81
.

If
(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
is an
L_{\infty}
-algebra in the category

{}_{\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
, then
(V[[\hbar]],\{\ell_{n}^{\star}\})
is a braided

L_{\infty}
-algebra.

This result is a far reaching generalization of what we did in Section
4.4
, where the differential graded Lie algebra
\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g}),\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\big)
underlying Chern–Simons gauge theory on a three-manifold
M
was twist deformed to the differential graded braided Lie algebra
\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]],\ell_{1}^{\star},\ell_{2}^{\star}\big)
of braided Chern–Simons theory on
M
. We will discuss how this procedure extends to more general field theories organised by generic
L_{\infty}
-algebras in Section
4.6
.

Similarly, we can twist deform cyclic structures on
L_{\infty}
-algebras to obtain cyclic braided
L_{\infty}
-algebras. Let
(V,\{\ell_{n}\},\langle-,-\rangle)
be a cyclic

L_{\infty}
-algebra in the category
{}_{\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
of left

\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
-modules, with
\mathbbm{R}
regarded as a trivial graded
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
-module sitting in degree
0
. This means that
(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
is an
L_{\infty}
-algebra in
{}_{\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
and the cyclic inner product

\langle-,-\rangle:V\otimes V\to\mathbbm{R}
is
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
-invariant:

\displaystyle X\langle v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\rangle=\langle\Delta(X)(v_{1}\otimes v_{2})\rangle=0\ ,

(4.82)

for all
X\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
and
v_{1},v_{2}\in V
. Following the standard prescription (
4.20
), we can twist
deform the inner product
\langle-,-\rangle
to a new pairing

\langle-,-\rangle_{\star}
defined by

\displaystyle\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle_{\star}:=\langle-,-\rangle\circ\mathcal{F}^{-1}(v_{1}\otimes v_{2})=\langle\bar{\sf f}^{k}(v_{1}),\bar{\sf f}_{k}(v_{2})\rangle\ .

(4.83)

Proposition 4.84
.

If
(V,\{\ell_{n}\},\langle-,-\rangle)
is a cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra in the category

{}_{\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
, then

(V[[\hbar]],\{\ell_{n}^{\star}\},\langle-,-\rangle_{\star})
is a cyclic braided

L_{\infty}
-algebra.

Proof.

Non-degeneracy of the pairing
\langle-,-\rangle_{\star}:V[[\hbar]]\otimes V[[\hbar]]\to\mathbbm{C}[[\hbar]]
follows from the untwisted non-degeneracy and an order by order argument in the formal deformation parameter
\hbar
. Graded symmetry of the cyclic inner product
\langle-,-\rangle

implies that the twisted inner product
\langle-,-\rangle_{\star}
is naturally
graded braided symmetric.

To check braided cyclicity, we compute

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\langle v_{0},\ell^{\star}_{n}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})\big\rangle_{\star}&=\big\langle\bar{\sf f}^{k}(v_{0})\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{k}\big(\ell_{n}(\bar{\sf f}^{l}(v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n-1})\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{l}(v_{n}))\big)\big\rangle\\[4.0pt]
&=\big\langle(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\ell_{n})\big(\bar{\sf f}^{k}(v_{0})\otimes\Delta(\bar{\sf f}_{k})\big[\bar{\sf f}^{l}(v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n-1})\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{l}(v_{n})\big]\big)\big\rangle\\[4.0pt]
&=\big\langle(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\ell_{n})\big(\Delta(\bar{\sf f}^{k})\big[\bar{\sf f}^{l}(v_{0})\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{l}(v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n-1})\big]\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{k}(v_{n})\big)\big\rangle\\[4.0pt]
&=\pm\,\big\langle\bar{\sf f}_{k}(v_{n})\otimes\ell_{n}\big(\Delta\big(\bar{\sf f}^{k})\big[\bar{\sf f}^{l}(v_{0})\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{l}(v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n-1})\big]\big)\big\rangle\\[4.0pt]
&=\pm\,\big\langle\bar{\sf f}_{k}(v_{n})\otimes\bar{\sf f}^{k}\big(\ell_{n}(\bar{\sf f}^{l}(v_{0})\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{l}(v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n-1}))\big)\big\rangle\\[4.0pt]
&=\pm\,\big\langle\bar{\sf f}^{k}\,\mathsf{R}_{m}(v_{n})\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{k}\,\mathsf{R}^{m}\big(\ell_{n}(\bar{\sf f}^{l}(v_{0})\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{l}(v_{1}\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}v_{n-1}))\big)\big\rangle\\[4.0pt]
&=\pm\,\big\langle\mathsf{R}_{m}(v_{n})\otimes\mathsf{R}^{m}\big(\ell_{n}^{\star}(v_{0}\otimes v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n-1})\big)\big\rangle_{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&=\pm\,\big\langle(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\ell_{n}^{\star})\big(\mathsf{R}_{m}(v_{n})\otimes\Delta_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}(\mathsf{R}^{m})\big[v_{0}\otimes(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n-1})\big]\big)\big\rangle_{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&=\pm\,\big\langle\mathsf{R}_{k_{0}}\,\mathsf{R}_{k_{1}}\cdots\mathsf{R}_{k_{n-1}}(v_{n}),\ell_{n}^{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}^{k_{0}}(v_{0}),\mathsf{R}^{k_{1}}(v_{1}),\dots,\mathsf{R}^{k_{n-1}}(v_{n-1})\big)\big\rangle_{\star}\ .\end{split}

(4.85)

In the first and seventh lines we use the definitions of the
star-pairing and the star-brackets, in the second and fifth lines we use commutativity
of
\ell_{n}
with the
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
-action, in the third line we use the cocycle property (
4.13
) for the inverse of the Drinfel’d twist along with
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
-invariance (
4.82
) of
the classical pairing, in the fourth line we use
classical cyclicity, in the sixth line we use the definition of the

{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix, in the eighth line we use commutativity of
\ell_{n}^{\star}
with the
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-action, and in the ninth line we use the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix identities (
4.19
).
∎

However, this level of generality is not
suitable for applications to field theory, as the loss of
strict
graded
symmetry and cyclicity would lead to problems with the variational principle for the corresponding action functionals. Following the concrete example of braided Chern–Simons theory from Section
4.4
, we need to restrict the types of Drinfel’d twists by abstracting the graded cyclicity property (
4.57
) for integration
of twisted differential forms.

Definition 4.86
.

A Drinfel’d twist
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]\otimes\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
is

compatible
with a cyclic structure
\langle-,-\rangle:V\otimes V\to\mathbbm{R}
on an
L_{\infty}
-algebra in
{}_{\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
if

\displaystyle\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle_{\star}=\langle\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{1}),\mathsf{R}^{k}(v_{2})\rangle_{\star}=(-1)^{|v_{1}|\,|v_{2}|}\,\langle v_{2},v_{1}\rangle_{\star}

(4.87)

for all homogeneous
v_{1},v_{2}\in V
.

In other words, a compatible Drinfel’d twist turns braided graded symmetry of the inner product into
strict
graded symmetry. It also turns the braided cyclicity condition into the strict cyclicity condition (see (
2.31
)), as inferred by

Corollary 4.88
.

Let
(V,\{\ell_{n}\},\langle-,-\rangle)
be a cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra
in the category
{}_{\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
, and let
\mathcal{F}
be a compatible Drinfel’d
twist. Then

(V[[\hbar]],\{\ell_{n}^{\star}\},\langle-,-\rangle_{\star})
is a strictly
cyclic braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

Proof.

The seventh line of the computation (
4.85
) shows

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\langle v_{0},\ell^{\star}_{n}(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n})\big\rangle_{\star}&=\pm\,\big\langle\mathsf{R}_{k}(v_{n}),\mathsf{R}^{k}\big(\ell_{n}^{\star}(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{n-1})\big)\big\rangle_{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&=\pm\,\big\langle v_{n},\ell_{n}^{\star}(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{n-1})\big\rangle_{\star}\ ,\end{split}

(4.89)

where in the second line we used (
4.87
).
∎

4.6
Braided
L_{\infty}
-algebras of noncommutative field theories

We shall now define noncommutative field theories in the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism by
employing a twist deformation of the approach discussed in
Section
2.4
, whereby a classical field theory is defined by its
4-term cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra as the initial input.
25
25

25

Here we treat only theories with irreducible gauge symmetries. We shall discuss an example with higher gauge symmetries in Section
6
.
We call a field theory obtained in this
way a
braided field theory
. That is, a braided field theory is
defined by a 4-term cyclic braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra as the initial input.

Starting from a classical field theory on a manifold
M
, described algebraically by a 4-term cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra
(V,\{\ell_{n}\},\langle-,-\rangle)
(over
\mathbbm{R}
) with underlying graded vector space

\displaystyle V=V^{0}\oplus V^{1}\oplus V^{2}\oplus V^{3}

(4.90)

in the representation category
{}_{\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
, following the
prescription of Section
4.5
we construct the
corresponding 4-term cyclic braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra

(V[[\hbar]],\{\ell_{n}^{\star}\},\langle-,-\rangle_{\star})
(over
\mathbbm{C}[[\hbar]]
) in the twisted representation category

{}_{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
. Since
\ell_{1}^{\star}=\ell_{1}
, this braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra has the same underlying cochain complex (
2.45
). In other words the noncommutative field theories obtained in this way have the same underlying free field theory as their classical versions, as in the case of the star-gauge theories of Section
3.1
. Only the interactions are different and they are encoded in the higher braided brackets
\ell_{n}^{\star}
with
n\geq 2
.

The (left) braided gauge transformation of a field
A\in V^{1}
by a gauge parameter
\lambda\in V^{0}
is defined by

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}A:=\ell_{1}^{\star}(\lambda)+\sum_{n\geq 1}\,\frac{(-1)^{n\choose 2}}{n!}\,\ell_{n+1}^{\star}(\lambda,A^{\otimes n})\ ,

(4.91)

as in the classical case (
2.46
), where now the positioning of
\lambda
inside the slots of the brackets
\ell_{n+1}^{\star}
matters because of braided symmetry (
4.75
). The braided gauge variations are extended to maps
\delta^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}_{\lambda}:V\to V
of degree
0
in the usual way as braided derivations. For the field theories we work with in this paper, whereby the algebra of gauge variations closes off-shell with field-independent gauge parameters, they close a braided Lie algebra under the braided commutator:

\displaystyle\big[\delta_{\lambda_{1}}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}},\delta_{\lambda_{2}}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\big]_{\circ}^{\star}=\delta_{-\ell^{\star}_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\ .

(4.92)

The braided closure property (
4.92
) is shown in
[
45
]
using the braided homotopy Jacobi identity (
4.74
) evaluated on
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},A^{\otimes n})
, order by order in the expansion in
A^{\otimes n}
. The calculations are considerably more involved than in the classical case
[
60
]
, due to the braided Leibniz rule (
4.46
) obeyed by the braided gauge variations, and because the braided symmetry properties (
4.75
) prevent one from combining various sums and simplifying the homotopy relations. The identities (
4.19
) are instrumental in manipulating the proliferation of
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix factors which ensue. We take the validity of this result, and the others which follow below, as a highly non-trivial vindication that braided gauge symmetries and covariant dynamics are controlled by the tightly knit algebraic structure of a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

The equations of motion are dictated by a braided version of the Maurer–Cartan equations
\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}=0
in
V^{2}[[\hbar]]
for a field
A\in V^{1}
, where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A}:=\ell_{1}^{\star}(A)+\sum_{n\geq 2}\,\frac{(-1)^{n\choose 2}}{n!}\,\ell^{\star}_{n}(A^{\otimes n})\ ,

(4.93)

exactly as in the classical case (
2.48
). The dynamics are braided covariant, that is, the expression (
4.93
) is covariant under the braided gauge variations (
4.91
):

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\,\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A}=\ell_{2}^{\star}(\lambda,\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star})+\sum_{n\geq 1}\,\frac{(-1)^{n\choose 2}}{(n+1)!}\ \sum_{i=0}^{n}\,(-1)^{i}\,\ell^{\star}_{n+2}\big(\lambda,A^{\otimes i},\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A},A^{\otimes n-i}\big)\ ,

(4.94)

for all gauge parameters
\lambda\in V^{0}
, as shown in
[
45
]
order by order using the braided homotopy Jacobi identity (
4.74
) evaluated on
(\lambda,A^{\otimes n})
. In the classical case, where
{\mathcal{R}}=1\otimes 1
, the expression (
4.94
) agrees with (
2.49
).

The absence of a (naive) moduli space of solutions to
\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}=0
that we observed in the case of braided Chern–Simons theory can be seen in this formalism to be a generic feature of noncommutative field theories with braided gauge symmetries: Although (
4.94
) implies that
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}=0
on solutions
A\in V^{1}
of the field equations
\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}=0
, computing the first order variation in any infinitesimal gauge parameter
\lambda\in V^{0}
gives

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A+\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}A}=\ell_{1}^{\star}\big(A+\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}A\big)+\sum_{n\geq 2}\,\frac{(-1)^{n\choose 2}}{n!}\,\Big(\ell_{n}^{\star}(A^{\otimes n})+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\,\ell_{n}^{\star}\big(A^{\otimes i},\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}A,A^{\otimes n-1-i}\big)\Big)+O(\lambda^{2})\ .

(4.95)

This cannot be brought to the form
\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A}+\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\,\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}+O(\lambda^{2})
because of the braided Leibniz rule (
4.46
), and it will generally differ from the result of (
4.94
) by
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix factors in the sum over
i
. Hence
\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A+\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}A}\neq\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A}+\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\,\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A}+O(\lambda^{2})
in general, and it does not make sense to take the (naive) quotient of the subspace in
V^{1}
of classical solutions by braided gauge transformations. We will return to this point in Section
4.9
where we explain how a braided version of the Maurer–Cartan moduli space
{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}\mathscr{C}(V[[\hbar]],\{\ell^{\star}_{n}\})
can still be defined from a more abstract perspective.

From a physical perspective, generalized braided gauge symmetries are well-defined through a braided version of the Noether identities which hold off-shell and encode gauge redundancies, analogously to the classical case. These identities follow from the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism, which also naturally controls kinematical violations through the braided homotopy Jacobi identities, such as the well-known failure of the Bianchi identity in braided geometry that we discussed in Section
4.4
. Analogously to the classical case, the braided Noether identities follow from a weighted sum over
n\geq 1
of the braided homotopy Jacobi identity (
4.74
) evaluated on
A^{\otimes n}
. However, because of the braided symmetry properties (
4.75
), we do not have available the simplification (
2.54
) which took place in the classical case and the weighted sum is very cumbersome to work with.

For the field theories we treat in this paper, where the gauge transformations are at most linear in the field
A
, the braided Noether identities are encoded by
[
45
]

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathsf{d}}^{\star}_{A}\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A}&:=\ell_{1}^{\star}(\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A})+\frac{1}{2}\,\big(\ell_{2}^{\star}(\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A},A)-\ell^{\star}_{2}(A,\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A})\big)-\sum_{n\geq 3}\,\frac{(-1)^{n\choose 2}}{n!}\,\ell_{1}^{\star}\big(\ell^{\star}_{n}(A^{\otimes n})\big)\\
&\quad\,-\sum_{n\geq 2}\,\frac{(-1)^{n\choose 2}}{2\,n!}\,\Big(\ell^{\star}_{2}\big(\ell^{\star}_{n}(A^{\otimes n}),A\big)-\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(A,\ell_{n}^{\star}(A^{\otimes n})\big)\Big)\ =\ 0\ .\end{split}

(4.96)

This uses the simplification provided by the braided homotopy Jacobi identity (
4.74
) evaluated on
A^{\otimes n+1}
which in this case reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\,\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(\ell_{n}^{\star}(A^{\otimes n-i},\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{\otimes i})),\mathsf{R}^{k}(A)\big)&=\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(A,\ell_{n}^{\star}(A^{\otimes n})\big)-\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(\ell_{n}^{\star}(A^{\otimes n}),A\big)\\
&\quad\,-(-1)^{n}\,\ell_{1}^{\star}\big(\ell_{n+1}^{\star}(A^{\otimes n+1})\big)\ ,\end{split}

(4.97)

for all
n\geq 2
.
Unlike the classical case (
2.53
), the braided Noether identity (
4.96
) is no longer linear in the field equations
\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}
, while the inhomogeneous terms involving solely the field
A
cancel in the classical limit
{\mathcal{R}}=1\otimes 1
after using (
4.97
). This generalizes the braided Chern–Simons Noether identity in the second line of (
4.70
), which is recovered after applying (
4.97
).

While the braided Noether identities evidently exhibit gauge redundancies through inhomogeneous differential identities among the field equations, we can understand their precise correspondence to braided gauge symmetries by appealing to a variational principle for the braided field theory. Using the cyclic inner product
\langle-,-\rangle_{\star}
on our braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra we define a braided version of the Maurer–Cartan action functional by

\displaystyle S_{\star}(A)=\frac{1}{2}\,\langle A,\ell_{1}^{\star}(A)\rangle_{\star}+\sum_{n\geq 2}\,\frac{(-1)^{n\choose 2}}{(n+1)!}\,\langle A,\ell^{\star}_{n}(A^{\otimes n})\rangle_{\star}\ ,

(4.98)

for all fields
A\in V^{1}
, exactly as in the classical case (
2.56
).

If
\mathcal{F}
is a compatible Drinfel’d twist, in the sense of Definition
4.86
, then using the ordinary Leibniz rule for general field variations
\delta A
on the tangent space
TV^{1}
to the space of fields we can easily compute the variation of (
4.98
) exactly as in the classical case to get

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta S_{\star}(A)&=\sum_{n\geq 1}\,\frac{(-1)^{n\choose 2}}{(n+1)!}\,\Big(\langle\delta A,\ell_{n}^{\star}(A^{\otimes n})\rangle_{\star}+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\,\langle A,\ell_{n}^{\star}(A^{\otimes i},\delta A,A^{\otimes n-i-1})\rangle_{\star}\Big)\\[4.0pt]
&=\sum_{n\geq 1}\,\frac{(-1)^{n\choose 2}}{n!}\,\langle\delta A,\ell^{\star}_{n}(A^{\otimes n})\rangle_{\star}=\langle\delta A,\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A}\rangle_{\star}\ ,\end{split}

(4.99)

where in the second equality we used strict cyclicity for compatible twists (see Corollary
4.88
) to set all terms in the sum over
i
equal to the first term. Hence by non-degeneracy of the pairing, the extrema of the action functional (
4.98
) are precisely the solutions
A\in V^{1}
of the equations of motion
\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A}=0
.

Braided gauge invariance of the action functional (
4.98
) is a bit more subtle, because the braided derivation property of
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}
is not generally compatible with the strict symmetry (
4.87
) of the inner product. Nevertheless, the gauge variation
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}S_{\star}(A)
of the action functional (
4.98
)
is
well-defined, because the strict cyclicity condition (
4.89
) ensures compatibility with strict symmetry of the pairing order by order in tensor powers of the fields
A^{\otimes n}
for
n\geq 1

[
45
]
, that is,

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\big\langle A,\ell^{\star}_{n}\big(A^{\otimes n}\big)\big\rangle_{\star}=\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\big\langle\ell^{\star}_{n}\big(A^{\otimes n}\big),A\big\rangle_{\star}\ .

(4.100)

However, in contrast to the classical case, a braided gauge variation
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}A
of a field
A\in V^{1}
along a gauge parameter
\lambda\in V^{0}
is no longer a special direction of a general variation
\delta A
of the field in the tangent space
TV^{1}
, because the gauge variations are now defined as
braided
derivations, in contrast to the general variations. In other words, in the braided case the first equality in (
2.57
) simply no longer holds in general:

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}S_{\star}(A)\ \neq\ \langle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}A,\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A}\rangle_{\star}\ ,

(4.101)

which can be seen explicitly by direct calculation.

Braided gauge invariance of the action functional, that is,
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}S_{\star}(A)=0
for all
\lambda\in V^{0}
, follows from an order by order calculation using the braided homotopy Jacobi identity (
4.74
) applied to
A^{\otimes n+1}

[
45
]
; this holds irrespectively of the strict or braided cyclicity of the inner product, that is, it is true for generic twists
\mathcal{F}
. Despite (
4.101
), the gauge variation of the action functional (
4.98
) can moreover be written in the form

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}S_{\star}(A)=-\langle\lambda,{\mathsf{d}}_{A}^{\star}\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A}\rangle_{\star}\ ,

(4.102)

exactly as in the classical case (
2.57
), by using cyclicity together with the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix identities (
4.19
) to isolate the gauge parameter
\lambda
. In this sense the operation
{\sf d}^{\star}_{A}
is the ‘braided adjoint’ to
-\delta^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}_{\lambda}
with respect to the cyclic inner product, and the braided Noether identities
{\mathsf{d}}^{\star}_{A}\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A}=0
then follow from braided gauge invariance
\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}S_{\star}(A)=0
of the action functional (
4.98
). We stress, however, that
{\sf d}^{\star}_{A}
is not what would normally be called the adjoint operator with respect to a non-degenerate pairing, as for example in (
2.62
), as this notion would take as starting point the right-hand side
\langle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}A,\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A}\rangle_{\star}
of the non-equality (
4.101
). Since
\langle\delta_{\lambda}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}A,\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A}\rangle_{\star}\neq 0
, the usual adjoint operator does not compose to zero with the braided Maurer–Cartan operator, as is reflected directly in the braided Noether identities (
4.96
).

The procedure described here yields systematic constructions of
new
examples of noncommutative field theories which are braided deformations of classical field theories. These braided theories contain no new degrees of freedom compared with their classical versions, while still possessing good classical limits at
\hbar=0
. Unlike our motivating example of Section
4.4
, in some cases the braided field theories exhibit some surprising features that are not simply the naive deformations of those found in the classical cases, in startling contrast to the conventional noncommutative field theories discussed in Section
3
. In addition to the braided Chern–Simons theory discussed in Section
4.4
, the examples of braided Einstein–Cartan–Palatini theories of gravity in three and four dimensions are worked out in detail in
[
45
]
.

In the subsequent sections we present three new examples: the standard noncommutative scalar field theory (regarded as a braided field theory), a braided version of
BF
theory in arbitrary dimensionality, and a braided version of noncommutative Yang–Mills theory for arbitrary gauge algebras. In the remainder of the present section we address some further technical points surrounding the constructions above.

4.7
Reality conditions

We have not yet addressed the reality conditions that are needed to make physical sense of the field equations as well as of the action functional of a braided field theory. For this, we need to restrict to special twists.

Definition 4.103
.

A Drinfel’d twist
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]\otimes\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
is
Hermitian
if it satisfies the reality condition
\mathcal{F}^{*}=\mathcal{F}_{21}
, where
∗
denotes complex conjugation.

For the inverse twist
\mathcal{F}^{-1}=\bar{\sf f}^{k}\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{k}
the condition of Definition
4.103
reads

\displaystyle\bar{\sf f}^{k}{}^{*}\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{k}{}^{*}=\bar{\sf f}_{k}\otimes\bar{\sf f}^{k}\ .

(4.104)

In particular, this turns the conjugation isomorphism into an anti-involution of the braided exterior algebra, that is,

\displaystyle(\alpha\wedge_{\star}\beta)^{*}=(-1)^{|\alpha|\,|\beta|}\,\beta^{*}\wedge_{\star}\alpha^{*}

(4.105)

for homogeneous forms
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M)[[\hbar]]
, and in this case we say that
\wedge_{\star}
is a
Hermitian star-product
. A large class of examples is provided by abelian twists (see Example
4.31
), which are Hermitian when we use the convention
\hbar^{\ast}=\hbar
for the formal deformation parameter.

Example 4.106
.

In the setting of Section
4.3
, let
A\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]]
be a braided gauge field and
\phi\in\Omega^{p}(M,W)[[\hbar]]
a braided matter field in a braided representation
W
of a gauge algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, which are both real:
A^{*}=A
and
\phi^{*}=\phi
. If the twist
\mathcal{F}
is Hermitian, then the complex conjugate of the left braided covariant derivative of
\phi
from (
4.48
) is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{A}\phi\big)^{*}&=\big(\mathrm{d}\phi+A\wedge_{\star}\phi\big)^{*}\\[4.0pt]
&=\mathrm{d}\phi^{*}+\big(\bar{\sf f}^{k}(A)\wedge\bar{\sf f}_{k}(\phi)\big)^{\ast}\\[4.0pt]
&=\mathrm{d}\phi^{*}+\bar{\sf f}_{k}(A^{*})\wedge\bar{\sf f}^{k}(\phi^{*})\\[4.0pt]
&=\mathrm{d}\phi+\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\phi)=\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{A}\phi\ ,\end{split}

(4.107)

where in the last line we used
\mathcal{F}_{21}^{-1}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\,{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}
.
Thus neither the left nor right braided covariant derivative preserves the reality of the matter field
\phi
in general. However, their symmetrized combination

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{A}+\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{A}\big)

(4.108)

does preserve reality, and in examples this is the combination that will naturally appear, as guaranteed by the general formalism below (cf. Section
4.4
).

Let
(V,\{\ell_{n}\},\langle-,-\rangle)
be a cyclic 4-term

L_{\infty}
-algebra over
\mathbbm{R}
encoding a classical Lagrangian field theory on a manifold
M
. This theory describes real dynamical fields, that is, they belong to a real vector space
V^{1}
which is a module over
C^{\infty}(M,\mathbbm{R})
. Similarly, the field equations are real and the action functional is real-valued. Twist deformation via a compatible Hermitian Drinfel’d twist
\mathcal{F}
yields a 4-term strictly cyclic braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra

(V[[\hbar]],\{\ell_{n}^{\star}\},\langle-,-\rangle_{\star})
over
\mathbbm{C}[[\hbar]]
. We will now show that the reality of the classical field theory survives this deformation.

Firstly, the braided field equations
\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}\in V^{2}[[\hbar]]
are real when restricted to real fields
A\in V^{1}[[\hbar]]
. Conjugating the brackets
\ell_{n}^{\star}(A^{\otimes n})\in V^{2}[[\hbar]]
yields brackets in the complex conjugate vector space
V^{2}[[\hbar]]^{*}
given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell_{n}^{\star}(A^{\otimes n})^{*}&=\ell_{n}\big(\bar{\sf f}^{k_{1}}(A)\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{k_{1}}(\bar{\sf f}^{k_{2}}(A)\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{k_{2}}(\cdots\bar{\sf f}^{k_{n-1}}(A)\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{k_{n-1}}(A))\cdots))\big)^{*}\\[4.0pt]
&=\ell_{n}\big(\bar{\sf f}_{k_{1}}(A^{*})\otimes\bar{\sf f}^{k_{1}}(\bar{\sf f}_{k_{2}}(A^{*})\otimes\bar{\sf f}^{k_{2}}(\cdots\bar{\sf f}_{k_{n-1}}(A^{*})\otimes\bar{\sf f}^{k_{n-1}}(A^{*}))\cdots))\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=\ell_{n}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k_{1}}(A^{*})\otimes_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k_{1}}(\mathsf{R}_{k_{2}}(A^{*})\otimes_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k_{2}}(\cdots\mathsf{R}_{k_{n-1}}(A^{*})\otimes_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k_{n-1}}(A^{*}))\cdots))\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=\ell_{n}^{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k_{1}}(A^{*})\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k_{1}}(\mathsf{R}_{k_{2}}(A^{*})\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k_{2}}(\cdots\mathsf{R}_{k_{n-1}}(A^{*})\otimes\mathsf{R}^{k_{n-1}}(A^{*}))\cdots))\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=\ell_{n}^{\star}(A^{*}{}^{\otimes n})\ ,\end{split}

(4.109)

where
A^{*}\in V^{1}[[\hbar]]^{*}
. In the second line we repeatedly used Hermiticity of the twist and compatibility of the classical brackets with complex conjugation,
{}^{*}\circ\ell_{n}=\ell_{n}\circ{}^{*}
(as they are
\mathbbm{R}
-multilinear maps extended linearly over the complexification of the underlying vector space), and in the third line we used the definition of the
\mathcal{R}
-matrix
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{F}_{21}\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}
. In the last two lines we used the definition of the braided brackets along with their braided graded antisymmetry. This shows

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star})^{*}=\mathfrak{F}_{A^{*}}^{\star}\ ,

(4.110)

and in particular the field equations are real on the subspace of fields where the conjugation isomorphism
A\mapsto A^{*}
is the identity.

Secondly, the braided action functional
S_{\star}:V^{1}[[\hbar]]\to\mathbbm{C}[[\hbar]]
is real-valued when restricted to real fields. This follows similarly to the computation above:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big[\langle A,\ell_{n}^{\star}(A^{\otimes n})\rangle_{\star}\big]^{*}&=\big[\big\langle\bar{\sf f}^{k}(A),\bar{\sf f}_{k}\big(\ell_{n}^{\star}(A^{\otimes n})\big)\big\rangle\big]^{*}\\[4.0pt]
&=\big\langle\bar{\sf f}_{k}(A^{*}),\bar{\sf f}^{k}\big(\ell_{n}^{\star}(A^{\otimes n})^{*}\big)\big\rangle\\[4.0pt]
&=\big\langle\mathsf{R}_{k}(A^{*}),\mathsf{R}^{k}\big(\ell_{n}^{\star}(A^{*}{}^{\otimes n})\big)\big\rangle_{\star}=\langle A^{*},\ell_{n}^{\star}(A^{*}{}^{\otimes n})\rangle_{\star}\ ,\end{split}

(4.111)

where in the last equality we additionally used the compatibility of the twist. This shows that
S_{\star}(A)^{*}=S_{\star}(A^{*})
, and the required reality follows.

4.8
Braided BV formalism

Let us now briefly explain how the notion of cyclic braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
(V[[\hbar]],\{\ell_{n}^{\star}\},\langle-,-\rangle_{\star})
in terms of star-brackets and braided homotopy relations is related to braided versions of the algebraic and coalgebraic formulations from Section
2.2
. The Drinfel’d twist deformation prescription defines the braided symmetric algebra with deformed symmetric product
\odot_{\star}:=\odot\circ\mathcal{F}^{-1}
, which we denote by
\mathrm{Sym}_{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}(V[1])
. On generators
v,v^{\prime}\in V[1]
this product is braided graded commutative:

\displaystyle v\odot_{\star}v^{\prime}=(-1)^{|v|\,|v^{\prime}|}\,\mathsf{R}_{k}(v^{\prime})\odot_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(v)\ .

(4.112)

Following
[
77
]
, it is straightforward to develop the Chevalley–Eilenberg algebra of a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra (as a braided noncommutative deformation of Definition
2.32
), that is, a braided version of the BV formalism presented in Section
2.3
. For this, we extend the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure on
V[[\hbar]]
to
\mathrm{Sym}_{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}(V[2])\otimes V[[\hbar]]
via the brackets

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell_{n}^{\star\,{\rm ext}}(\zeta_{1}\otimes v_{1},\dots,\zeta_{n}\otimes v_{n}):&\!=\ell_{n}^{\,\rm ext}\big((\zeta_{1}\otimes v_{1})\otimes_{\star}\cdots\otimes_{\star}(\zeta_{n}\otimes v_{n})\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=\pm\,\big(\zeta_{1}\odot_{\star}\mathsf{R}_{k_{1}^{\textrm{\tiny 1}}}(\zeta_{2})\odot_{\star}\cdots\odot_{\star}\mathsf{R}_{k_{n-1}^{1}}\cdots\mathsf{R}_{k_{1}^{n-1}}(\zeta_{n})\big)\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\,\otimes\,\ell_{n}^{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}^{k_{n-1}^{1}}\cdots\mathsf{R}^{k_{1}^{1}}(v_{1}),\dots,\mathsf{R}^{k_{1}^{n-1}}(v_{n-1}),v_{n}\big)\ ,\end{split}

(4.113)

for all
n\geq 1
,
\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{n}\in\mathrm{Sym}_{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}(V[2])
and
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in V
. Similarly, we extend the cyclic structure via the pairing

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\zeta_{1}\otimes v_{1},\zeta_{2}\otimes v_{2}\rangle_{\star}^{\rm ext}:=\langle(\zeta_{1}\otimes v_{1})\otimes_{\star}(\zeta_{2}\otimes v_{2})\rangle^{\rm ext}=\pm\,\big(\zeta_{1}\odot_{\star}\mathsf{R}_{k}(\zeta_{2})\big)\,\langle\mathsf{R}^{k}(v_{1}),v_{2}\rangle_{\star}\ .\end{split}

(4.114)

We can now define a braided version of the BV differential
Q_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}^{\star}:\mathrm{Sym}_{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}(V[1])^{*}\to\mathrm{Sym}_{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}(V[1])^{*}
by

\displaystyle Q_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}^{\star}\xi=-\sum_{n\geq 1}\,\frac{(-1)^{n\choose 2}}{n!}\,\ell_{n}^{\star\,\rm ext}(\xi^{\otimes n})\ ,

(4.115)

where the contracted coordinate functions

\displaystyle\xi:=\tau^{\alpha}\otimes\tau_{\alpha}\ \in\ \mathrm{Sym}_{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}(V[2])\otimes V[[\hbar]]

(4.116)

of degree
1
are defined by a choice of basis
\{\tau_{\alpha}\}\subset V
with dual basis
\{\tau^{\alpha}\}\subset V^{*}\simeq V[3]
. Similarly, we define a braided version of the BV symplectic
2
-form

\displaystyle\omega_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}^{\star}:=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\langle\delta\xi,\delta\xi\rangle_{\star}^{\rm ext}\ \in\ \Omega^{2}(V[1])[[\hbar]]\ ,

(4.117)

whose inverse is a braided graded Poisson bracket
\{-,-\}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}^{\star}
, that is, a braided graded Lie bracket which is a braided graded derivation on
\mathrm{Sym}_{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}(V[2])
in each of its slots, and which moreover is compatible with the differential
Q_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}^{\star}
. The action of the braided BV differential can then be represented in terms of this braided Poisson bracket as

\displaystyle Q_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}^{\star}=\{S^{\star}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}},-\}^{\star}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\ ,

(4.118)

where the braided BV action functional

\displaystyle S^{\star}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}=\sum_{n\geq 1}\,\frac{(-1)^{n\choose 2}}{(n+1)!}\,\langle\xi,\ell_{n}^{\star\,\rm ext}(\xi^{\otimes n})\rangle_{\star}^{\rm ext}\ \in\ \mathrm{Sym}_{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}(V[2])

(4.119)

of degree
0
satisfies the classical master equation

\displaystyle\{S^{\star}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}},S^{\star}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\}^{\star}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}=0\ .

(4.120)

A crucial point here is that the classical master equation, or equivalently nilpotency
(Q_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}^{\star})^{2}=0
, follows from precisely the same calculation as in the ordinary case
[
60
, Section 4.3]
. This is because the contracted coordinate functions
\xi
are
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-invariant elements of
\mathrm{Sym}_{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}(V[2])\otimes V[[\hbar]]
,
26
26

26

Invariance of
\xi
follows from either a direct calculation or by the
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-isomorphism
V\otimes V^{*}\simeq{\rm Hom}_{{}_{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}}(V,V)
, under which
\xi
corresponds to the identity morphism on
V
.
and hence all appearances of
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrices in the properties of the extended brackets
\ell_{n}^{\star\,\rm ext}
and of the extended pairing
\langle-,-\rangle_{\star}^{\rm ext}
disappear when evaluated on tensor powers of the
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-invariant element
\xi
. Another important feature is that this differential graded algebra perspective circumvents the issues discussed in Section
4.5
with defining a moduli space
{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}\mathscr{C}(V[[\hbar]],\{\ell^{\star}_{n}\})
of classical solutions in the braided setting. This is because it characterises this space by its differential graded algebra
{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{A}_{\textrm{\tiny MC}}
of equivariant functions, and so the (coarse) moduli space may be defined through the degree
0
cohomology of the differential graded braided commutative algebra
\big(\mathrm{Sym}_{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}(V[1])^{*},Q_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}^{\star}\big)
. These two observations will be combined together in Section
4.9
below to sketch a precise description of the moduli space
{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}\mathscr{C}(V[[\hbar]],\{\ell^{\star}_{n}\})
which resolves the technical issues mentioned in Section
4.5
.

In a similar vein, one can derive a duality with differential graded braided cocommutative coalgebras (as a braided noncommutative deformation of Definition
2.19
). The braided symmetric algebra
\mathrm{Sym}_{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}(V[1])
has a natural free noncocommutative coalgebra structure with coproduct
\Delta_{\textrm{\tiny$V$}}^{\star}:\mathrm{Sym}_{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}(V[1])\to\mathrm{Sym}_{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}(V[1])\otimes\mathrm{Sym}_{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}(V[1])
obtained by Drinfel’d twist deformation of the cocommutative coproduct (
2.18
) in the usual way, replacing permutation actions
\tau^{\sigma}:V[1]^{\otimes n}\to V[1]^{\otimes n}
with
\tau_{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}^{\sigma}
for
\sigma\in S_{n}
. The braided graded symmetric multilinear maps

\displaystyle b_{n}^{\star}:=s\circ\ell_{n}^{\star}\circ(s^{-1})^{\otimes n}

(4.121)

of degree
1
define a map
\sum_{n\geq 1}\,b_{n}^{\star}:\mathrm{Sym}_{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}(V[1])\to(V[1])[[\hbar]]
, which uniquely extends to a coderivation

\displaystyle D_{\star}:\mathrm{Sym}_{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}(V[1])\longrightarrow\mathrm{Sym}_{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}(V[1])

(4.122)

of degree
1
. This is a differential,
(D_{\star})^{2}=0
, as a consequence of the braided homotopy Jacobi identities, and it too provides a natural setting for the moduli space of classical solutions
{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}\mathscr{C}(V[[\hbar]],\{\ell^{\star}_{n}\})
as the degree
0
cohomology of the corresponding differential graded braided cocommutative coalgebra
(\mathrm{Sym}_{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}(V[1]),D_{\star})
.

The coalgebra formulation also provides a natural interpretation of a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra as a deformation of an
L_{\infty}
-algebra in the category
{}_{\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}

[
52
]
: The classical differential graded coalgebra
(\mathrm{Sym}(V[1]),D)
naturally inherits the structure of a cocommutative Hopf algebra compatible with the differential
D
,
27
27

27

More precisely, here one needs to work with the unreduced symmetric algebra and curved
L_{\infty}
-algebras.
which can be twisted to a new noncocommutative Hopf algebra by the techniques of Section
4.2
with a compatible differential
D_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}
. The braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
(V[[\hbar]],D_{\star})
may then be interpreted as an
L_{\infty}
-module for the resulting twisted
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

4.9
Moduli spaces of classical solutions

We conclude this section by briefly sketching how to potentially make sense of the moduli space of classical solutions
{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}\mathscr{C}(V[[\hbar]],\{\ell^{\star}_{n}\})
in braided field theory, which we refer to as the ‘braided Maurer--Cartan moduli space’. We shall be intentionally brief and gloss over many technical details in order to illustrate the main ideas;
28
28

28

Nevertheless, this is the most technical part of the paper. The reader not interested in the formal descriptions of moduli spaces may safely skip this section, which is not essential to the rest of the paper.
a more thorough and rigorous construction will be presented elsewhere. We start by recalling the description of the classical Maurer–Cartan moduli space
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{C}(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
of an
L_{\infty}
-algebra
(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
, whose construction is originally due to Getzler
[
51
]
, see also
[
80
]
for a more explicit alternative construction and
[
75
]
for the special case when
(V,\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
is a differential graded Lie algebra (as in the example of Chern–Simons gauge theory).

We begin with some preliminary notation. Let
{\sf dAlg}
denote the category of differential graded commutative algebras over
\mathbbm{R}
, whose morphisms are algebra homomorphisms of degree
0
which intertwine the differentials.
29
29

29

The appropriate homotopical analogue generalizing the spectrum of a commutative algebra to differential graded commutative algebras takes place in the
\infty
-category of ‘simplicial’ sets, but this is not needed in our presentation below and we shall work solely with the category
{\sf Set}
of sets. Similarly, to define appropriate notions of representability of functors we should work with the
\infty
-category of ‘Artin’ differential graded commutative algebras in non-positive degrees, but we do not indicate this detail and stick to the full source category
{\sf dAlg}
below.

Let
\mathcal{L}=(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
be a classical
L_{\infty}
-algebra (in the category of real vector spaces). Given a differential graded commutative algebra
\mathsf{A}=(\mathcal{A},{\mathsf{d}}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{A}$}})
(with the product on
\mathcal{A}
denoted by juxtaposition), there is a tensor product
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathsf{A}\otimes\mathcal{L}=\big(\mathcal{A}\otimes V,\big\{\ell_{n}^{\textrm{\tiny$\mathsf{A}$}}\big\}\big)
with homogeneous subspaces
(\mathcal{A}\otimes V)^{k}=\bigoplus_{l\in\mathbbm{Z}}\,\mathcal{A}^{-l}\otimes V^{l+k}
for
k\in\mathbbm{Z}
and brackets given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell_{1}^{\textrm{\tiny$\mathsf{A}$}}(a_{1}\otimes v_{1})=({\mathsf{d}}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{A}$}}+\ell_{1})(a_{1}\otimes v_{1}):&\!={\mathsf{d}}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{A}$}}a_{1}\otimes v_{1}+(-1)^{|a_{1}|}\,a_{1}\otimes\ell_{1}(v_{1})\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{n}^{\textrm{\tiny$\mathsf{A}$}}(a_{1}\otimes v_{1},\dots,a_{n}\otimes v_{n})&=\pm\,(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})\otimes\ell_{n}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})\ ,\end{split}

(4.123)

for all
n\geq 2
,
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
and
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in V
. This generalizes the extended
L_{\infty}
-algebra construction of Section
2.3
.

Following
[
80
]
, the (coarse) Maurer–Cartan moduli space
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{C}(\mathcal{L})
of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathcal{L}=(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
is the set of solutions
A\in V^{1}
of the Maurer–Cartan equation
\mathfrak{F}_{A}=0
, modulo the gauge equivalence relation
A\sim A^{\prime}
if there exists a gauge parameter
\lambda\in V^{0}
and a path of fields
A(t)\in V^{1}
for
t\in[0,1]
with
A(0)=A
,
A(1)=A^{\prime}
which satisfies the differential equation

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}A(t)=\delta_{\lambda}A(t)

(4.124)

for all
t\in[0,1]
. The infinitesimal version of (
4.124
) (its evaluation at
t=0
) gives the usual infinitesimal gauge transformation
\delta_{\lambda}A
of
A
. With the given initial condition and an appropriate completeness condition on the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathcal{L}
, the differential equation (
4.124
) always admits a solution
A(t)
which satisfies the Maurer–Cartan equation for all
t\in[0,1]
, that is,
\mathfrak{F}_{A(t)}=0
. In the case that
\mathcal{L}
is a differential graded Lie algebra, this definition is equivalent to the equivalence relation arising from the (integrated) group action of
V^{0}
on
V^{1}
(see e.g.
[
75
]
).

The moduli space
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{C}(\mathcal{L})
is a (formal derived) stack that is described by its ‘functor of points’
\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{L}}:{\sf dAlg}\to{\sf Set}
, as we now explain. This defines a presheaf on the category of differential graded affine schemes over
\mathbbm{R}
, and so from this perspective the moduli space is described not as a set of objects but rather as a space which parametrizes those objects.

Definition 4.125
.

Let
\mathcal{L}=(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
be an
L_{\infty}
-algebra and
\mathsf{A}=(\mathcal{A},{\mathsf{d}}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{A}$}})
a differential graded commutative algebra.

(a)

A
family of classical solutions
in
\mathcal{L}
parametrized by
\mathsf{A}
is an element
\alpha=a^{l}\otimes v_{l}
, with
a^{l}\in\mathcal{A}^{-l}
and
v_{l}\in V^{l+1}
for
l\in\mathbbm{Z}
, which solves the Maurer–Cartan equation

\displaystyle({\mathsf{d}}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{A}$}}+\ell_{1})\alpha+\sum_{n\geq 2}\,\frac{(-1)^{n\choose 2}}{n!}\,\ell_{n}^{\textrm{\tiny$\mathsf{A}$}}(\alpha^{\otimes n})=0

(4.126)

in the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathsf{A}\otimes\mathcal{L}
.

(b)

Two families
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
of classical solutions in
\mathcal{L}
are
gauge equivalent
if there exists
\lambda=b^{l}\otimes w_{l}
, with
b^{l}\in\mathcal{A}^{-k}
and
w_{l}\in V^{k}
for
l\in\mathbbm{Z}
, and a path
\alpha(t)=a^{l}(t)\otimes v_{l}(t)
in the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathsf{A}\otimes\mathcal{L}
which satisfies the differential equation

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\alpha(t)=({\mathsf{d}}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{A}$}}+\ell_{1})\lambda+\sum_{n\geq 1}\,\frac{(-1)^{n\choose 2}}{n!}\,\ell_{n+1}^{\textrm{\tiny$\mathsf{A}$}}\big(\lambda,\alpha(t)^{\otimes n}\big)\ ,

(4.127)

such that
\alpha=\alpha(0)
and
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha(1)
.

For a family parametrized by a one-point space, that is, for
\mathsf{A}=\mathbbm{R}_{0}:=(\mathbbm{R},0)
with
\mathbbm{R}
sitting in degree
0
, this definition reduces to the notion of gauge equivalence of classical solutions discussed above.

Definition 4.128
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an
L_{\infty}
-algebra.

(a)

The
moduli functor
is the covariant functor

\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{L}}:{\sf dAlg}\to{\sf Set}

which associates to every differential graded commutative algebra
\mathsf{A}
the set
\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{L}}(\mathsf{A})
of gauge equivalence classes of families of classical solutions in
\mathcal{L}
parametrized by
\mathsf{A}
, and to every morphism
f:\mathsf{A}\to\mathsf{B}
of differential graded commutative algebras it associates the pushforward
\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{L}}(f)
sending families parametrized by
\mathsf{A}
to families parametrized by
\mathsf{B}
.

(b)

A (
fine
)
Maurer–Cartan moduli space

\mathscr{M}\mathscr{C}(\mathcal{L})
is a representation of the moduli functor, that is, a pair
(\hat{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{\Phi})
where
\hat{\mathsf{A}}
is an object of the category
{\sf dAlg}
and
{\mathsf{\Phi}}:{\rm Hom}_{\sf dAlg}(\hat{\mathsf{A}},-)\to\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
is a natural transformation of functors.

The idea behind this definition is that the functor
{\rm Hom}_{\sf dAlg}(\hat{\mathsf{A}},-):{\sf dAlg}\to{\sf Set}
describes all possible ways that any other differential graded affine scheme may be mapped to the moduli space, which completely captures the (derived) geometric structure of the moduli scheme given by the spectrum of the differential graded commutative algebra
\hat{\mathsf{A}}
. The distinguished element
\hat{\alpha}={\sf\Phi}(\mathbbm{1}_{\hat{A}})\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{L}}(\hat{\mathsf{A}})
is called the
universal classical solution
: For every pair
(\mathsf{A},\alpha)
with
\mathsf{A}
an object of
{\sf dAlg}
and
\alpha\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{L}}(\mathsf{A})
, there is a unique morphism
{\sf\Lambda}\in{\rm Hom}_{\sf dAlg}(\hat{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{A})
such that
(\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{L}}\circ{\sf\Lambda})(\hat{\alpha})=\alpha
. When it exists, a universal object
(\hat{\mathsf{A}},\hat{\alpha})
is unique up to a unique isomorphism, and in this sense it is the space of
all
classical solutions modulo gauge transformations. For a more accurate and explicit description of the Maurer–Cartan moduli space using simplicial methods, see
[
80
]
.

This description of the moduli space of classical solutions can be adapted to the braided noncommutative setting in the following way, which uses a description of functors of points similar to those studied in
[
23
]
in the context of toric noncommutative geometry. For this, let now
\mathcal{L}=(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
be an
L_{\infty}
-algebra in the category
{}_{\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
of left
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
-modules. Similarly, we work with differential graded commutative algebras in the category
{}_{\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
. These form the category
{}^{\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}}{\sf dAlg}
of differential graded commutative left
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
-module algebras, whose morphisms are equivariant algebra homomorphisms of degree
0
intertwining the differentials. The description of the Maurer–Cartan moduli space
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{C}(\mathcal{L})
proceeds exactly as above, by restricting the moduli functor
\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{L}}:{\sf dAlg}\to{\sf Set}
to the subcategory
{}^{\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}}{\sf dAlg}
.

Given a Drinfel’d twist
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]\otimes\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}[[\hbar]]
, by Proposition
4.81
we obtain a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathcal{L}^{\star}=(V[[\hbar]],\{\ell_{n}^{\star}\})
. Similarly, by the usual constructions of Section
4.2
we obtain a (functorially equivalent) category
{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}{\sf dAlg}
of differential graded braided commutative algebras. An object
\mathsf{A}^{\star}=(\mathcal{A},{\mathsf{d}}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{A}$}})
of this category consists of a graded braided commutative algebra
\mathcal{A}
(with the product on
\mathcal{A}
denoted by
\star
), that is,

\displaystyle a\star a^{\prime}=(-1)^{|a|\,|a^{\prime}|}\,\mathsf{R}_{k}(a^{\prime})\star\mathsf{R}^{k}(a)\ ,

(4.129)

for all homogeneous
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
, together with a differential
{\mathsf{d}}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{A}$}}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
of degree
1
which is a graded derivation, that is,
{\mathsf{d}}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{A}$}}(a\star a^{\prime})={\mathsf{d}}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{A}$}}a\star a^{\prime}+(-1)^{|a|}\,a\star{\mathsf{d}}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{A}$}}a^{\prime}
. There is a braided tensor product
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathsf{A}^{\star}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\star}=\big(\mathcal{A}\otimes V[[\hbar]],\big\{\ell_{n}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny$\mathsf{A}$}}}\big\}\big)
whose differential is unchanged from the classical case (
4.123
), while the higher brackets of (
4.123
) are modified to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell_{n}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny$\mathsf{A}$}}}(a_{1}\otimes v_{1},\dots,a_{n}\otimes v_{n})&=\pm\,\big(a_{1}\star\mathsf{R}_{k_{1}^{\textrm{\tiny 1}}}(a_{2})\star\cdots\star\mathsf{R}_{k_{n-1}^{1}}\cdots\mathsf{R}_{k_{1}^{n-1}}(a_{n})\big)\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\,\otimes\,\ell_{n}^{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}^{k_{n-1}^{1}}\cdots\mathsf{R}^{k_{1}^{1}}(v_{1}),\dots,\mathsf{R}^{k_{1}^{n-1}}(v_{n-1}),v_{n}\big)\ ,\end{split}

(4.130)

for all
n\geq 2
,
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
and
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in V
. This generalizes the extended braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra construction of Section
4.8
.

Definition 4.131
.

Let
\mathcal{L}^{\star}=(V[[\hbar]],\{\ell^{\star}_{n}\})
be a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra and
\mathsf{A}^{\star}=(\mathcal{A},{\mathsf{d}}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{A}$}})
a differential graded braided commutative algebra.

(a)

A
family of classical solutions
in
\mathcal{L}^{\star}
parametrized by
\mathsf{A}^{\star}
is a
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-invariant element
\alpha=a^{l}\otimes v_{l}
, with
a^{l}\in\mathcal{A}^{-l}
and
v_{l}\in V^{l+1}[[\hbar]]
for
l\in\mathbbm{Z}
, which solves the braided Maurer–Cartan equation

\displaystyle({\mathsf{d}}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{A}$}}+\ell^{\star}_{1})\alpha+\sum_{n\geq 2}\,\frac{(-1)^{n\choose 2}}{n!}\,\ell_{n}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny$\mathsf{A}$}}}(\alpha^{\otimes n})=0

(4.132)

in the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathsf{A}^{\star}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\star}
.

(b)

Two families
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
of classical solutions in
\mathcal{L}^{\star}
are
braided gauge equivalent
if there exists a
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-invariant element
\lambda=b^{l}\otimes w_{l}
, with
b^{l}\in\mathcal{A}^{-k}
and
w_{l}\in V^{k}[[\hbar]]
for
l\in\mathbbm{Z}
, and a
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-invariant path
\alpha(t)=a^{l}(t)\otimes v_{l}(t)
in the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathsf{A}^{\star}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\star}
which satisfies the differential equation

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\alpha(t)=({\mathsf{d}}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{A}$}}+\ell_{1}^{\star})\lambda+\sum_{n\geq 1}\,\frac{(-1)^{n\choose 2}}{n!}\,\ell_{n+1}^{\star{\textrm{\tiny$\mathsf{A}$}}}\big(\lambda,\alpha(t)^{\otimes n}\big)\ ,

(4.133)

such that
\alpha=\alpha(0)
and
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha(1)
.

This is our key definition: part (b) of the definition now makes sense non-trivially, because all
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix insertions disappear when evaluated on the
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-invariant elements of the set of tensor product solutions
\alpha
to the braided Maurer–Cartan equations
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}^{\star}=0
in
\mathsf{A}^{\star}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\star}
, and so braided gauge transformations act as in the classical setting of Definition
4.125
. Note that this does not preclude noncommutative deformations of the solutions compared to Definition
4.125
, as both
\mathsf{A}^{\star}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\star}
are objects in the symmetric monoidal category
{}_{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}
, and so each individually involve braided operations: only in their braided tensor product
\mathsf{A}^{\star}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\star}
do we look for
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-invariant elements. This also clarifies what goes wrong in the setting of Section
4.6
: to recover that situation we would set
\mathsf{A}^{\star}=\mathbbm{R}_{0}
in this definition, which means that the classical solutions should be
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-invariant fields. Then all issues concerning the action of braided gauge transformations, the braided Noether identities and related matters disappear. Of course we are mostly interested in non-invariant fields, but to detect those one has to consider differential graded braided commutative algebras
\mathsf{A}^{\star}
which carry a non-trivial
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-action. In this sense, the braided moduli space does not contain any (interesting) ‘points’, as we further exemplify below.

The description of the braided Maurer–Cartan moduli space is now a simple extension of the classical case, as the latter definitions make sense for covariant functors from any (locally small) category to the category of sets. The definition of moduli functor from part (a) of Definition
4.128
generalizes immediately to give a moduli functor
{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{L}^{\star}}:{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}{\sf dAlg}\to{\sf Set}
which sends each differential graded braided commutative algebra
\mathsf{A}^{\star}
to the set
{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{L}^{\star}}(\mathsf{A}^{\star})
of braided gauge equivalence classes of families of classical solutions in the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathcal{L}^{\star}
parametrized by
\mathsf{A}^{\star}
. Part (b) then generalizes easily in the following manner.

Definition 4.134
.

Let
\mathcal{L}^{\star}
be a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra. A
braided Maurer–Cartan moduli space

{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{M}\mathscr{C}(\mathcal{L}^{\star})
is a pair
(\hat{\mathsf{A}}^{\star},\hat{\alpha}^{\star})
, where
\hat{\mathsf{A}}^{\star}
is an object of the category
{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}{\sf dAlg}
and
\hat{\alpha}^{\star}\in{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{L}^{\star}}(\hat{A}^{\star})
, which is a universal object representing the moduli functor
{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{L}^{\star}}:{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}{\sf dAlg}\to{\sf Set}
.

This definition has the same meaning as in the classical case,
30
30

30

By Yoneda’s lemma, this definition is bijectively equivalent to the existence of a natural transformation
{\sf\Phi}^{\star}:{\rm Hom}_{{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}{\sf dAlg}}(\hat{\mathsf{A}}^{\star},-)\to{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{L}^{\star}}
with
\hat{\alpha}^{\star}={\sf\Phi}^{\star}(\mathbbm{1}_{\hat{\mathsf{A}}^{\star}})
.
but with a crucial difference: since the moduli space is necessarily an object in the source category of the functor
{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{L}^{\star}}
, and we allow noncommutative parameter spaces (regarded as dual to differential graded braided commutative algebras), the braided Maurer–Cartan moduli space is naturally a (differential graded) ‘noncommutative scheme’, which is the tradeoff for making sense of the braided gauge equivalence classes. Of course, the highly non-trivial aspect of this construction, which we do not address here, is whether there actually exists a representation of the functor
{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{L}^{\star}}
which brings our arguments above to fruition, and hence provides a realization of the moduli space of classical solutions in braided field theory.

Noncommutative moduli spaces have previously appeared in braided noncommutative geometry as solution spaces to noncommutative instanton equations in
[
32
]
, where it is shown that the noncommutativity of the parameter spaces is a gauge artefact: one may always recover an equivalent description in terms of the usual notion of classical moduli space by a suitable choice of gauge transformation and a noncommutative quotient construction. It would be interesting to understand the braided Maurer–Cartan moduli space (if it really exists in this sense) in a similar vein, which would elucidate the physical meaning of the abstract arguments presented here.

This braided Maurer–Cartan moduli space may also be extracted from the braided BV formalism of Section
4.8
and its braided noncommutative algebra of functions
{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{A}_{\textrm{\tiny MC}}
. For this, one considers the functor of points
{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{L}^{\star}}^{\textrm{\tiny$\vee$}}:{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}{\sf dAlg}\to{\sf Set}
defined on objects by
{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{L}^{\star}}^{\textrm{\tiny$\vee$}}(\mathsf{A}^{\star})={\rm Hom}_{{}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}{\sf dAlg}}\big({}^{\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}}\mathscr{A}_{\textrm{\tiny MC}},\mathsf{A}^{\star}\big)
. Taking again
\mathsf{A}^{\star}=\mathbbm{R}_{0}
, one only detects
\mathsf{U}_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}\mathfrak{v}
-invariant points, whereas the interesting fields appear for non-trivial differential graded braided commutative algebras
\mathsf{A}^{\star}
.

5
Noncommutative scalar field theory

The
L_{\infty}
-algebra framework also accommodates field theories without gauge symmetries. This simply translates to trivial homogeneous subspaces
V^{0}=V^{3}=\{0\}
and corresponding zero brackets. The appearance of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure is then tautological, as it is just given by polynomial expansion of the field equations. In this section we will review the formalism in the example of an interacting scalar field theory, and subsequently twist deform this
L_{\infty}
-algebra to obtain the standard noncommutative scalar field theory. This example also serves to illustrate how to handle more general theories which are not diffeomorphism invariant, via twist deformation quantization along the global spacetime symmetries of the classical field theory. It moreover shows how to incorporate global symmetries into the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra framework, which we describe following the classical treatment of
[
60
]
.
31
31

31

Of course, we are not interested in a moduli space of globally (braided) symmetric solutions (as these are not gauge symmetries). In principle, one is interested instead in the weakly conserved currents corresponding to these infinitesimal symmetries, which would follow from a braided version of Noether’s first theorem. We do not develop this interesting point in the present paper.

5.1
Scalar field theory in the
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism

Let
(M,g)
be a
d
-dimensional oriented Lorentzian manifold.
32
32

32

The discussion is presented for Lorentzian signature in order to compare later on with previous points of view on global symmetries. The more general pseudo-Riemannian cases differ only by a sign in the Laplace–Beltrami operator.
Let
\mathrm{d}{\rm vol}_{g}\in\Omega^{d}(M)
be the induced volume form and
\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}:\Omega^{k}(M)\to\Omega^{d-k}(M)
the corresponding Hodge duality operator. Denote by
{\square}:=\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}:C^{\infty}(M,\mathbbm{R})\to C^{\infty}(M,\mathbbm{R})
the Laplace–Beltrami operator acting on smooth functions.

The action functional for an interacting real scalar field
\phi\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbbm{R})
is defined by

\displaystyle S(\phi)=\int_{M}\,\Big(\frac{1}{2}\,\phi\,\big(\square-m^{2}\big)\phi-\sum_{n\geq 3}\,\frac{\kappa_{n-1}}{n!}\,\phi^{n}\Big)\ \mathrm{d}{\rm vol}_{g}\ ,

(5.1)

for a mass parameter
m^{2}\geq 0
and coupling constants
\kappa_{n-1}\in\mathbbm{R}
. The corresponding field equations are given by
\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}=0
where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}=\big(\square-m^{2}\big)\phi-\sum_{n\geq 2}\,\frac{\kappa_{n}}{n!}\,\phi^{n}\ ,

(5.2)

from which the cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra is immediately read off.

The underlying graded vector space is concentrated in degrees
1
and
2
, comprised of two copies of the space of functions on
M
:

\displaystyle V=V^{1}\oplus V^{2}\qquad\mbox{with}\quad V^{1}=V^{2}=C^{\infty}(M,\mathbbm{R})\ ,

(5.3)

and we denote its homogeneous elements by
\phi\in V^{1}
and
\varPhi\in V^{2}
. The underlying cochain complex
(V,\ell_{1})
has as differential the Klein–Gordon operator, and the non-trivial brackets are

\displaystyle\ell_{1}(\phi)=\big(\square-m^{2}\big)\phi\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell_{n}(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{n})=-(-1)^{n\choose 2}\,\kappa_{n}\,\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{n}\ ,

(5.4)

where
n\geq 2
, while the cyclic pairing is

\displaystyle\langle\phi,\varPhi\rangle:=\int_{M}\,\phi\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\varPhi=\int_{M}\,\phi\ \varPhi\ \mathrm{d}{\rm vol}_{g}\ .

(5.5)

The homotopy Jacobi identities follow trivially for degree reasons.
Cyclicity with respect to
\ell_{n}
for
n\geq 2
is tautological, due to commutativity of pointwise multiplication of functions. Cyclicity with respect to
\ell_{1}
follows since
\square
is a (formally) self-adjoint operator with respect to the inner product (
5.5
) on
C^{\infty}(M,\mathbbm{R})
.

5.2
Noncommutative scalar field theory in the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism

Suppose now that the spacetime
(M,g)
has a non-trivial set of isometries. That is, there exists a non-zero Lie subalgebra of Killing vector fields
\mathfrak{k}\subset\mathfrak{v}={\sf\Gamma}(TM)
, such that
\mathrm{L}_{\xi}\,g=0
for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{k}
. This implies that

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}_{\xi}\circ\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\circ\mathrm{L}_{\xi}

(5.6)

for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{k}
, and so the Laplace–Beltrami operator
\square=\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}
also commutes with the action of all Killing vector fields. It follows that all brackets (
5.4
) of the scalar field
L_{\infty}
-algebra commute with the action of
\mathfrak{k}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}_{\xi}\circ\ell_{n}=\ell_{n}\circ\mathrm{L}_{\xi}

(5.7)

for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{k}
and
n\geq 1
. In other words,
(V,\{\ell_{n}\})
is an
L_{\infty}
-algebra in the category of
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{k}
-modules.

By the construction of Section
4.5
, a choice of a
Killing
Drinfel’d twist
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{k}[[\hbar]]\otimes\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{k}[[\hbar]]
results in a 2-term braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\big(V[[\hbar]],\{\ell^{\star}_{n}\}\big)
with non-zero brackets

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell_{1}^{\star}(\phi)=\big(\square-m^{2}\big)\phi\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell_{n}^{\star}(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{n})=-(-1)^{n\choose 2}\,\kappa_{n}\,\phi_{1}\star\cdots\star\phi_{n}\ ,\end{split}

(5.8)

where
n\geq 2
and
\star
denotes the star-product of functions. By the prescription of Section
4.6
, the braided field equations landing in
V^{2}[[\hbar]]=C^{\infty}(M,\mathbbm{R})[[\hbar]]
are encoded by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{\phi}=\sum_{n\geq 1}\,\frac{(-1)^{n\choose 2}}{n!}\,\ell_{n}^{\star}(\phi^{\otimes n})=\big(\square-m^{2}\big)\phi-\sum_{n\geq 2}\,\frac{\kappa_{n}}{n!}\,\phi^{\star n}\ ,

(5.9)

where
\phi^{\star n}:=\phi\star\cdots\star\phi
(
n
times). This is just the usual noncommutative deformation of scalar field equations.

The cyclic inner product (
5.5
) is invariant under the action of
\mathfrak{k}
, since
\mathrm{L}_{\xi}\,\mathrm{d}{\rm vol}_{g}=0
for all Killing vector fields
\xi\in\mathfrak{k}
. By twisting we obtain the braided cyclic pairing

\displaystyle\langle\phi,\varPhi\rangle_{\star}:=\int_{M}\,\phi\star\varPhi\ \mathrm{d}{\rm vol}_{g}\ ,

(5.10)

from which we may define the braided action functional following the prescription of Section
4.6
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{\star}(\phi):=\sum_{n\geq 1}\,\frac{(-1)^{n\choose 2}}{(n+1)!}\,\langle\phi,\ell_{n}^{\star}(\phi^{\otimes n})\rangle_{\star}=\int_{M}\,\Big(\frac{1}{2}\,\phi\star\big(\square-m^{2}\big)\phi-\sum_{n\geq 3}\,\frac{\kappa_{n-1}}{n!}\,\phi^{\star n}\Big)\ \mathrm{d}{\rm vol}_{g}\ ,\end{split}

(5.11)

which, again, is the usual action functional for noncommutative scalar field theory; note that, for fields of suitable asymptotic decay, the kinetic term
\phi\star\square\,\phi\ \mathrm{d}{\rm vol}_{g}
may be integrated by parts to
\mathrm{d}\phi\wedge_{\star}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}\phi
. Of course, in order to derive the field equations as the critical locus of this braided action functional in the usual sense, we have to restrict to compatible Drinfel’d twists which make the pairing strictly cyclic, as discussed in Section
4.6
.
