We emphasize that even standard noncommutative scalar field theory is naturally encoded in a
braided

L_{\infty}
-algebra, even though no braided gauge symmetries are present; in other words, it is also an example of a braided field theory.
This can be compared to the standard classical
L_{\infty}
-algebra formulation of noncommutative scalar field theory: in order to maintain strict symmetry of the brackets, as opposed to braided symmetry, one needs to instead symmetrize star-products of fields to write the higher brackets as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n!}\,\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\,\ell_{n}^{\star}(\phi_{\sigma(1)},\dots,\phi_{\sigma(n)})=-(-1)^{n\choose 2}\,\frac{\kappa_{n}}{n!}\,\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\,\phi_{\sigma(1)}\star\cdots\star\phi_{\sigma(n)}\ ,

(5.12)

for
n\geq 2
, where the sum runs over all permutations of degree
n
.
At the classical level this results in the same noncommutative field theory, but at the quantum level different interaction vertices arise. In particular, in the braided case one loses the distinction between planar and non-planar Feynman diagrams from dropping the symmetrization
[
77
]
, raising the possibility that no UV/IR mixing occurs in braided quantum field theory, which as a consequence would have drastically improved renormalizability features over the standard noncommutative quantum field theory
[
84
]
. The braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism also alters the implementation of global symmetries, as we discuss in detail in Section
5.3
below.

5.3
Global braided symmetry

The global symmetries of the classical scalar field theory are the isometries of the spacetime
(M,g)
, which are generated by the Killing Lie algebra
\mathfrak{k}
acting on fields via the Lie derivative. In the noncommutative scalar field theory, only the subalgebra of
\mathfrak{k}
that commutes with the legs
{\sf f}^{k},{\sf f}_{k}\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{k}
of the Killing twist
\mathcal{F}
generates global symmetries in this sense. However, irrespective of the twist, the noncommutative scalar field theory is invariant under the
braided
Lie algebra of twisted Killing symmetries
(\mathfrak{k}[[\hbar]],[-,-]_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\star})
acting on fields via the braided Lie derivative. This is a finite-dimensional “global” or “rigid” braided Lie algebra of symmetries of the field theory. We shall now explain how this is also naturally understood within the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra framework.

To incorporate these global symmetries, we extend the 2-term
L_{\infty}
-algebra of Section
5.1
to a 4-term
L_{\infty}
-algebra by adding in non-zero homogeneous subspaces of degrees
0
and
3
, with corresponding brackets for the action of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{k}
.
33
33

33

Strictly speaking, in view of the derived quotient interpretation of the kinematical part
V^{0}\oplus V^{1}
of the original
L_{\infty}
-algebra, we should really be specifying an external action of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{k}
on
V^{0}\oplus V^{1}\oplus V^{2}\oplus V^{3}
as we are not interested in the (derived) quotient by global symmetries.
Thus the underlying graded vector space is now
V=V^{0}\oplus V^{1}\oplus V^{2}\oplus V^{3}
with

\displaystyle V^{0}=\mathfrak{k}\ ,\quad V^{1}=V^{2}=C^{\infty}(M,\mathbbm{R})\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad V^{3}=\mathfrak{k}^{*}\ ,

(5.13)

where
\mathfrak{k}^{*}
is the linear dual of
\mathfrak{k}
.
We denote elements in degree 0 by
\xi\in\mathfrak{k}
and in degree 3 by
\xi^{\textrm{\tiny$\vee$}}\in\mathfrak{k}^{*}
. Note that these are not fields, but rather should be regarded as constant maps on
M
.

Abstractly, the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{k}
naturally acts on itself by the adjoint action,
\xi_{1}\cdot\xi_{2}:=[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{k}}
for all
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathfrak{k}
, and on its dual by the coadjoint action
\xi\cdot\xi^{\textrm{\tiny$\vee$}}
defined by

\displaystyle\big\langle\xi^{\prime},\xi\cdot\xi^{\textrm{\tiny$\vee$}}\big\rangle=-\big\langle\xi\cdot\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\textrm{\tiny$\vee$}}\big\rangle=-\big\langle[\xi,\xi^{\prime}]_{\mathfrak{k}},\xi^{\textrm{\tiny$\vee$}}\big\rangle

(5.14)

for all
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{k}
and
\xi^{\textrm{\tiny$\vee$}}\in\mathfrak{k}^{*}
, where
\langle-,-\rangle:V^{0}\otimes V^{3}\to\mathbbm{R}
is the canonical dual pairing between
\mathfrak{k}
and
\mathfrak{k}^{*}
. Concretely,
\mathfrak{k}
can be represented by vector fields on
M
acting on fields via the Lie derivative, with
[-,-]_{\mathfrak{k}}
the restriction of the Lie bracket of vector fields to the subalgebra
\mathfrak{k}
. These representations obviously all live in the category of
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{k}
-modules, and hence are amenable to twisting by
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{k}[[\hbar]]\otimes\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{k}[[\hbar]]
via the construction of Section
4
.

The braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
(V[[\hbar]],\{\ell_{n}^{\star}\})
is now extended by including, in addition to the brackets (
5.8
), the non-zero
2
-brackets

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell_{2}^{\star}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=-[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\star}\ ,\quad\ell_{2}^{\star}(\xi,\phi)=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}\,\phi\ ,\quad\ell_{2}^{\star}(\xi,\varPhi)=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}\,\varPhi\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell_{2}^{\star}(\xi,\xi^{\textrm{\tiny$\vee$}})=\xi\star\xi^{\textrm{\tiny$\vee$}}\ ,\end{split}

(5.15)

where
\xi\star\xi^{\textrm{\tiny$\vee$}}
is the left braided coadjoint action of
\xi\in\mathfrak{k}
on
\xi^{\vee}\in\mathfrak{k}^{*}[[\hbar]]
.
That these extra brackets are compatible with the braided homotopy Jacobi identities is a consequence of the fact that they are a twist deformation of the classical brackets of
[
60
]
, from which the statement follows trivially again for degree reasons and since the
\mathfrak{k}
-action commutes with the Laplace--Beltrami operator.
34
34

34

Note that
[
60
]
only considers classical
\phi^{4}
-theory on Minkowski space, but the generalization is immediate.

The cyclic structure
\langle-,-\rangle_{\star}
is naturally extended to the new homogeneous components by twisting the canonical dual pairing
\langle-,-\rangle:V^{0}\otimes V^{3}\to\mathbbm{R}
in the usual way.

These brackets encode the global braided transformation rules for the scalar fields:

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\phi=\ell_{2}^{\star}(\xi,\phi)=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}\,\phi\ .

(5.16)

These close the braided Lie algebra

\displaystyle\big[\delta_{\xi_{1}}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}},\delta_{\xi_{2}}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\big]_{\circ}^{\star}=\delta^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}_{-\ell_{2}^{\star}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}=\delta_{[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\star}}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\ ,

(5.17)

which follows directly by the closure property of the braided Lie derivative
\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}
. The braided field equations (
5.9
) are covariant,

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}^{\star}=\ell_{2}^{\star}(\xi,\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}^{\star})=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}\,\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}^{\star}\ ,

(5.18)

which follows directly from the braided derivation property of
\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}
. Finally, the noncommutative action functional (
5.11
) is invariant under these global braided transformations,
\delta_{\xi}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}S_{\star}(\phi)=0
, which may also be checked directly. In the classical limit, these are all just the correct global symmetry transformation properties of classical scalar field theory.

Abelian twists

Consider the special case where the Drinfel’d twist
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{t}[[\hbar]]\otimes\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{t}[[\hbar]]
is constructed on the enveloping Hopf algebra of the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{t}\subseteq\mathfrak{k}
, i.e. from a commuting set of Killing vector fields of the spacetime
(M,g)
. Then
\mathcal{F}
is an abelian twist (see Example
4.31
). For vector fields
\xi\in\mathfrak{t}
, the braided Lie derivative
\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}
coincides with the ordinary Lie derivative and the Lie bracket
[-,-]_{\mathfrak{t}}^{\star}=0
is also unchanged by the twist deformation (in fact, all brackets involving elements from
\mathfrak{t}
are unchanged). That is, the braided symmetry transformations from the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{t}
act exactly as in the classical theory, and we recover the anticipated statement that the noncommutative scalar field theory is invariant under the classical action of global symmetry transformations from
\mathfrak{t}
. However, this is not generally true for vector fields
\xi\notin\mathfrak{t}
. The novelty of our approach is that, when the Killing Lie algebra
\mathfrak{k}
is nonabelian, the noncommutative scalar field theory is nevertheless invariant under the braided action of symmetry transformations from all of
\mathfrak{k}
. We consider in detail the traditional example in Section
5.4
below.

5.4
Example: Braided Lorentz invariance

Let
M=\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
with the standard flat Minkowski metric
g=\eta
. The Killing Lie algebra of
(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\eta)
is the Poincaré algebra

\displaystyle\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{iso}(1,d-1)=\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}\rtimes\mathfrak{so}(1,d-1)\ .

(5.19)

The abelian translation algebra
\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
is generated by
\{\mathsf{P}_{\mu}\}_{\mu=0,1,\dots,d-1}
and the nonabelian Lorentz algebra
\mathfrak{so}(1,d-1)
by
\{\mathsf{M}_{\mu\nu}\}_{\mu,\nu=0,1,\dots,d-1}
, with
\mathsf{M}_{\mu\nu}=-\mathsf{M}_{\nu\mu}
and the semi-direct product Lie brackets

\displaystyle\begin{split}[\mathsf{P}_{\mu},\mathsf{P}_{\nu}]_{\mathfrak{k}}&=0\qquad,\qquad[\mathsf{M}_{\mu\nu},\mathsf{P}_{\lambda}]_{\mathfrak{k}}=\eta_{\mu\lambda}\,\mathsf{P}_{\nu}-\eta_{\nu\lambda}\,\mathsf{P}_{\mu}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
[\mathsf{M}_{\mu\nu},\mathsf{M}_{\lambda\rho}]_{\mathfrak{k}}&=\eta_{\mu\lambda}\,\mathsf{M}_{\nu\rho}-\eta_{\mu\rho}\,\mathsf{M}_{\nu\lambda}-\eta_{\nu\lambda}\,\mathsf{M}_{\mu\rho}+\eta_{\nu\rho}\,\mathsf{M}_{\mu\lambda}\ ,\end{split}

(5.20)

where
\eta=\frac{1}{2}\,\eta_{\mu\nu}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\otimes\mathrm{d}x^{\nu}
in coordinates
x=(x^{\mu})\in\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
; as previously we use Einstein summation conventions throughout.

Denoting the basis vector fields of
\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}
by
\partial_{\mu}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}
, the action of these generators on fields may be realised by the vector fields implementing translations and Lorentz transformations

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\mathsf{M}_{\mu\nu}=x_{\mu}\,\partial_{\nu}-x_{\nu}\,\partial_{\mu}\ .

(5.21)

Let
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{U}\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}[[\hbar]]\otimes\mathsf{U}\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}[[\hbar]]
be the Moyal–Weyl twist (see Example
4.23
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=\exp\big(-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}\,\hbar}{2}\,\theta^{\mu\nu}\,\mathsf{P}_{\mu}\otimes\mathsf{P}_{\nu}\big)\ ,

(5.22)

where
(\theta^{\mu\nu})
is a constant antisymmetric real
d{\times}d
matrix. As is well-known (see e.g.
[
85
]
), the noncommutative scalar field theory is then translationally invariant, but it breaks Lorentz invariance in the classical sense.

The full relativistic invariance of the noncommutative scalar field theory on
(\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1},\eta)
is restored by twisting the action of the Lorentz vector fields
\mathsf{M}_{\mu\nu}
to a braided representation. A quick check reveals that the twist
\mathcal{F}
in this example acts trivially on the Lie bracket,
[-,-]_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\star}=[-,-]_{\mathfrak{k}}
, so the underlying braided Lie algebra of Killing vector fields coincides with the classical Poincaré algebra. A generic element of the Poincaré algebra

\displaystyle\xi=\varepsilon^{\mu}\,\mathsf{P}_{\mu}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\omega^{\mu\nu}\,\mathsf{M}_{\mu\nu}

(5.23)

is specified by a set of real translation parameters
\varepsilon^{\mu}
and real Lorentz transformation parameters
\omega^{\mu\nu}=-\omega^{\nu\mu}
. Its braided action on a scalar field
\phi
is given by

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\phi=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}\,\phi=\varepsilon^{\mu}\,\partial_{\mu}\phi+\omega^{\mu\nu}\,x_{\nu}\star\partial_{\mu}\phi=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}\phi-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}\,\hbar}{2}\,\omega^{\mu\nu}\,\theta_{\nu\lambda}\,\partial^{\lambda}\partial_{\mu}\phi\ .

(5.24)

As expected, only Lorentz transformations of
\phi
are modified from their classical rules; note that the transformations of the coordinate functions
(x\mapsto x^{\mu}):\mathbbm{R}^{1,d-1}\to\mathbbm{R}
themselves are unchanged, as
\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}x^{\mu}=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}x^{\mu}
. These braided Poincaré transformations close the
classical
Poincaré algebra.

Nevertheless, our braided Lorentz transformations are
not
the same as the ‘twisted Lorentz transformations’ of
[
36
,
90
]
. Those act as the classical Lorentz symmetry on a single field, and do not follow a braided derivation rule on star-products of fields but rather a deformed Leibniz rule defined by the Drinfel’d twist of the coproduct
\Delta_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}
on the enveloping Hopf algebra of
\mathfrak{k}
. It would be interesting to explore whether the standard Wigner classification of particles holds for braided representations of the Lorentz algebra, as it does for the twisted actions of
[
36
]
.

6
Braided
BF
theory

Let us now return to examples of field theories with gauge symmetries. In this section we look at an example of a topological field theory which is a natural extension of Chern–Simons theory, considered as our prototypical guiding example of gauge theories in the (braided)
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism in Sections
2
and
4
. The story spelled out for Chern–Simons theory is easily adapted for arbitrary
BF
theories, whose classical
L_{\infty}
-algebra formulation was worked out in detail by
[
43
]
. These examples serve to nicely illustrate generic features of field theories with higher (or reducible) gauge symmetries in a simple framework, and provide new examples of ensuing novelties with the corresponding higher braided gauge symmetries which also drastically deviate from their classical and star-gauge counterparts.

6.1
BF
theory in the
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism

Let
M
be a closed oriented
d
-dimensional manifold. Let
(\mathfrak{g},[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}})
be a Lie algebra, and let
W
be a
\mathfrak{g}
-module with a nondegenerate pairing
\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}:W\otimes\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
which is invariant under the
\mathfrak{g}
-action:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big((X\cdot w)\otimes Y+w\otimes[X,Y]_{\mathfrak{g}}\big)=0\ ,

(6.1)

for
w\in W
and
X,Y\in\mathfrak{g}
. The standard example is when
\mathfrak{g}
is a quadratic Lie algebra, where
W=\mathfrak{g}
is a
\mathfrak{g}
-module under the adjoint action of
\mathfrak{g}
on itself, as in Section
2.1
.

The
BF
action functional is given by

\displaystyle S(B,A)=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}(B\wedge F_{A})\ ,

(6.2)

where
F_{A}\in\Omega^{2}(M,\mathfrak{g})
is
the curvature of a connection
1
-form
A\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
and
B\in\Omega^{d-2}(M,W)
. The field equations of
BF
theory are solved by pairs of a flat
\mathfrak{g}
-connection on
M
and a covariantly constant

(d{-}2)
-form valued in the representation
W
of
\mathfrak{g}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{F}_{B}&:=F_{A}=\mathrm{d}A+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}=0\ \in\ \Omega^{2}(M,\mathfrak{g})\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mathfrak{F}_{A}&:=\mathrm{d}^{A}B=\mathrm{d}B+A\wedge B=0\ \in\ \Omega^{d-1}(M,W)\ ,\end{split}

(6.3)

where again
A\wedge B
computes the exterior product of the form
components while pairing the components in
\mathfrak{g}
and
W
via the

\mathfrak{g}
-action.

The action functional (
6.2
) is invariant under standard gauge transformations
\lambda\in\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
acting as

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}(B,A)=\big(-\lambda\cdot B\,,\,\mathrm{d}\lambda+[A,\lambda]_{\mathfrak{g}}\big)\ .

(6.4)

Compared to Chern–Simons theory, however, for
d\geq 3
there is an extra ‘shift’ symmetry generated by
(d{-}3)
-forms
\tau\in\Omega^{d-3}(M,W)
valued in
W
, which act as

\displaystyle\delta_{\tau}(B,A):=(\mathrm{d}\tau+A\wedge\tau,0)\ .

(6.5)

The corresponding Noether identities coincide with the usual Bianchi identities

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathsf{d}}_{(B,A)}(\mathfrak{F}_{B},\mathfrak{F}_{A}):\!&=\big((-1)^{d-3}\,\mathrm{d}^{A}\mathfrak{F}_{B}\,,\,\mathrm{d}^{A}\mathfrak{F}_{A}-\mathfrak{F}_{B}\wedge B\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=\big((-1)^{d-3}\,\mathrm{d}^{A}F_{A}\,,\,(\mathrm{d}^{A})^{2}B-F_{A}\wedge B\big)=(0,0)\ \in\ \Omega^{3}(M,\mathfrak{g})\times\Omega^{d}(M,W)\ .\end{split}

(6.6)

Here the first slot is the second Bianchi identity which in this example corresponds to the shift symmetry, in contrast to Chern–Simons theory where it corresponds to the usual gauge transformations generated by
\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
. The latter symmetries correspond to the first Bianchi identity in
BF
theory, which is the second slot.

The cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra of
BF
theory in
d
dimensions is given by the underlying graded vector space
V:=V^{0}\oplus V^{1}\oplus V^{2}\oplus V^{3}
,
where

\displaystyle\begin{split}V^{0}=\Omega^{d-3}(M,W)\times\Omega^{0}\big(M,\mathfrak{g}\big)\qquad&,\qquad V^{1}=\Omega^{d-2}(M,W)\times\Omega^{1}\big(M,\mathfrak{g}\big)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
V^{2}=\Omega^{2}\big(M,\mathfrak{g}\big)\times\Omega^{d-1}(M,W)\qquad&,\qquad V^{3}=\Omega^{3}(M,\mathfrak{g})\times\Omega^{d}(M,W)\ .\end{split}

(6.7)

We denote gauge parameters by
(\tau,\lambda)\in V^{0}
, dynamical fields by
(B,A)\in V^{1}
, field equations by
(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})\in V^{2}
, and Noether identities by
(\mathcal{T},\varLambda)\in V^{3}
. The non-trivial brackets then comprise the differential

\displaystyle\ell_{1}(\tau,\lambda)=(\mathrm{d}\tau,\mathrm{d}\lambda)\ ,\quad\ell_{1}(B,A)=(\mathrm{d}A,\mathrm{d}B)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell_{1}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})=(\mathrm{d}\mathcal{B},\mathrm{d}\mathcal{A})\ ,

(6.8)

along with the
2
-brackets

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell_{2}\big((\tau_{1},\lambda_{1})\,,\,(\tau_{2},\lambda_{2})\big)&=\big(-\lambda_{1}\cdot\tau_{2}+\lambda_{2}\cdot\tau_{1}\,,\,-[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}\big)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}\big((\tau,\lambda)\,,\,(B,A)\big)&=\big(-\lambda\cdot B+A\wedge\tau\,,\,-[\lambda,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}\big)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}\big((\tau,\lambda)\,,\,(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})\big)&=\big(-[\lambda,\mathcal{B}]_{\mathfrak{g}}\,,\,-\lambda\cdot\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}\wedge\tau\big)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}\big((\tau,\lambda)\,,\,(\mathcal{T},\varLambda)\big)&=\big(-[\lambda,\mathcal{T}]_{\mathfrak{g}}\,,\,-\lambda\cdot\varLambda+(-1)^{d-3}\,\mathcal{T}\wedge\tau\big)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}\big((B_{1},A_{1})\,,\,(B_{2},A_{2})\big)&=-\big([A_{1},A_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}\,,\,A_{1}\wedge B_{2}+A_{2}\wedge B_{1}\big)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}\big((B,A)\,,\,(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})\big)&=-\big([A,\mathcal{B}]_{\mathfrak{g}}\,,\,A\wedge\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{B}\wedge B\big)\ .\end{split}

(6.9)

Thus the dynamics of
BF
theory in any dimension
d
is also organised by a differential graded Lie algebra.
The cyclic inner product is given naturally as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle(B,A)\,,\,(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})\rangle&:=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}(B\wedge\mathcal{B})+\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}(\mathcal{A}\wedge A)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\langle(\tau,\lambda)\,,\,(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{P})\rangle&:=\int_{M}\,(-1)^{d-3}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}(\tau\wedge\mathcal{T})+\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}(\varLambda\wedge\lambda)\ .\end{split}

(6.10)

6.2
Noncommutative
BF
theory in the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism

The differential graded Lie algebra of
d
-dimensional
BF
theory from Section
6.1
lives in the category of
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{v}
-modules, as a consequence of diffeomorphism invariance of the topological field theory. Applying the general formalism of braided field theory from Section
4.6
, we twist deform it to the 4-term differential graded braided Lie algebra
\big(V[[\hbar]],\ell_{1}^{\star},\ell_{2}^{\star}\big)
with
1
-bracket
\ell_{1}^{\star}=\ell_{1}
and the non-trivial
2
-brackets

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\tau_{1},\lambda_{1})\,,\,(\tau_{2},\lambda_{2})\big)&=\big(-\lambda_{1}\star\tau_{2}+\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda_{2})\star\mathsf{R}^{k}(\tau_{1})\,,\,-[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]^{\star}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\tau,\lambda)\,,\,(B,A)\big)&=\big(-\lambda\star B+\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\tau)\,,\,-[\lambda,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\tau,\lambda)\,,\,(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})\big)&=\big(-[\lambda,\mathcal{B}]^{\star}_{\mathfrak{g}}\,,\,-\lambda\star\mathcal{A}+\mathsf{R}_{k}(\mathcal{B})\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\tau)\big)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\tau,\lambda)\,,\,(\mathcal{T},\varLambda)\big)&=\big(-[\lambda,\mathcal{T}]^{\star}_{\mathfrak{g}}\,,\,-\lambda\star\varLambda+(-1)^{d-3}\,\mathsf{R}_{k}(\mathcal{T})\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\tau)\big)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((B_{1},A_{1})\,,\,(B_{2},A_{2})\big)&=-\big([A_{1},A_{2}]^{\star}_{\mathfrak{g}}\,,\,A_{1}\wedge_{\star}B_{2}+\mathsf{R}_{k}(A_{2})\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(B_{1})\big)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((B,A)\,,\,(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})\big)&=-\big([A,\mathcal{B}]^{\star}_{\mathfrak{g}}\,,\,A\wedge_{\star}\mathcal{A}-\mathsf{R}_{k}(\mathcal{B})\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(B)\big)\ .\end{split}

(6.11)

The notation
\lambda\star B
again stands for the left braided representation of
\lambda\in\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
on
B\in\Omega^{d-2}(M,W)[[\hbar]]
as described in Section
4.3
, and similarly for
A\wedge_{\star}B
.

A pair of gauge parameters
(\tau,\lambda)\in\Omega^{d-3}(M,W)\times\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
defines the (left) braided gauge transformations of the fields
(B,A)\in\Omega^{d-2}(M,W)\times\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})

by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta_{(\tau,\lambda)}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}(B,A):\!&=\ell_{1}^{\star}(\tau,\lambda)+\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\tau,\lambda)\,,\,(B,A)\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=\big(\mathrm{d}\tau+\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\tau)-\lambda\star B\,,\,\mathrm{d}\lambda-[\lambda,A]^{\star}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big)\ ,\end{split}

(6.12)

encoding the expected deformation of the symmetries. Notice that in the braided framework, the shift symmetry is twisted naturally irrespective of the dimension
d
, gauge algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and representation
W
. The closure as a braided Lie algebra

\displaystyle\big[\delta_{(\tau_{1},\lambda_{1})}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}},\delta_{(\tau_{2},\lambda_{2})}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\big]_{\circ}^{\star}=\delta_{-\ell_{2}^{\star}((\tau_{1},\lambda_{1}),(\tau_{2},\lambda_{2}))}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}=\delta^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}_{(\lambda_{1}\star\tau_{2}-\mathsf{R}_{k}(\lambda_{2})\star\mathsf{R}^{k}(\tau_{1}),[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]^{\star}_{\mathfrak{g}})}

(6.13)

follows naturally as in the commutative case, giving a braided deformation of the semi-direct product

\displaystyle\Omega^{d-3}(M,W)[[\hbar]]\rtimes_{\star}\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]]\ ,

(6.14)

where we regard the vector space
W
(and hence
\Omega^{d-3}(M,W)[[\hbar]]
) as an abelian Lie algebra.

Braided
BF
equations

Following the prescription from Section
4.6
, the field equations
\mathfrak{F}_{(B,A)}^{\star}=0
valued in
\Omega^{2}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]]\times\Omega^{d-1}(M,W)[[\hbar]]
are encoded by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{F}_{(B,A)}^{\star}=(\mathfrak{F}_{B}^{\star},\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}):\!&=\ell_{1}^{\star}(B,A)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((B,A)\,,\,(B,A)\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=\big(\mathrm{d}A+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[A,A]^{\star}_{\mathfrak{g}}\,,\,\mathrm{d}B+A\wedge_{\star}B+\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(B)\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=\big(F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\,,\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{A}B+\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{A}B)\big)\ ,\end{split}

(6.15)

where in the third line we have identified the left and right braided covariant derivatives from Section
4.3
. The field equations state that the braided
\mathfrak{g}
-connection
A
is flat while the field
B
obeys a symmetrized braided covariant constancy condition. Such symmetrized combinations are expected since they preserve reality of the fields (cf. Example
4.106
), and hence are a generic feature of braided field theories.

According to the general braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra formulation, we obtain the braided gauge covariance

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta_{(\tau,\lambda)}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\mathfrak{F}_{(B,A)}^{\star}&=\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\tau,\lambda)\,,\,(\mathfrak{F}_{B}^{\star},\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star})\big)=\big(-[\lambda,\mathfrak{F}_{B}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,,\,-\lambda\star\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}+\mathsf{R}_{k}(\mathfrak{F}_{B}^{\star})\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\tau)\big)\ ,\end{split}

(6.16)

which may also be easily verified explicitly. This extends the statement from Section
4.3
that the braided curvature, left and right covariant derivatives are covariant under gauge transformations from
\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
.

Braided Noether identities

From the general prescription of Section
4.6
, the braided Noether identities which are valued in
\Omega^{3}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]]\times\Omega^{d}(M,W)[[\hbar]]
are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathsf{d}}_{(B,A)}^{\star}\mathfrak{F}_{(B,A)}^{\star}:\!&=\ell_{1}^{\star}(\mathfrak{F}_{B}^{\star},\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star})-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\Big(\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((B,A)\,,\,(\mathfrak{F}_{B}^{\star},\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star})\big)-\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\mathfrak{F}_{B}^{\star},\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star})\,,\,(B,A)\big)\Big)\\
&\quad\,+\tfrac{1}{4}\,\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(B,A)\,,\,\ell_{2}^{\star}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(B,A)\,,\,(B,A))\big)\ =\ (0,0)\ ,\end{split}

(6.17)

where
\mathsf{R}_{k}(B,A):=\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(B),\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)\big)
. These translate to a pair of differential identities among the field equations (
6.15
). Expanding and collecting terms, the first slot of (
6.17
) is the Noether identity corresponding to the braided shift symmetry:

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{A}\mathfrak{F}_{B}^{\star}+\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{A}\mathfrak{F}_{B}^{\star}\big)+\tfrac{1}{4}\,\big[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ =\ 0\ .

(6.18)

The second slot is the Noether identity corresponding to the usual braided gauge transformations generated by
\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tfrac{1}{2}\,&\big(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{A}\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}+\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{A}\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}-\mathsf{R}_{k}(\mathfrak{F}_{B}^{\star})\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(B)-\mathfrak{F}_{B}^{\star}\wedge_{\star}B\big)\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt+\tfrac{1}{4}\,\Big(-[A,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)]^{\star}_{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(B)+\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)\wedge_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}^{k}(A)\wedge_{\star}B\big)\\
&{\phantom{\hskip 56.9055pt+\tfrac{1}{4}\,\big(-[A,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)]^{\star}_{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(B)+}}+{\mathsf{R}_{k}}_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}(A)\wedge_{\star}\big({\mathsf{R}_{k}}_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}(A)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(B)\big)\Big)\ =\ 0\ ,\end{split}

(6.19)

where we used the Sweedler notation
\Delta_{\textrm{\tiny$\mathcal{F}$}}(\mathsf{R}_{k})=:{\mathsf{R}_{k}}_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}\otimes{\mathsf{R}_{k}}_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}
(with summations understood).
This identity may also be derived
directly by summing over the braided deformations of the first Bianchi identity in twisted noncommutative differential geometry:

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{A})^{2}B&=F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\wedge_{\star}B+\tfrac{1}{2}\,A\wedge_{\star}(A\wedge_{\star}B)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)\wedge_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}^{k}(A)\wedge_{\star}B\big)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{A})^{2}B&=\mathsf{R}_{k}(F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}})\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(B)\\
&\quad\,+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathsf{R}_{k}\,\mathsf{R}_{l}(A)\wedge_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{m}\,\mathsf{R}^{l}(A)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{m}\,\mathsf{R}^{k}(B)\big)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\mathsf{R}_{k}}_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}(A)\wedge_{\star}\big({\mathsf{R}_{k}}_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}(A)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(B)\big)\ ,\end{split}

(6.20)

and the mixed derivative expressions. These follow by simply expanding the braided covariant derivatives. The last two terms cancel each other in the classical limit, giving the usual identity
(\mathrm{d}^{A})^{2}B=F\wedge B
.

Braided
BF
functional

When
M
is closed and oriented, the inner product (
6.10
) twists to a (braided) cyclic pairing given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle(B,A)\,,\,(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})\rangle_{\star}&:=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}(B\wedge_{\star}\mathcal{B})+\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}(\mathcal{A}\wedge_{\star}A)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\langle(\tau,\lambda)\,,\,(\mathcal{T},\varLambda)\rangle_{\star}&:=\int_{M}\,(-1)^{d-3}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}(\tau\wedge_{\star}\mathcal{T})+\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}(\varLambda\wedge_{\star}\lambda)\ .\end{split}

(6.21)

We shall write out the braided action functional in two forms: First by using a braided cyclic pairing (or forgetting the
\wedge_{\star}
-cyclicity in the case of compatible twists), and then by employing a compatible twist and its resulting
\wedge_{\star}
-cyclicity under the integral. This is instructive for considerations of more complicated braided field theories.

For a braided cyclic pairing, the action functional is given by the prescription of Section
4.6
, which in the example at hand reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{\star}(B,A):\!&=\frac{1}{2}\,\big\langle(B,A)\,,\,\ell_{1}^{\star}(B,A)\big\rangle_{\star}-\frac{1}{6}\,\big\langle(B,A)\,,\,\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((B,A),(B,A)\big)\big\rangle_{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&=\frac{1}{2}\,\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(B\wedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}A\big)+\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(A\wedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}B\big)\\
&\quad\,+\frac{1}{6}\,\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(B\wedge_{\star}[A,A]^{\star}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big)+\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\Big(A\wedge_{\star}\big(A\wedge_{\star}B+\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}(B)\big)\Big)\\[4.0pt]
&=\int_{M}\,\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(B\wedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}A\big)+\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(\mathrm{d}A\wedge_{\star}B\big)\\
&\qquad\qquad\,+\frac{1}{6}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(B\wedge_{\star}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)+\frac{1}{6}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(B)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}([A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\big)\\
&\qquad\qquad\,+\frac{1}{6}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(B)\wedge_{\star}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)\ ,\end{split}

(6.22)

where in the last equality we integrated by parts and used invariance of
\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}(-\wedge_{\star}-)
under the left braided
\mathfrak{g}
-action, which follows from the classical invariance (
6.1
) order by order in the deformation parameter
\hbar
. Identifying the braided curvature
2
-form
F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}=\mathrm{d}A+\frac{1}{2}\,[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}
, we arrive at

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{\star}(B,A)&=\int_{M}\,\frac{1}{2}\,\Big(\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(B\wedge_{\star}F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\big)+\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\wedge_{\star}B\big)\Big)\\
&\qquad\qquad\,-\frac{1}{12}\,\Big(\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(B\wedge_{\star}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)+\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(B)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}([A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\big)\Big)\\
&\qquad\qquad\,+\frac{1}{6}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(B)\wedge_{\star}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)\ .\end{split}

(6.23)

The first two terms of (
6.23
) are “naive” deformations which give the classical action functional (
6.2
) in the classical limit. The last three terms cancel each other in the classical limit, but are individually non-covariant. However, one may easily check that together they form a braided covariant combination. The possible non-covariance arises from terms proportional to
\mathrm{d}\lambda
in a gauge transformation of
A
, while the covariant terms vanish by invariance of
\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}
. Indeed one finds

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(B\wedge_{\star}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)&=\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(B)\wedge_{\star}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(\mathrm{d}\lambda),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)\\
&\quad\,+\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(B)\wedge_{\star}[\mathsf{R}_{l}(A),\mathsf{R}^{l}\,\mathsf{R}^{k}(\mathrm{d}\lambda)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=2\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(B)\wedge_{\star}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(\mathrm{d}\lambda),A]^{\star}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big)\ ,\end{split}

(6.24)

where we used the braided symmetry of the bracket in the second equality. Similarly, we find

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(B)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}([A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\big)=2\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(B)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}([\mathrm{d}\lambda,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\big)

(6.25)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(B)\wedge_{\star}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)&=\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(B)\wedge_{\star}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(\mathrm{d}\lambda),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)\\
&\quad\,+\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(B)\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{k}([\mathrm{d}\lambda,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\big)\ .\end{split}

(6.26)

Adding the three variations with the prefactors in (
6.23
) shows that they cancel, and the action functional is braided gauge invariant as expected by the general discussion of Section
4.6
.

Restricting to compatible Drinfel’d twists, the pairing becomes a strictly cyclic structure on the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\big(V[[\hbar]],\ell_{1}^{\star},\ell_{2}^{\star}\big)
. In this case, the action functional
(
6.23
) further simplifies to

\displaystyle S_{\star}(B,A)=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}\big(B\wedge_{\star}F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\big)\ ,

(6.27)

where the first two terms of (
6.23
) combine by
\wedge_{\star}
-cyclicity, while the last three terms cancel for the same reason together with an
\mathcal{R}
-matrix identity (
4.19
) applied to the final term. Thus the braided noncommutative
BF
functional is the expected “naive” deformation. By varying it with respect to each of fields
B
and
A
, it is easy to explicitly derive the field equations (
6.15
) using
\wedge_{\star}
-cyclicity under the integral, invariance of
\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}
and
\mathcal{R}
-matrix identities (
4.19
). Similarly, braided gauge invariance under (
6.12
) and the corresponding Noether identities (
6.17
) are verified explicitly. For Hermitian twists and real fields the action functional is real-valued as discussed in Section
4.6
.

6.3
Higher braided gauge symmetry

Another new feature of
BF
theories, compared to Chern–Simons gauge theory, is that they generally possess additional
higher shift symmetries: The shift
symmetry (
6.5
) is on-shell reducible in dimensions

d\geq 4
. This means that, strictly speaking, we should also include
in (
6.7
) the negatively-graded vector spaces

V^{-k}=\Omega^{d-3-k}(M,W)
for

k=1,\dots,d-3
, which parameterize ‘higher gauge transformations’,
together with their duals
V^{k+3}=\Omega^{k+3}(M,\mathfrak{g})
and the obvious brackets in
(
6.8
) and (
6.9
). We spell out this out explicitly for

BF
theories in the simplest case of dimension
d=4
.

Any element
\tau\in\Omega^{1}(M,W)
generates the shift symmetry

\displaystyle\delta_{\tau}(B,A)=\big(\mathrm{d}^{A}\tau,0\big)\ \in\ \Omega^{2}(M,W)\times\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})\ .

(6.28)

Now consider
\tau^{\prime}:=\tau+\mathrm{d}^{A}\varepsilon\,\in\Omega^{1}(M,W)
for any
\varepsilon\in\Omega^{0}(M,W)
. This generates the shift symmetry

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta_{\tau^{\prime}}(B,A)&=\big(\mathrm{d}^{A}\tau^{\prime},0\big)=\big(\mathrm{d}^{A}\tau+(\mathrm{d}^{A})^{2}\varepsilon,0\big)=\big(\delta_{\tau}B+F_{A}\cdot\varepsilon,0\big)=\big(\delta_{\tau}B+\mathfrak{F}_{B}\cdot\varepsilon,0\big)\ ,\end{split}

(6.29)

and so the two transformations differ by a term proportional to a
field equation, that is,
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}=:\tau+\delta_{(\varepsilon,A)}\tau
generate the same symmetry on-shell. This
leads to a redundancy in the subspace of
V^{0}
generating the action of shift symmetries on

V^{1}
; the redundancy lives in the subspace of
covariantly exact
1
-forms valued in
W
. We may parameterize this by

V^{-1}:=\Omega^{0}(M,W)
; for
d=4
this is
enough and no further gauge redundancy in the description exists,
while in higher dimensions
d>4
the form degrees change and similarly higher-to-higher gauge transformations are required, and so on.

Following
[
43
]
, let us now describe the complete extended
L_{\infty}
-algebra of
BF
theory in
d=4
dimensions. We
extend the cochain complex by introducing

\displaystyle V^{-1}:=\Omega^{0}(M,W)\qquad\text{and}\qquad V^{4}:=\Omega^{4}(M,\mathfrak{g})\ ,

(6.30)

and denote the corresponding elements by
\varepsilon\in V^{-1}
and
\mathcal{E}\in V^{4}
. The brackets
(
6.8
) and (
6.9
) are extended as

\displaystyle\ell_{1}(\varepsilon)=(\mathrm{d}\varepsilon,0)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\ell_{1}(\mathcal{T},\varLambda)=\mathrm{d}\mathcal{T}\ ,

(6.31)

together with

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell_{2}\big(\varepsilon\,,\,(\tau,\lambda)\big)=\lambda\cdot\varepsilon\quad,\quad\ell_{2}\big(\varepsilon\,,\,(B,A)\big)&=-(A\cdot\varepsilon,0)\quad,\quad\ell_{2}\big(\varepsilon\,,\,(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})\big)=(\mathcal{B}\cdot\varepsilon,0)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}\big(\varepsilon\,,\,(\mathcal{T},\varLambda)\big)=-(0,\mathcal{T}\cdot\varepsilon)\quad,\quad\ell_{2}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{E})&=(0,\mathcal{E}\cdot\varepsilon)\quad,\quad\ell_{2}\big((\tau,\lambda)\,,\,\mathcal{E}\big)=-[\lambda,\mathcal{E}]_{\mathfrak{g}}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}\big((B,A)\,,\,(\mathcal{T},\varLambda)\big)=-[A,\mathcal{T}]_{\mathfrak{g}}\quad&,\quad\ell_{2}\big((\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{1})\,,\,(\mathcal{B}_{2},\mathcal{A}_{2})\big)=-[\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}\ .\end{split}

(6.32)

The cyclic inner product extends naturally as

\displaystyle\langle\varepsilon,\mathcal{E}\rangle=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}(\varepsilon\wedge\mathcal{E})\ .

(6.33)

The higher gauge transformation is now encoded in the expected way via (
2.58
) as

\displaystyle\delta_{(\varepsilon,(B,A))}(\tau,\lambda)=\ell_{1}(\varepsilon)-\ell_{2}\big(\varepsilon,(B,A)\big)=\big(\mathrm{d}^{A}\varepsilon,0\big)\ .

(6.34)

Its adjoint defines the higher Noether operator

\displaystyle{\mathsf{d}}_{(B,A)}^{{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}(\mathcal{T},\varLambda)=\ell_{1}(\mathcal{T},\varLambda)-\ell_{2}\big((B,A),(\mathcal{T},\varLambda)\big)=\mathrm{d}^{A}\mathcal{T}\ ,

(6.35)

which encodes the level 1 Noether identity via (
2.64
) as

\displaystyle{\mathsf{d}}_{(B,A)}^{{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}\circ{\mathsf{d}}_{(B,A)}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})=-\ell_{2}\big((\mathfrak{F}_{B},\mathfrak{F}_{A}),(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})\big)=[\mathfrak{F}_{B},\mathcal{B}]_{\mathfrak{g}}\ .

(6.36)

The extension of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra carries through the usual deformation to produce a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra without any issues. However, we currently do not have a good physical interpretation of these ‘higher’ braided gauge symmetries. The issue is related to the problem of defining the braided moduli space of classical solutions. We first briefly describe the problem without explicitly writing out the detailed extended braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra, and then show how it is a general consequence within the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra framework.

For
d=4
the braided shift symmetry generated by
\tau\in\Omega^{1}(M,W)
acts as
\delta_{\tau}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}(B,A)=(\mathrm{d}^{A}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}\tau,0)
. For a higher gauge parameter
\varepsilon\in\Omega^{0}(M,W)
, we would expect the deformation of the action on gauge parameters to be
\delta_{(\varepsilon,A)}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\tau=\mathrm{d}^{A}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}\varepsilon\,\in\Omega^{1}(M,W)[[\hbar]]
. This, however, does not seem to generate an equivalent (in the classical sense) braided gauge transformation via
\tau^{\prime}:=\tau+\mathrm{d}^{A}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}\varepsilon
, since

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta_{\tau^{\prime}}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}(B,A)&=\big(\mathrm{d}^{A}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}\tau^{\prime},0\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=\big(\mathrm{d}^{A}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}\tau+(\mathrm{d}^{A}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}})^{2}\varepsilon,0\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=\big(\delta_{\tau}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}B+\mathsf{R}_{k}(\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{B})\star\mathsf{R}^{k}(\varepsilon)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)\wedge_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(A)\star\mathsf{R}^{l}\,\mathsf{R}^{k}(\varepsilon)\big)\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathsf{R}_{k}\,\mathsf{R}_{l}(A)\wedge_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{m}\,\mathsf{R}^{l}(A)\star\mathsf{R}^{m}\,\mathsf{R}^{k}(\varepsilon)\big)\,,\,0\big)\ .\end{split}

(6.37)

That is, they do not generate the same symmetry on-shell. The last two polynomial terms spoil this symmetry, which is a consequence of the breaking of the first Bianchi identity (
6.20
) used in the third equality. We now explain that this is a generic feature of any higher braided gauge theory, by using its braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra incarnation.

For this, we extend the braided brackets (
6.11
) by twisting the corresponding classical extended brackets, with
\ell^{\star}_{1}=\ell_{1}
as usual and the braided 2-brackets

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell^{\star}_{2}\big(\varepsilon\,,\,(\tau,\lambda)\big)=\lambda\star\varepsilon\qquad&,\qquad\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(\varepsilon\,,\,(B,A)\big)=-\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)\star\mathsf{R}^{k}(\varepsilon),0\big)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell^{\star}_{2}\big(\varepsilon\,,\,(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})\big)=\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(\mathcal{B})\star\mathsf{R}^{k}(\varepsilon),0\big)\qquad&,\qquad\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(\varepsilon\,,\,(\mathcal{T},\varLambda)\big)=-\big(0,\mathsf{R}_{k}(\mathcal{T})\star\mathsf{R}^{k}(\varepsilon)\big)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}^{\star}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{E})=\big(0,\mathsf{R}_{k}(\mathcal{E})\star\mathsf{R}^{k}(\varepsilon)\big)\qquad&,\qquad\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\tau,\lambda)\,,\,\mathcal{E}\big)=-[\lambda,\mathcal{E}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((B,A)\,,\,(\mathcal{T},\varLambda)\big)=-[A,\mathcal{T}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\qquad&,\qquad\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{1})\,,\,(\mathcal{B}_{2},\mathcal{A}_{2})\big)=-[\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ .\end{split}

(6.38)

The cyclic inner product extends as

\displaystyle\langle\varepsilon,\mathcal{E}\rangle_{\star}=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\textrm{\tiny$W$}}}(\varepsilon\wedge_{\star}\mathcal{E})\ .

(6.39)

Using these brackets, we see that the (left) higher braided gauge transformation is expressed exactly as in the classical case (
2.58
), that is

\displaystyle\delta_{(\varepsilon,(B,A))}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}(\tau,\lambda)=\big(\mathrm{d}^{A}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}\varepsilon,0\big)=\ell^{\star}_{1}(\varepsilon)-\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(\varepsilon\,,\,(B,A)\big)\ .

(6.40)

However, using the braided graded Jacobi identities one easily verifies that the classical closure property (
2.59
) no longer holds, as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta_{(\varepsilon,(B,A))}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\big(\delta_{\tau}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}(B,A)\big)&=\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(\varepsilon,\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{(B,A)}\big)+\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(\varepsilon,\mathsf{R}_{k}(B,A)\big)\,,\,\mathsf{R}^{k}(B,A)\big)\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(\varepsilon,\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((B,A)\,,\,(B,A)\big)\big)\ ,\end{split}

(6.41)

whereby expanding the brackets explicitly recovers the formula (
6.37
), as expected. Since this is a feature of any differential graded braided Lie algebra, this will be a generic trait of any higher gauge theory based on a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra, where higher brackets will generally contribute higher tensor powers of the fields to this expression.

One may also use cyclicity of the extended pairing to define the adjoint
{\mathsf{d}}^{{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}_{(B,A)}:V^{3}[[\hbar]]\to V^{4}[[\hbar]]
of the operator
\delta_{(\varepsilon,(B,A))}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}
, exactly as in the classical case (
2.62
). However, the meaning of this operator is currently unclear, due to both the breakdown of the on-shell closure propery of its adjoint operation and also the peculiar definition of the level 0 Noether operator (
4.96
) in the braided field theory. It would be interesting to further explore these issues and develop a proper formulation of higher braided gauge theories. As before, the braided BV formalism of Section
4.8
circumvents these issues, while an appropriate higher category generalization of the description of Section
4.9
ought to make sense of higher braided gauge symmetries in the setting of the braided Maurer--Cartan moduli spaces discussed there.
35
35

35

This would require the
\infty
-category setting mentioned in Footnote
29
.

7
Braided noncommutative Yang–Mills theory

In this final section we look at our first example of a noncommutative field theory with gauge symmetry based on a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra which is not a differential graded braided Lie algebra. We twist deform the
L_{\infty}
-algebra formulation of Yang–Mills theory discussed in Section
2.5
to uncover a new noncommutative deformation of Yang–Mills theory with novel and surprising features. This is in marked contrast to scalar field theory, whose braided deformation gives the standard noncommutative field theory, and the braided Chern–Simons and
BF
theories, which are based on differential graded braided Lie algebras, and whose field equations and variational principles are also given by the naive replacements of products of fields with star-products.
36
36

36

Of course, this does not preclude the different kinematical sectors of these theories due to the braided Lie brackets, as well as the novel general nature of classical solutions in braided field theory and the corresponding modified forms of gauge redundancies through the braided Noether identities.

7.1
Noncommutative Yang–Mills theory in the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra formalism

In this section we consider the braided noncommutative deformation of the Yang–Mills gauge theory described in Section
2.5
. As in the example of noncommutative scalar field theory, we suppose now that the background spacetime
(M,g)
has a non-trivial Killing Lie subalgebra of vector fields
\mathfrak{k}\subset\mathfrak{v}={\sf\Gamma}(TM)
. For the same reasons as spelled out in Section
5.2
, the Yang–Mills cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra
(V,\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3},\langle-,-\rangle)
lives in the category of
\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{k}
-modules.

We now apply the general formalism of braided field theory from Section
4.6
to the more elaborate example of Yang--Mills theory. A choice of a Killing Drinfel’d twist
37
37

37

In
d=4
dimensions, Yang–Mills theory is further invariant under conformal transformations. In this dimension, one may also use Drinfel’d twists generated by the conformal Killing Lie algebra of
(M,g)
. An analogous statement holds in
d=2
dimensions, wherein Yang–Mills theory is invariant under all area-preserving diffeomorphisms.

\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{k}[[\hbar]]\otimes\mathsf{U}\mathfrak{k}[[\hbar]]
deforms the underlying
L_{\infty}
-algebra to a 4-term braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\big(V[[\hbar]],\ell_{1}^{\star},\ell_{2}^{\star},\ell_{3}^{\star}\big)
with 1-bracket
\ell_{1}^{\star}=\ell_{1}
, the 2-brackets

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell_{2}^{\star}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=-[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\qquad&,\qquad\ell_{2}^{\star}(\lambda,A)=-[\lambda,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}^{\star}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})=-[\lambda,\mathcal{A}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\qquad,\qquad\ell_{2}^{\star}(\lambda,\varLambda)&=-[\lambda,\varLambda]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\qquad,\qquad\ell_{2}^{\star}(A,\mathcal{A})=-[A,\mathcal{A}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}^{\star}(A_{1},A_{2})=-\mathrm{d}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A_{1},A_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-&[A_{1},\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}A_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+(-1)^{d}\,[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}A_{1},A_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,\end{split}

(7.1)

and finally the non-zero 3-bracket

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell_{3}^{\star}(A_{1}&,A_{2},A_{3})\\[4.0pt]
&=-\big[A_{1},\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A_{2},A_{3}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-\big[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A_{2}),\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A_{1}),A_{3}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+(-1)^{d}\,\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A_{1},A_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,,A_{3}\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,\end{split}

(7.2)

where
[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}:=[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}\circ\mathcal{F}^{-1}
.

The (left) braided gauge transformation of a gauge field
A\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
by a gauge parameter
\lambda\in\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
follows the standard rule

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}A:=\ell_{1}^{\star}(\lambda)+\ell_{2}^{\star}(\lambda,A)=\mathrm{d}\lambda-[\lambda,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,

(7.3)

closing as a braided Lie algebra:

\displaystyle\big[\delta_{\lambda_{1}}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}},\delta_{\lambda_{2}}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\big]_{\circ}^{\star}=\delta^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}_{-\ell_{2}^{\star}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}=\delta_{[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\ ,

(7.4)

for all
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})
.

Braided Yang–Mills equations

The braided Yang–Mills field equations turn out to have a rather surprising form. Following the prescription from Section
4.6
, the field equations
\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A}=0
valued in
\Omega^{d-1}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]]
are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{tabular}[]{|l|}\hline\cr\\
$\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(\mathrm{d}^{A}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}+\mathrm{d}^{A}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\big)$\\[5.69054pt]
$\displaystyle\quad\,\hskip 28.45274pt+\,\tfrac{1}{6}\,\big[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-\tfrac{1}{12}\,\big[A,\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+\tfrac{(-1)^{d}}{12}\,\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,,A\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}$\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{tabular}\end{split}

(7.5)

where
F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}=\mathrm{d}A+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\in\Omega^{2}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]]
is the braided curvature 2-form.
Indeed, some simple algebra gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A}&=\ell_{1}^{\star}(A)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell_{2}^{\star}(A,A)-\tfrac{1}{3!}\,\ell_{3}^{\star}(A,A,A)\\[4.0pt]
&=\mathrm{d}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}A+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{d}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[A,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-\tfrac{(-1)^{d}}{2}\,[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\\
&\quad\,+\tfrac{1}{6}\,\big[A,\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+\tfrac{1}{6}\,\big[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-\tfrac{(-1)^{d}}{6}\,\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,,A\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&=\mathrm{d}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big[A,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}A+\tfrac{1}{2}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-\tfrac{(-1)^{d}}{2}\,\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}A+\tfrac{1}{2}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,,\,A\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\\
&\quad\,-\tfrac{1}{12}\,\big[A,\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+\tfrac{1}{6}\,\big[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+\tfrac{(-1)^{d}}{12}\,\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,,A\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,\end{split}

(7.6)

and the result follows by identifying the curvature and left/right covariant derivatives.

The first term of the field equations (
7.5
) is the expected symmetrized braided covariant derivative of
\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}
, which reduces to the covariant constancy condition in the classical limit, similarly to the example of braided
BF
theory from Section
6.2
. However, the appearance of the last three terms is completely unexpected, since in the classical limit
{\mathcal{R}}=1\otimes 1
and the terms conspire to cancel. Hence these terms could not have been guessed or even motivated by naively deforming the classical field equations. The braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra perspective necessitates their presence. Similar features occur in four-dimensional braided gravity
[
45
]
, and we expect such genuinely new interaction terms to be a generic feature of gauge theories whose underlying
L_{\infty}
-algebra is not simply a differential graded Lie algebra.

The field equations (
7.5
) differ drastically from those in the usual approaches to noncommutative Yang–Mills theory based on star-gauge symmetry (cf. Section
3.1
), wherein only the unbraided version of the first term appears (with left and right covariant derivatives coinciding). This either imposes restrictions on the gauge algebra
\mathfrak{g}
or the introduction of (infinitely many) new degrees of freedom. Our braided field theory instead makes sense for any Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
without introducing any spurious degrees of freedom. The braided field equations (
7.5
) are furthermore real when restricted to real gauge fields, as follows from the general discussion of Section
4.6
.

The general braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra formulation guarantees the gauge covariance of the field equations (
7.5
) under the braided gauge transformations (
7.3
). Gauge covariance in the present example reads in the expected way as

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\,\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}=\ell_{2}^{\star}(\lambda,\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star})=-[\lambda,\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ .

(7.7)

Given the explicit form of (
7.5
), this is a non-trivial statement. The first term is manifestly braided covariant, as it involves covariant derivatives. On the other hand, the last three terms are not separately braided covariant objects. However, it is straightforward to show that together they do form a braided covariant combination, as guaranteed by the braided homotopy relations of Section
4.6
. We will demonstrate how this works explicitly in Section
7.2
below.

Braided Noether identities

From the general prescription of Section
4.6
, the gauge redundancies of braided Yang–Mills theory are encoded by the braided Noether identities

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathsf{d}}_{A}^{\star}\mathfrak{F}^{\star}_{A}&:=\ell_{1}^{\star}(\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star})-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(\ell_{2}^{\star}(A,\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star})-\ell_{2}^{\star}(\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star},A)\big)\\
&\quad\,-\tfrac{1}{12}\,\ell_{1}^{\star}\big(\ell_{3}^{\star}(A,A,A)\big)+\tfrac{1}{4}\,\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\,\ell_{2}^{\star}(\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A)\big)\\
&\quad\,+\tfrac{1}{12}\,\Big(\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(\ell_{3}^{\star}(A,A,A),A\big)-\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(A,\ell_{3}^{\star}(A,A,A)\big)\Big)\ =\ 0\ ,\end{split}

(7.8)

where we used
\ell^{\star}_{3}\big(\mathcal{A},A_{1},A_{2}\big)=0
. Expanding and collecting terms, this translates to the differential identity

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{tabular}[]{|l|}\hline\cr\\
$\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{A}\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}+\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{A}\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}\big)+\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny L}}}^{A}Z_{A}+\mathrm{d}_{\star{\textrm{\tiny R}}}^{A}Z_{A}-3\,\mathrm{d}Z_{A}+\tfrac{1}{4}\,\big(\mathrm{d}\tilde{Z}_{A}-[A,\tilde{Z}_{A}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-[\tilde{Z}_{A},A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)$\\[5.69054pt]
$\hskip 42.67912pt+\,\tfrac{1}{4}\,\big[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)\,,\,\mathrm{d}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-(-1)^{d}\,[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ =\ 0$\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{tabular}\end{split}

(7.9)

where we define
Z_{A}
to be the new covariant combination from the field equations (
7.5
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{A}:=\tfrac{1}{6}\,\big[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-\tfrac{1}{12}\,\big[A,\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+\tfrac{(-1)^{d}}{12}\,\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,,A\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,\end{split}

(7.10)

and
\tilde{Z}_{A}
to be the first term of this expression:

\displaystyle\tilde{Z}_{A}:=\big[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ .

(7.11)

In the classical limit
Z_{A}
vanishes, while the rest of the inhomogeneous terms cancel upon application of the classical Jacobi identity for the graded Lie algebra
\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g}),[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}\big)
. Thus we obtain the classical Noether identity
\mathrm{d}^{A}\mathfrak{F}_{A}=0
, as expected.

Braided Yang–Mills functional

By twisting the classical Yang–Mills inner product (
2.74
), we obtain the (braided) cyclic structure

\displaystyle\langle A,\mathcal{A}\rangle_{\star}:=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}(A\wedge_{\star}\mathcal{A})\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\langle\lambda,\Lambda\rangle_{\star}:=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\lambda\wedge_{\star}\Lambda)\ .

(7.12)

We shall use this to write out and simplify the braided Yang–Mills action functional in two forms, as we did with the braided
BF
theory of Section
6.2
.
First we consider an arbitrary Drinfel’d twist
\mathcal{F}
, and hence a braided cyclic pairing (or for a compatible twist we simply forget the strict cyclicity).
We define the action functional
via the prescription of Section
4.6
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{\star}(A):\!&=\frac{1}{2}\,\langle A,\ell^{\star}_{1}(A)\rangle_{\star}-\frac{1}{3!}\,\langle A,\ell^{\star}_{2}(A,A)\rangle_{\star}-\frac{1}{4!}\,\langle A,\ell^{\star}_{3}(A,A,A)\rangle_{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&=\int_{M}\,\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}(A\wedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}A)\\
&\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{6}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\Big(A\wedge_{\star}\big(-\mathrm{d}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-[A,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+(-1)^{d}\,[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)\,\Big)\\
&\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{24}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\Big(A\wedge_{\star}\big(-\big[A,\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-\big[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\\
&\hskip 227.62204pt+(-1)^{d}\,\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,,A\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)\,\Big)\ .\end{split}

(7.13)

We simplify (
7.13
) by making the term
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}(F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\wedge_{\star}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}})
appear, and hence view the remaining terms as a deformation of the “naive” noncommutative Yang–Mills action functional. This requires the use of the
\mathcal{R}
-matrix identities (
4.19
), as well as the invariance of
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}(-\wedge_{\star}-)
under the left and right braided adjoint actions, which follows from the classical invariance order by order in the deformation parameter
\hbar
. For instance, we may derive the useful identity

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big(A\wedge_{\star}[A,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big)&=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big([\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\mathsf{R}^{k}(A)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\wedge_{\star}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}A\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big([A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\wedge_{\star}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}A\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big(\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\wedge_{\star}\mathrm{d}A\big)\ ,\end{split}

(7.14)

where in the first line we use the braided
{\rm ad}(\mathfrak{g})
-invariance of
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}(-\wedge_{\star}-)
, followed by braided symmetry of
[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}
on 1-forms and strict symmetry of the Hodge duality operator
\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
with respect to the cyclic inner product. In a completely analogous way, we find

\displaystyle\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big(A\wedge_{\star}\,\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,,A\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big)=-(-1)^{d}\,\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big([A,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\wedge_{\star}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big)\ .

(7.15)

By treating the rest of the terms in a similar fashion, after a long but straightforward calculation we reach a simplified and more familiar form of the braided noncommutative Yang–Mills functional:

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{\star}(A)&=\int_{M}\,\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big(F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\wedge_{\star}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\big)+\frac{1}{6}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big([A,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\wedge_{\star}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathsf{R}^{k}(F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}})\big)\\
&\quad\qquad\,-\frac{1}{12}\,\Big(\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big(F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\wedge_{\star}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big)+\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big(\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\wedge_{\star}\,F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\big)\Big)\\
&\quad\qquad\,+\frac{1}{24}\,\Big(\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big([A,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\wedge_{\star}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\,-\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big([A,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\wedge_{\star}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathsf{R}^{k}([A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,)\big)\Big)\ .\end{split}

(7.16)

The first term is the “naive” deformation of the classical Yang–Mills action functional (
2.66
), similarly to that discussed in Section
3.1
. It is invariant under braided gauge transformations, due to braided
{\rm ad}(\mathfrak{g})
-invariance of
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}
and covariance of the braided curvature
F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}
. The remaining terms are unexpected new interactions that represent the non-trivial deformation we uncover in the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra framework. They vanish in the classical limit where
\mathcal{R}=1\otimes 1
, recovering the classical action functional. This is completely in line with the extra terms we find in the braided field equations (
7.5
). Given that the (braided)
L_{\infty}
-algebra formulation of field theories is a broad generalization of Chern–Simons theory, it seems natural that Chern–Simons type terms, involving the gauge field
A
on its own, turn up in our definition of braided field theories. Although these terms are individually non-covariant, together they form braided gauge covariant combinations. We discuss this point in more detail in Section
7.2
below.

Restricting to compatible Drinfel’d twists, the pairing becomes a strictly cyclic structure for the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
(V[[\hbar]],\ell_{1}^{\star},\ell_{2}^{\star},\ell_{3}^{\star})
. In this case, the action functional (
7.16
) simplifies further to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{tabular}[]{|l|}\hline\cr\\
$\displaystyle S_{\star}(A)=\frac{1}{2}\,\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big(F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\wedge_{\star}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}F_{A}^{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}\big)+\frac{1}{24}\,\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big([A,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\wedge_{\star}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big)$\\[11.38109pt]
$\displaystyle\quad\,\hskip 28.45274pt-\frac{1}{24}\,\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big([A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\wedge_{\star}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big)$\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{tabular}\end{split}

(7.17)

where the first three unexpected terms cancel each other by
\wedge_{\star}
-cyclicity under the integral, and the last term similarly simplifies.
One may derive the braided field equations (
7.5
) by varying this explicit action functional via similar manipulations as above. Of course, this is simply a confirmation of the general arguments from Section
4.6
. For Hermitian twists and real gauge fields, it is real-valued.

7.2
Braided gauge symmetry

It is instructive to see how gauge invariance of braided Yang–Mills theory is realised explicitly. We start with the field equations (
7.5
). Consider a gauge transformation of the last term:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,,A\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}&=\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,,A\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\delta_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,,A\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\\
&\quad\,+\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathsf{R}_{k}([A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\,,\delta_{\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda)}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}A\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&=\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathrm{d}\lambda,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,,A\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[[\lambda,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star},A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,,A\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\\
&\quad\,+\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\mathsf{R}^{k}(\mathrm{d}\lambda)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,,A\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),[\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,,A\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\\
&\quad\,+\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathsf{R}_{k}([A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\,,\mathsf{R}^{k}(\mathrm{d}\lambda)\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathsf{R}_{k}([A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\,,[\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&=2\,\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathrm{d}\lambda,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,,A\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-(-1)^{d}\,\big[\mathrm{d}\lambda,\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\\
&\quad\,-\big[\lambda,[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,\end{split}

(7.18)

where in the last equality we used the linearity of the Hodge duality operator and the braided Jacobi identity to combine the terms cubic in
A
into the sought covariant form. The breaking of covariance arises from the terms involving
\mathrm{d}\lambda
.

Similarly, one may check that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\big[A,\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}&=2\,\big[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(\mathrm{d}\lambda),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+\big[\mathrm{d}\lambda,\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\\
&\quad\,-\big[\lambda,[A,\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,\end{split}

(7.19)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}&=\big[\mathrm{d}\lambda,\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}+\big[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(\mathrm{d}\lambda),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\\
&\quad\,-(-1)^{d}\,\big[\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathrm{d}\lambda,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,,A\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}-\big[\lambda,[\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ .\end{split}

(7.20)

Taking the weighted sum of these three braided variations specified by (
7.5
), we see that the non-covariant terms cancel. Hence the combination in the field equations (
7.5
) is indeed braided gauge covariant.

Let us now turn to the general form (
7.16
) of the action functional. The second, third and fourth terms form a covariant combination by the same calculation for the braided
BF
functional from Section
6.2
. The last two terms also give a surprising covariant combination. The potential non-covariance arises from the terms proportional to
\mathrm{d}\lambda
in the gauge transformation, and dropping the covariant components which vanish by braided
{\rm ad}(\mathfrak{g})
-invariance of
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big([A,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\wedge_{\star}\,\mathsf{R}^{k}(\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\big)\\[4.0pt]
&\qquad\quad=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big([\mathrm{d}\lambda,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\wedge_{\star}\,\mathsf{R}^{k}(\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\big)+\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big([\mathsf{R}_{l}(A),\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\wedge_{\star}\,\mathsf{R}^{k}(\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{l}(\mathrm{d}\lambda),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\big)\\
&\qquad\quad\quad\,+\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big([\mathsf{R}_{l}(A),\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\wedge_{\star}\,\mathsf{R}^{k}(\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}_{m}(A),\mathsf{R}^{m}\,\mathsf{R}^{l}(\mathrm{d}\lambda)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\big)\\
&\qquad\quad\quad\,+\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big([\mathsf{R}_{l}(A),\mathsf{R}_{k}\,\mathsf{R}^{m}\,\mathsf{R}^{l}(\mathrm{d}\lambda)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\wedge_{\star}\,\mathsf{R}^{k}\,\mathsf{R}_{m}(\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\big)\\[4.0pt]
&\qquad\quad=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\wedge_{\star}\,[\mathsf{R}^{k}(\mathrm{d}\lambda),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big)\\
&\qquad\quad\quad\,+\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big(\mathsf{R}_{m}\,\mathsf{R}^{k}(\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{l}(\mathrm{d}\lambda),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\wedge_{\star}\,\mathsf{R}^{m}([\mathsf{R}_{l}(A),\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\big)\\
&\qquad\quad\quad\,+\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big([\mathsf{R}_{l}(A),\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\wedge_{\star}\,\mathsf{R}^{k}(\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{l}(\mathrm{d}\lambda),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\big)+\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big([\mathrm{d}\lambda,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\wedge_{\star}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big)\\[4.0pt]
&\qquad\quad=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\wedge_{\star}\,[\mathsf{R}^{k}(\mathrm{d}\lambda),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big)+\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big([\mathrm{d}\lambda,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\wedge_{\star}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big)\\
&\qquad\quad\quad\,+2\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big(\mathsf{R}_{l}(\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(\mathrm{d}\lambda),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\wedge_{\star}\,[\mathsf{R}^{l}\,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big)\\[4.0pt]
&\qquad\quad=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}(\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\wedge_{\star}\,[\mathsf{R}^{k}(\mathrm{d}\lambda),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big)+\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big([\mathrm{d}\lambda,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\wedge_{\star}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big)\\
&\qquad\quad\quad\,+2\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big(\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathrm{d}\lambda,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\wedge_{\star}\,[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big)\ ,\end{split}

(7.21)

where we repeatedly used braided symmetry of
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}(-\wedge_{\star}-)
and of
[-,-]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}
on 1-forms along with the
\mathcal{R}
-matrix identities (
4.19
). In a completely analogous way, one may check that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta_{\lambda}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}&\big([A,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\wedge_{\star}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big)\\[4.0pt]
&=2\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big([\mathrm{d}\lambda,\mathsf{R}_{k}(A)]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\wedge_{\star}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(A),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big)+\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big([\mathrm{d}\lambda,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\wedge_{\star}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big)\\
&\quad\,+\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\big(\mathsf{R}_{k}([A,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})\wedge_{\star}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}[\mathsf{R}^{k}(\mathrm{d}\lambda),A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\,\big)\ .\end{split}

(7.22)

Thus the non-covariant terms cancel by strict symmetry of the Hodge duality operator, ensuring gauge invariance of the action functional as expected from the general theory of Section
4
.

7.3
Global braided symmetry

Similarly to the example of noncommutative scalar theory from Section
5.3
, braided Yang–Mills theory is invariant under the finite-dimensional braided Lie algebra of twisted Killing symmetries
(\mathfrak{k},[-,-]_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\star})
, acting via the braided Lie derivative. These are incorporated into the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra framework of Section
7.1
by extending the degree
0
and
3
homogeneous subspaces of the underlying cochain complex of Section
2.5
to get
38
38

38

The caveat discussed in Footnote
33
should also be borne in mind here.

\displaystyle V^{0}=\mathfrak{k}\times\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})\ ,\quad V^{1}=\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})\ ,\quad V^{2}=\Omega^{d-1}(M,\mathfrak{g})\ ,\quad V^{3}=\mathfrak{k}^{*}\times\Omega^{d}(M,\mathfrak{g})\ ,

(7.23)

with the differential extended as

\displaystyle\ell_{1}^{\star}(\xi,\lambda)=\mathrm{d}\lambda\ ,\quad\ell_{1}^{\star}(A)=\mathrm{d}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\mathrm{d}A\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell_{1}^{\star}(\mathcal{A})=(0,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{A})\ ,

(7.24)

and by modifying the
2
-brackets of (
7.1
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\xi_{1},\lambda_{1})\,,\,(\xi_{2},\lambda_{2})\big)=\big(-[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\star}&\ ,\,-\mathrm{L}_{\xi_{1}}^{\star}\lambda_{2}+\mathrm{L}^{\star}_{\mathsf{R}_{k}(\xi_{2})}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda_{1})-[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\xi,\lambda)\,,\,A\big)=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}A-[\lambda,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\qquad&,\qquad\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\xi,\lambda)\,,\,\mathcal{A}\big)=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}\mathcal{A}-[\lambda,\mathcal{A}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\xi,\lambda)\,,\,(\xi^{\textrm{\tiny$\vee$}},\varLambda)\big)=\big(\xi\star\xi^{\textrm{\tiny$\vee$}}\,,\,\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}\varLambda-[&\lambda,\varLambda]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)\qquad,\qquad\ell_{2}^{\star}(A,\mathcal{A})=\big(0,-[A,\mathcal{A}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\big)\ ,\end{split}

(7.25)

while the brackets
\ell_{2}^{\star}(A_{1},A_{2})
and
\ell_{3}^{\star}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})
are unchanged.
The cyclic structure (
7.12
) is naturally extended as

\displaystyle\langle A,\mathcal{A}\rangle_{\star}=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}(A\wedge_{\star}\mathcal{A})\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\langle(\xi,\lambda)\,,\,(\xi^{\textrm{\tiny$\vee$}},\varLambda)\rangle_{\star}=\langle\xi,\xi^{\textrm{\tiny$\vee$}}\rangle_{\star}+\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\lambda\wedge_{\star}\varLambda)\ .

(7.26)

The extended braided transformations of the gauge fields

\displaystyle\delta^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}_{(\xi,\lambda)}A=\ell_{1}^{\star}(\xi,\lambda)+\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\xi,\lambda)\,,\,A\big)=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}A+\mathrm{d}\lambda-[\lambda,A]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}

(7.27)

satisfy the gauge algebra

\displaystyle\big[\delta_{(\xi_{1},\lambda_{1})}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}},\delta_{(\xi_{2},\lambda_{2})}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}\big]_{\circ}^{\star}=\delta_{-\ell_{2}^{\star}((\xi_{1},\lambda_{1}),(\xi_{2},\lambda_{2}))}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}=\delta^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}_{([\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\star},\mathrm{L}_{\xi_{1}}^{\star}\lambda_{2}-\mathrm{L}^{\star}_{\mathsf{R}_{k}(\xi_{2})}\mathsf{R}^{k}(\lambda_{1})+[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star})}\ ,

(7.28)

with bracket of the braided semi-direct product Lie algebra

\displaystyle\mathfrak{k}[[\hbar]]\ltimes_{\star}\Omega^{0}(M,\mathfrak{g})[[\hbar]]\ .

(7.29)

The braided noncommutative Yang–Mills equations (
7.5
) are correspondingly covariant,

\displaystyle\delta^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}_{(\xi,\lambda)}\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}=\ell_{2}^{\star}\big((\xi,\lambda)\,,\,\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}\big)=\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}-[\lambda,\mathfrak{F}_{A}^{\star}]_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}\ ,

(7.30)

and likewise the action functional (
7.16
) is invariant,
\delta_{(\xi,\lambda)}^{{\textrm{\tiny${\mathcal{R}}$}}}S_{\star}(A)=0
.
These extended properties also follow directly from the closure and derivation properties of the braided Lie derivative
\mathrm{L}_{\xi}^{\star}
.
